A Auxiliary Results A.1 Filtrations, adapted basis, and nested sets A.1.1 Filtrations of vector spaces A.1.2 Tuples of filtrations and splittings A.1.3 Adapted bases A.1.4 Nested sets of linear subspaces A.1.5 Nested sets of projective subspaces A.2 Exterior algebra A.2.1 Multivectors and subspaces A.2.2 Contraction A.3 Saturated subsheaves A.3.1 Subsheaves and subbundles A.3.2 Saturated subsheaves and subbundles A.3.3 Determinant line bundle A.4 Distributions on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ A.4.1 Homogeneous multivector fields on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ A.4.2 Multivector fields tangent to hyperplanes B Essential and irreducible configurations C Miyaoka-Yau and Kähler-Einstein metrics List of Symbols References License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2411.09573v1 [math.AG] 14 Nov 2024 A Miyaoka-Yau inequality for hyperplane arrangements in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ Martin de Borbon Note: Loughborough University Email: (m.de-borbon@lboro.ac.uk) Dmitri Panov Note: King’s College London Email: (dmitri.panov@kcl.ac.uk) August 24, 2026 Abstract Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a hyperplane arrangement in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . We define a quadratic form $Q$ on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ that is entirely determined by the intersection poset of $\mathcal{H}$ . Using the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality for parabolic bundles, we show that if $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ is such that the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable, then $Q(\mathbf{a})\leq 0$ . As an application, we consider the symmetric case where all the weights are equal. The inequality $Q(a,\ldots,a)\leq 0$ gives a lower bound for the total sum of multiplicities of codimension $2$ intersection subspaces of $\mathcal{H}$ . The lower bound is attained when every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects all the other members of $\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}$ along $(1-2/(n+1))|\mathcal{H}|+1$ codimension $2$ subspaces; extending from $n=2$ to higher dimensions a condition found by Hirzebruch for line arrangements in the complex projective plane. Let $N=|\mathcal{H}|$ be the number of hyperplanes, which we assume to be finite, and fix a labelling, say $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\}$ . We assume that $H_{i}\neq H_{j}$ for $i\neq j$ . In this paper, we introduce a quadratic form $Q:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}$ associated to the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ and show that $Q\leq 0$ on a certain convex polyhedral cone $C\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{N}$ , that we call the semistable cone of $\mathcal{H}$ . The quadratic form $Q$ of $\mathcal{H}$ . The multiplicity $m_{L}$ of a linear subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the number of hyperplanes $H_{i}\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ . A codimension $2$ subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is reducible if $m_{L}=2$ , and irreducible if $m_{L}\geq 3$ . Let $\sigma_{i}$ be the number of codimension $2$ irreducible subspaces contained in the hyperplane $H_{i}$ . The quadratic form $Q:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is defined by the symmetric matrix with entries given by $Q_{ij}=\begin{cases}-(n+1)\sigma_{i}+2n&\text{ if }i=j\,,\\ -2&\text{ if }i\neq j\text{ and }L=H_{i}\cap H_{j}\text{ is reducible},\\ \,\,n-1&\text{ if }i\neq j\text{ and }L=H_{i}\cap H_{j}\text{ is irreducible}.\end{cases}$ Remark. If $n=2$ , then $(-Q_{ij})$ is equal to the matrix given by [Hir85, Equation (3)] in the context of Höfer’s formula for ‘the proportionality’ $3c_{2}-c_{1}^{2}$ of algebraic surfaces obtained as branched covers of the projective plane branching along a line arrangement. The semistable and stable cones. A basis $\mathcal{B}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is a subset $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}$ of $n+1$ linearly independent hyperplanes. The indicator function $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}$ of a basis $\mathcal{B}$ is the vector in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ whose $i$ -th component is $1$ if $H_{i}\in\mathcal{B}$ and $0$ otherwise. The semistable cone $C$ is the conical hull of the vectors $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}$ with $\mathcal{B}$ ranging over all bases of $\mathcal{H}$ . Put differently, if $P$ is the convex hull of the vectors $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}$ , then $C=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot P$ is the cone over $P$ . The convex set $P$ is called the matroid polytope of $\mathcal{H}$ . Dually, the cone $C$ can be described in terms of defining linear inequalities. Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the set of non-empty and proper linear subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained by intersecting hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ . The semistable cone $C$ is the set of points $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$ with non-negative components $a_{i}\geq 0$ such that, for every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ , the following holds: $\sum_{i\,|\,L\subset H_{i}}a_{i}\leq\frac{\codim L}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\,.$ The stable cone $C^{\circ}$ is the interior of the semistable cone $C$ . Equivalently, $C^{\circ}$ is the subset of $\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{N}$ where the above inequalities hold strictly. Remark. The hyperplanes $H_{i}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ correspond to points $p_{i}\in(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}$ . If the numbers $a_{i}$ are positive integers, then $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})$ belongs to $C$ (resp. $C^{\circ}$ ) if and only if the weighted configuration of points $\{(p_{i},a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}$ is semistable (resp. stable) in the sense of Geometric Invariant Theory, as follows from [Dol03, Theorem 11.2]. Miyaoka-Yau inequality. Our main result, in its most general form, is Theorem 6.29. It asserts that the quadratic form $Q$ is non-positive on the semistable cone: $C\subset\{Q\leq 0\}\,.$ We think of this as a version of the Miyaoka-Yau inequality because we have the following expectation. Conjecture: suppose that $\mathbf{a}\in C^{\circ}$ is such that $Q(\mathbf{a})=0$ and $0<a_{i}<1$ for all $i$ . Then there is a Kähler metric on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of constant holomorphic sectional curvature with cone angles $2\pi\alpha_{i}$ along the hyperplanes $H_{i}$ , where $\alpha_{i}=1-a_{i}$ . (For a more precise version, in the case of zero curvature, see Conjecture 1.3.) This conjecture holds for $n=1$ , in which case the quadratic form $Q$ is identically zero, and the existence of the metric is proven in [McO88] and [Tro91] (see also [dBP22] for an approach similar to this paper). For $n=2$ and zero curvature the conjecture holds by [Pan09, Theorem 1.12]. An important class of examples that fit into the above conjecture are complex reflection arrangements, for which the existence of the metric is proved in [CHL05]. In light of our expectation, we propose the following. Problem: classify the arrangements for which there exists $\mathbf{a}\in C^{\circ}$ with $Q(\mathbf{a})=0$ . Note that if $\mathcal{H}$ is as above then its quadratic form $Q$ is degenerate and negative semidefinite. We believe that such arrangements should be very special and rigid for $n\geq 2$ . For example, in dimension $2$ , their complements are $K(\pi,1)$ , see [PP16, §11]. Hirzebruch arrangements. We analyse the above problem in the particular case that $\mathbf{a}$ is the vector $\mathbf{1}=(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$ . It is easy to see that $\mathbf{1}$ belongs to the kernel of $Q$ precisely when every $H_{i}\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects the other members of $\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H_{i}\}$ along $\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)\cdot N+1$ codimension $2$ subspaces. If $\mathcal{H}$ satisfies this property, and the additional requirement that $\mathbf{1}\in C$ , we say that $\mathcal{H}$ is a Hirzebruch arrangement. We show that complex reflection arrangements, defined by irreducible unitary reflection groups $G\subset U(n+1)$ , are Hirzebruch. It remains an open problem to understand if all Hirzebruch arrangements come from reflection groups. If $n=2$ , this is an old question posed by Hirzebruch in [Hir85, §3], and it has a positive answer for real line arrangements by [Pan18]. The Hirzebruch quadratic form of a matroid. In Section 7.4 , we state our results in the wider context of matroid theory. Given a simple matroid $M$ on the ground set with $N$ elements, we define a quadratic form $Q:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}$ , which we call the Hirzebruch quadratic form of $M$ . Our results show that, if $M$ is representable over $\mathbb{C}$ , then the quadratic form $Q$ is $\leq 0$ on the cone over the matroid polytope of $M$ . 1.1 Main result: klt and CY arrangements Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted hyperplane arrangement. To avoid writing unnecessary indices, we don’t label the elements of the arrangement. Thus, $\mathcal{H}$ is a finite set of pairwise distinct complex hyperplanes $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , and $\mathbf{a}$ is a weight vector in $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ whose components are positive real numbers $a_{H}>0$ indexed by the elements $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the set of non-empty and proper linear subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as intersections of hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ . Klt and CY arrangements. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is: • klt if $\forall\,L\in\mathcal{L}:\,\,\sum_{H\,|\,L\subset H}a_{H}<\codim L\,,$ (1.1) where the sum runs over all the hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ ; • Calabi-Yau, or CY for short, if $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}=n+1\,.$ (1.2) Remark. Equation (1.1) applied to $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{L}$ , together with the assumption that the weights $a_{H}$ are positive, imply that $\forall\,H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,0<a_{H}<1\,.$ Our main result, in its primitive technical form is the following. Theorem 1.1. Suppose that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is a weighted arrangement that is both klt and CY. Then the following inequality holds: $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}\leq 0\,.$ (1.3) The terms in Equation (1.3) are defined as follows: • $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ is the set of codimension $2$ irreducible subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ ; • $a_{L}$ for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ is given by $2\cdot a_{L}=\sum\limits_{H\,|\,L\subset H}a_{H}$ ; • $B_{H}+1$ is equal to the number of elements $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ that are contained in $H$ . Remark 1.2. The set of weights $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ such that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY is equal to the relative interior $P^{\circ}$ of the matroid polytope $P$ of $\mathcal{H}$ as a subset of the hyperplane $\sum a_{H}=n+1$ . It is well-known that, if the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible,11 1 I.e. $\bigcap_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H=\emptyset$ and $\mathcal{H}$ is not isomorphic to a product -as in Definition 2.5- of two non-empty arrangements. then $\dim P=|\mathcal{H}|-1$ and therefore $P^{\circ}$ is non-empty. See [BGW03, Theorem 1.12.9] and Appendix B for a self contained proof. 1.2 Sketch proof of Theorem 1.1 If $n=2$ , then Theorem 1.1 is part of Theorem 1.12 in [Pan09]. Our proof follows the same approach: we work on a logarithmic resolution of the arrangement, define a natural parabolic structure on the pullback tangent bundle, show that it is slope stable, and deduce Theorem 1.1 from Mochizuki’s Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality for parabolic bundles [Moc06]. Next, we present a more detailed account of these steps. The resolution. We take the logarithmic resolution of the arrangement given by the minimal De Concini-Procesi model of $\mathcal{H}$ . This is a smooth projective variety $X$ together with a birational morphism $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ such that $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ is a simple normal crossing divisor. We refer to $X$ as the resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ . The variety $X$ is constructed as an iterated blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along the irreducible subspaces of the arrangement, and it is a particular instance of the wonderful models of subspace arrangements introduced in [DCP95]. Concretely, a linear subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as intersection of hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible, if the localized arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,|\,H\supset L\}$ is irreducible (Definition 2.1). We denote by $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ the set of all non-empty and proper irreducible subspaces. The irreducible components of the divisor $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ are in one to one correspondence with elements $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . Specifically, the irreducible decomposition of $D$ is $D=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}D_{L}\,,$ where $D_{L}$ is the unique irreducible component of $D$ such that $\pi(D_{L})=L$ . The parabolic bundle. Let $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ and let $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}(T\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ be the pullback tangent bundle. We define a natural parabolic bundle (Definition 4.1) $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $(X,D)$ where $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ . The parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is defined by increasing filtrations of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}$ (for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ ) by vector subbundles $F^{L}_{a}$ with $0<a\leq 1$ . The vector subbundles $F^{L}_{a}$ are defined as $F^{L}_{a}=\begin{cases}\pi^{*}(TL)&\text{ if }a<a_{L},\\ \mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}&\text{ if }a\geq a_{L},\end{cases}$ where the weights $a_{L}$ are given by $a_{L}=(\codim L)^{-1}\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\,.$ Note that the klt condition (1.1) implies that $0<a_{L}<1$ . The parabolic first Chern class $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ (Definition 4.7) is an element of the real cohomology group $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})$ . Using the CY condition (1.2), we show that $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0\,.$ The locally abelian property and the parabolic second Chern character. A parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $(X,D)$ is locally abelian (Definition 4.8) if the filtrations that define it satisfy certain compatibility conditions at the intersections $\cap D_{i}$ , where $D_{i}$ are the irreducible components of the divisor $D$ . In a few words, the locally abelian condition requires that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally isomorphic to a direct sum of parabolic line bundles. If the complex dimension is $n=2$ , then every parabolic bundle is locally abelian. However, if $n>2$ , the locally abelian condition imposes strong restrictions on the parabolic structure, and the compatibility conditions are not satisfied for generic choices of filtrations. Theorem 4.29 asserts that the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on the resolution $X$ is locally abelian. The proof relies on the fact that a collection of divisors $D_{L}$ with $L\in\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ has non-empty common intersection $\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}D_{L}$ if and only if the set $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ (Definition 3.11). The locally abelian property of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is used to define the parabolic second Chern character $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\in H^{4}(X,\mathbb{R})$ (Definition 4.17). We calculate $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ in terms of the intersection poset of the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ . Let $h=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))$ be the generator of $H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\mathbb{Z})$ , and let $\gamma_{L}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ -with $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ - be the Poincaré dual of the divisor $D_{L}$ . We derive the formula $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=-\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}+\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,,$ where the last sum is over all pairs $(L,M)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $L\subsetneq M$ . The Picard group of $X$ and the polarization. The Picard group of $X$ is the free abelian group generated by $[\pi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)]$ together with the classes $[D_{L}]$ of the exceptional divisors $D_{L}$ of the birational morphism $\pi:X\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . The exceptional divisors $D_{L}$ correspond to non-empty irreducible subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of codimension $\geq 2$ , we denote the set of all such subspaces by $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}:=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\setminus\mathcal{H}$ . We fix positive integers $b_{L}$ for each $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ , that we call polarization coefficients, such that $P_{k}=k\cdot\pi^{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\big)-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}b_{L}\cdot D_{L}$ is an ample divisor on $X$ for all $k\gg 1$ . Stability of the parabolic bundle. Theorem 5.1 asserts that the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is $P_{k}$ -stable (Definition 4.22) for all $k\gg 1$ . Theorem 5.1 is a central result of this paper, we refer to it as the stability theorem. To prove it, we must show that for every non-zero and proper saturated subsheaf $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ (Definition A.51), we have $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})<0\,,$ where $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is the naturally induced parabolic structure on $\mathcal{V}$ (Definition 4.18). The saturated subsheaf $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ defines a distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ (Section A.4) which, by a slight abuse of notation, we denote by $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . To prove stability, it is easy to reduce to the case where the index $\imath$ (Definition A.57) of the distribution $\mathcal{V}$ is positive (this is essentially a consequence of the fact that, with our conventions, the parabolic degree is less or equal than the degree). We show that, if $\imath\geq 0$ , then $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})\leq\left(\imath\,-\,\sum_{H\,|\,H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\right)\cdot k^{n-1}\,+\,O(k^{n-2})\,.$ The above sum is over all hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that are transverse to $\mathcal{V}$ . The term $O(k^{n-2})$ denotes a polynomial in $k$ of degree at most $n-2$ whose coefficients are uniformly bounded in absolute value by a number that depends only on the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ and the polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ . Given the above inequality for $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})$ , the key estimate necessary to prove the stability theorem is provided by Proposition 5.10. This proposition asserts that, if the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY, then there is $\delta>0$ such that, for every distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of index $\imath\geq 0$ the following holds: $\sum_{H\,|\,H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\geq\imath+\delta\,.$ The stability Theorem 5.1 follows from this. The Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality. Since the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is stable with respect to the polarization $P_{k}$ for all $k\gg 1$ and $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0$ , the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality [Moc06, Theorem 6.5] implies that $\forall k\gg 1:\,\,c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\leq 0\,.$ The cup product $p(k)=c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ defines a polynomial of degree $n-2$ in $k$ that we write as $p(k)=C_{n-2}\cdot k^{n-2}+O(k^{n-3})\,.$ The leading order coefficient of $p(k)$ is given by $C_{n-2}=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ . More geometrically, the coefficient $C_{n-2}$ is the parabolic second Chern character of the restriction of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ to a generic $2$ -plane. Note that, since $p(k)<0$ for $k\gg 1$ , we must have $C_{n-2}\leq 0$ . Using results on the cohomology ring of $X$ from Section 3.2 together with our previous formula for $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ , we show that $C_{n-2}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}\,.$ Theorem 1.1 follows from this together with the inequality $C_{n-2}\leq 0$ . 1.3 Context: PK manifolds Our motivation for proving Theorem 1.1 comes from the study of polyhedral Kähler (PK) manifolds. Let $X$ be a complex manifold and let $D\subset X$ be a complex hypersurface with irreducible decomposition $D=\cup_{i}D_{i}$ . We say that $g$ is a PK metric on $X$ with cone angles $2\pi\alpha_{i}$ along $D_{i}$ if the following conditions hold: (i) $g$ is a flat Kähler metric on $X\setminus D$ ; (ii) the metric completion of $g$ is a polyhedral manifold $M$ and the inclusion map $X\setminus D\subset X$ extends continuously to $M$ as a homeomorphism $M\cong X$ ; (iii) if $p\in D_{i}$ is a smooth point of $D$ then $g$ is holomorphically isometric near $p$ to the product of a $2$ -cone of total angle $2\pi\alpha_{i}$ with a flat Euclidean factor $\mathbb{C}^{n-1}$ . The Levi-Civita connection $\nabla$ of a PK metric is a meromorphic flat torsion-free unitary connection on $TX$ with simple poles along the hypersurfaces $D_{i}$ . The residues of $\nabla$ give a number of topological relations on the pair $(X,D)$ . Conjecturally, these topological relations characterize the existence of PK metrics. In the particular case when $X=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $D$ is a union of hyperplanes, we believe that these topological relations can be derived from quadratic expression (1.3) and we propose the following. Conjecture 1.3. If equality holds in (1.3) then there is a PK metric on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with cone angles $2\pi\alpha_{H}$ along the hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , with $\alpha_{H}=1-a_{H}$ . As we already mentioned, this conjecture holds for $n=2$ by [Pan09, Theorem 1.12]. The proof for $n=3$ , under some additional stability assumptions and applying the foundational results established in [dBP21], will be the subject of a forthcoming paper. 1.4 Outline Section 2 contains background material on hyperplane arrangements. In Section 3 , we review the minimal De Concini-Procesi model $X$ of $\mathcal{H}$ , calculate certain cup products in the integer cohomology $H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ , and introduce the polarizations $P_{k}$ used in the stability theorem. In Section 4 , we define the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ , show that it is locally abelian, and calculate its first and second Chern characters. In Section 5 , we show that the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is $P_{k}$ -stable for $k\gg 1$ and deduce our main Theorem 1.1 from this. In Section 6 , we introduce the quadratic form $Q$ of the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ , we define the semistable cone $C$ , and show that $C\subset\{Q\leq 0\}$ (Theorem 6.29). In Section 7 , we introduce the class of Hirzebruch arrangements, show that complex reflection arrangements defined by irreducible unitary reflection groups belong to this class, and define the quadratic form $Q$ in the broader context of matroids. We propose an extension (Conjecture 7.42) of Theorem 6.29 to pseudoline arrangements, which relates to Bogomolov-Miyaoka-Yau inequality for symplectic $4$ -maniolds. Appendix A contains supplementary results needed in our proofs. We collect some basic results on filtrations of vector spaces, adapted basis, and nested sets; which are used in the proof of the locally abelian property of our parabolic bundle. We also provide some background results on exterior algebra, saturated subsheaves, and distributions on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ ; that are used in the proof of stability of our parabolic bundle. In Appendix B , we prove that if $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible then the stable cone $C^{\circ}$ is non-empty. In Appendix C , we show that if the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY then there is a (unique up to scale) weak Ricci-flat Kähler metric on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ whose volume form has prescribed singularities of conical type along the hyperplanes of the arrangement. Acknowledgments We thank Nikolai Mnëv, Artie Prendergast-Smith, and Calum Spicer for useful discussions. We also thank Jorge Vitório Pereira for giving us Example 5.8. 2 Hyperplane arrangements In Section 2.1 , we review standard terminology related to hyperplane arrangements. In Section 2.2 , we introduce irreducible arrangements and subspaces, and discuss their basic properties. In Section 2.3 , we consider weighted arrangements. We show that if the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY, then $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible. This implies that there are no non-zero holomorphic vector fields on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ that are tangent to all the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ . We will make use of this fact later, in Section 5, to show that our parabolic bundle is stable. 2.1 Basic definitions Let $\mathbb{CP}^{n}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})$ be the complex projective space of dimension $n$ , $\mathbb{CP}^{n}=\left(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\right)\big/\,\mathbb{C}^{*}\,.$ A hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ , or an arrangement for short, is a finite collection of complex hyperplanes $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . We don’t allow multiplicities, meaning that the elements of $\mathcal{H}$ are distinct hyperplanes. By abuse of notation, we write $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ if we want to emphasize the ambient projective space. Two arrangements $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{K}$ are isomorphic if they are linearly equivalent, i.e., if there is an element of $\PGL(n+1,\mathbb{C})$ that maps the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ to the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{K}$ . Linear subspaces. A (projective or linear) subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the image of a linear subspace of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ under the quotient projection $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}.$ We write $\mathbb{P}(L^{\mathbf{c}})=L$ , where $L^{\mathbf{c}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ is the unique linear subspace that projects to $L$ . Similarly, $\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{c}}$ denotes the linear arrangement of hyperplanes $H^{\mathbf{c}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , and we write $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{c}})=\mathcal{H}$ . The codimension of $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is equal to the codimension of the linear subspace $L^{\mathbf{c}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . We also consider the empty subset of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ to be a linear subspace with $\emptyset^{\mathbf{c}}=\{0\}$ and $\codim\emptyset=n+1$ . Centre and essential arrangements. The centre $T$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is the common intersection of all its members, i.e., $T=\bigcap_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H.$ We say that $\mathcal{H}$ is essential if $T=\emptyset$ , or alternatively, if the common intersection of the elements of $\mathcal{H}^{\mathbf{c}}$ is the origin $\{0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Clearly, $\mathcal{H}$ is essential if and only if it contains $n+1$ linearly independent hyperplanes. Sum and complementary subspaces. If $U$ and $V$ are two projective subspaces of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ then their sum (or join) $U+V$ is the smallest projective subspace that contains both $U$ and $V$ . Equivalently, $U+V=\mathbb{P}(U^{\mathbf{c}}+V^{\mathbf{c}})\,,$ where $U^{\mathbf{c}}+V^{\mathbf{c}}$ denotes the usual sum of vector subspaces of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . The subspaces $U$ and $V$ are said to be complimentary if $U+V=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $U\cap V=\emptyset$ . In this case, we also say that $V$ is a complement of $U$ , and vice-versa. Essentialization. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an arrangement with centre $T$ . Choose a subspace $S$ that is a complement of $T$ . The essentialization of $\mathcal{H}$ is the arrangement given by $\mathcal{H}/T=\{H\cap S\text{ with }H\in\mathcal{H}\}.$ Since $T\cap S=\emptyset$ , the arrangement $\mathcal{H}/T$ is essential. Suppose that $S^{\prime}$ is another complement of $T$ and let $f:S\to S^{\prime}$ be the projective transformation which maps $p\in S$ to $p^{\prime}=f(p)$ with $p^{\prime}=(p+T)\cap S^{\prime}$ . Since every hyperplane $H\in\mathcal{H}$ contains $T$ , it follows that $f$ maps the intersection $H\cap S$ to $H\cap S^{\prime}$ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ ; showing that $\mathcal{H}/T$ is independent of the choice of complement. Intersection poset. The intersection poset $\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is the set of all subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as intersection of hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ . We equip $\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ with the partial order given by reverse inclusion. The intersection poset $\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ has a unique minimal element $L=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ corresponding to the intersection over the empty subset of $\mathcal{H}$ and a unique maximal element $L=T$ given by the centre of the arrangement. We will mainly work with the subposet $\mathcal{L}\subset\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ consisting of all non-empty and proper intersection subspaces. In other words, we exclude $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $\emptyset$ (if $\mathcal{H}$ is essential) from $\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ and let $\mathcal{L}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}\,\setminus\,\{\emptyset,\,\mathbb{CP}^{n}\}$ . Localization and multiplicity. The localization of $\mathcal{H}$ at a subspace $L\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ is the set of all hyperplanes of the arrangement that contain $L$ , $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\{H\in\mathcal{H}\,\text{ such that }\,L\subset H\}.$ The centre of $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is equal to $L$ . The link of $\mathcal{H}$ at $L$ is the essential arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{L}/L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{m}$ where $m=\codim L-1$ . The multiplicity $m_{L}$ of $L$ is the number of hyperplanes that contain $L$ , i.e., $m_{L}=|\mathcal{H}_{L}|$ . Induced arrangement and complements. Let $L\in\mathcal{L}$ . The induced arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{L}$ is the hyperplane arrangement obtained by intersecting $L$ with the elements $H\in\mathcal{H}$ such that $L\not\subset H$ , $\mathcal{H}^{L}=\{H\cap L\textup{ with }H\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{L}\}.$ The complement of $\mathcal{H}^{L}$ in $L$ is denoted by $L^{\circ}$ , i.e., $L^{\circ}=L\setminus\bigcup_{H|L\not\subset H}(L\cap H).$ Similarly, we write $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ for the arrangement complement, $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}H\,.$ 2.2 Irreducible arrangements and subspaces A splitting of a hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ consists of two subsets $\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathcal{H}$ satisfying the following two properties. (i) Every hyperplane of $\mathcal{H}$ belongs to either $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ or $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ , i.e., $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\bigcup\mathcal{H}_{2}\,.$ (2.1) (ii) If $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ are the centres of $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ , then $T_{1}+T_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\,.$ (2.2) The splitting is non-trivial if furthermore: (iii) Both $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ are non-empty. We write $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}$ to indicate that $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ form a splitting of $\mathcal{H}$ . It follows from Equation (2.2) that the union (2.1) is disjoint. If $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}$ then $\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}_{T_{1}}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}=\mathcal{H}_{T_{2}}$ . If the splitting is non-trivial then $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ belong to the poset $\mathcal{L}$ of non-empty and proper hyperplane intersections. Definition 2.1. The arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible if it admits a non-trivial splitting. The arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible if it is not reducible. Example 2.2. The empty arrangement is irreducible. An arrangement consisting of only one hyperplane is irreducible. An arrangement made of two hyperplanes is always reducible. Example 2.3. If $n=1$ then $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ is reducible if and only if $|\mathcal{H}|=2$ . Indeed, if $|\mathcal{H}|\geq 3$ and $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}$ is a splitting of $\mathcal{H}$ , then at least one of the subarrangements, say $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ , must contain $2$ or more points, so $T_{1}=\emptyset$ and $T_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ , i.e., $\mathcal{H}_{2}=\emptyset$ ; showing that $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible. Lemma 2.4. An arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible if and only if, up to a linear change of coordinates, we can write $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\cup\mathcal{H}_{2}$ where $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ are non-empty and the defining linear equations for the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ share no common variables. Proof. Suppose that $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible and let $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}$ be a non-trivial splitting of $\mathcal{H}$ . Take linear coordinates $x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1}$ in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $T_{1}=\{x_{i}=0\textup{ for }i\in I_{1}\}\,\,\textup{ and }\,\,T_{2}=\{x_{i}=0\textup{ for }i\in I_{2}\}$ where $I_{1}$ and $I_{2}$ are subsets of $[n+1]=\{1,\ldots,n+1\}$ . The defining linear equation for the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}_{1}$ depend only on the variables $x_{i}$ for $i\in I_{1}$ and similarly for $\mathcal{H}_{2}$ . We will show that $I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\emptyset$ . The subspace $T_{1}$ is the span in projective space of the vectors $\partial_{x_{i}}$ for $i$ in the complement index set $I^{c}_{1}=[n+1]\setminus I_{1}$ and similarly for $T_{2}$ . The fact that $T_{1}+T_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ implies that $I_{1}^{c}\cup I_{2}^{c}=[n+1]$ . Taking complements we get that $I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\emptyset$ . The converse is similar. ∎ Definition 2.5. Let $\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{2}}$ be two arrangements. Let $\mathbb{CP}^{n}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}+1}\times\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}+1})$ with $n=n_{1}+n_{2}+1$ . Embed the projective spaces $\mathbb{CP}^{n_{i}}$ as disjoint subspaces $P_{i}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $P_{1}+P_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ given by $P_{1}=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}+1}\times\{0\}\right)\,\,\textup{ and }\,\,P_{2}=\mathbb{P}\left(\{0\}\times\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}+1}\right)\,.$ The product arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is obtained by taking the joins $H_{1}+P_{2}$ for $H_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}$ together with $P_{1}+H_{2}$ for $H_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}$ . Remark 2.6. The arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}$ is the projectivization of $\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\mathbf{c}}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\mathbf{c}}$ , where $\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\mathbf{c}}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\mathbf{c}}$ is the usual product of linear arrangements in a vector space as defined in [OT92, Definition 2.13]. From the Lemma 2.4 , we have the following. Corollary 2.7. The arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible if and only if it is linearly isomorphic to the product of two non-empty arrangements. Remark 2.8. Every arrangement is linearly isomorphic to the product of irreducible arrangements. Moreover, this decomposition is unique up to re-labelling of the factors. See [dBP21, Lemma 2.32]. Remark 2.9. An arrangement is reducible if and only if the Euler characteristic of its complement is equal to zero. One direction is easy, namely if $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible then there is a free $\mathbb{C}^{*}$ -action on $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ , hence its Euler characteristic is zero. The converse is more subtle and involves the theory of Möbius functions, see [STV95, Theorem 5 (2)]. The next result will be crucial in the proof of Theorem 5.1 . Lemma 2.10. Suppose that $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is essential and irreducible. If $Y$ is a holomorphic vector field on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ that is tangent to all the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ then $Y=0$ . Proof. The vector field $Y$ is given by a linear transformation $f$ of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . The tangency condition means that $f(H^{\mathbf{c}})\subset H^{\mathbf{c}}$ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . Let $g=f^{*}$ be the dual action on $(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ . Choose defining linear equations $\ell_{H}$ for the hyperplanes of the arrangement. The tangency condition implies that $g(\ell_{H})=\lambda_{H}\cdot\ell_{H}$ for some $\lambda_{H}\in\mathbb{C}$ . Since $\mathcal{H}$ is essential, we can take $n+1$ linearly independent hyperplanes $H_{1},\ldots H_{n+1}$ whose corresponding defining equations $\ell_{i}$ make a basis of $(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ of eigenvectors of $g$ with eigenvalues $\lambda_{i}$ . We claim that $\lambda_{1}=\ldots=\lambda_{n+1}$ . If not, let $W_{1}$ be the $\lambda_{1}$ -eigenspace of $g$ and let $W_{2}$ be the direct sum of all the other eigenspaces. Then $\mathcal{H}=\{H\,\,|\,\,\ell_{H}\in W_{1}\}\uplus\{H\,\,|\,\,\ell_{H}\in W_{2}\}$ is a non-trivial splitting of $\mathcal{H}$ , contradicting irreducibility. We conclude that $f$ is a scalar multiple of the identity, hence $Y=0$ . ∎ Remark 2.11. If $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible, then the proof above shows that the stabilizer of $\mathcal{H}$ , consisting of all elements in $PGL(n,\mathbb{C})$ that preserve each of the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ , is trivial. In particular, the automorphism group of $\mathcal{H}$ , consisting of linear isomorphisms that permute the members of $\mathcal{H}$ , is finite. Definition 2.12. A subspace $L\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ is irreducible if the localization $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is an irreducible arrangement. Similarly, a subspace $L$ is reducible if $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is reducible. Example 2.13. If $L=\emptyset$ then $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\mathcal{H}$ , so $L$ is irreducible if and only if $\mathcal{H}$ is. In the other extreme case, if $L=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , then $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\emptyset$ , which is always irreducible. Example 2.14. The hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ are irreducible subspaces. Example 2.15. A subspace $L\in\mathcal{L}$ of codimension $2$ is irreducible if and only if its multiplicity $m_{L}$ is $\geq 3$ . Example 2.16. If $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is an irreducible subspace of codimension $\geq 2$ then $m_{L}>\codim L$ . However, if $\codim L\geq 3$ then the converse is not true. For example, consider the arrangement in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ made of $3$ planes intersecting along a line $L$ together with an extra plane $H$ transverse to $L$ . The intersection point $p=L\cap H$ has multiplicity $4$ but the point $p$ is a reducible subspace. Notation 2.17. Write $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{i}$ for the set of non-empty and proper irreducible subspaces, where $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{i}$ is the subset of all $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $\dim L=i$ . In particular, $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-1}$ is equal to $\mathcal{H}$ . Definition 2.18. Let $L\in\mathcal{L}$ . The irreducible components of $L$ are the maximal (with respect to the order in $\mathcal{L}$ by reverse inclusion) elements of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ that contain $L$ . The term irreducible component is taken from [CHL05, §2.1]. Notation 2.19. Write $\Irr(L)$ for the irreducible components of $L$ . In particular, $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is reducible if and only if $|\Irr(L)|>1$ . Lemma 2.20. Let $L\in\mathcal{L}$ and let $\Irr(L)=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\}$ . Then the following holds. (i) If $M\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ contains $L$ then $M\supset L_{i}$ for some $1\leq i\leq k$ . (ii) $L$ is the transversal intersection22 2 See Definition A.21 . of the subspaces $L_{i}$ , i.e., $L=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}\hskip 5.69054pt\textup{ and }\hskip 5.69054pt\codim L=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\codim L_{i}\,.$ (2.3) Proof. Item (i) follows immediately from the definition. Item (ii) follows from the fact that the arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{L}/L$ is linearly isomorphic to the product $\mathcal{H}_{L}/L\cong(\mathcal{H}_{L_{1}}/L_{1})\times\ldots\times(\mathcal{H}_{L_{k}}/L_{k})\,,$ see [dBP21, Lemma 2.44]. ∎ Lemma 2.21. Let $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ be two irreducible subspaces such that their intersection $L=L_{1}\cap L_{2}$ is non-empty and reducible. Then $\Irr(L)=\{L_{1},L_{2}\}$ . In particular, $L_{1}+L_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Proof. Let $\Irr(L)=\{L_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,L_{p}^{\prime}\}$ . We want to show that $p=2$ and that, up to a re-label, $L_{1}=L_{1}^{\prime}$ and $L_{2}=L_{2}^{\prime}$ . Since $L_{1}$ is irreducible, it must contain one of the irreducible components of $L$ , say $L_{1}\supset L_{1}^{\prime}$ . Similarly, $L_{2}\supset L_{i}^{\prime}$ for some $i$ . We claim that $i\neq 1$ . Indeed, if $L_{2}\supset L_{1}^{\prime}$ then $L=L_{1}\cap L_{2}$ would contain $L_{1}^{\prime}$ but $L\subsetneq L_{1}^{\prime}$ . We can assume that $L_{2}\supset L_{2}^{\prime}$ . Since $L=L_{1}\cap L_{2}$ and $\codim L_{i}\leq\codim L_{i}^{\prime}$ for $i=1,2$ ; we get that $\codim L\leq\codim L_{1}+\codim L_{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\codim L_{i}^{\prime}=\codim L,$ where the last equality follows from Equation (2.3). We conclude that all inequalities must be equalities. In particular, $p=2$ and $\codim L_{i}=\codim L_{i}^{\prime}$ for $i=1,2$ ; therefore $L_{1}=L_{1}^{\prime}$ and $L_{2}=L_{2}^{\prime}$ . ∎ Notation 2.22. Let $L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ , we write $L_{1}\pitchfork L_{2}$ if $L_{1}\cap L_{2}$ is non-empty and reducible. Example 2.23. Let $H_{1}$ and $H_{2}$ be two hyperplanes of the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ and let $L$ be their intersection. Then $H_{1}\pitchfork H_{2}$ if and only if $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\{H_{1},H_{2}\}$ . 2.3 Weighted arrangements Definition 2.24. A weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is a hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ together with a weight vector $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ whose components are positive real numbers $a_{H}>0$ indexed by the elements $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted arrangement. For an arbitrary non-empty and proper linear subspace $L\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , consider the equation $\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}<\codim L\,.$ (2.4) Lemma 2.25. The following conditions are equivalent: (i) Equation (2.4) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ ; (ii) Equation (2.4) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ ; (iii) Equation (2.4) holds for every non-empty and proper linear subspace $L\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Proof. Let us show first that (i) implies (ii). Let $L\in\mathcal{L}$ and consider its irreducible decomposition $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\mathcal{H}_{L_{1}}\uplus\ldots\uplus\mathcal{H}_{L_{k}}.$ (2.5) Each $L_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and $L=\cap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}$ is their common transverse intersection. In particular, $\codim L=\codim L_{1}+\ldots+\codim L_{k}.$ (2.6) Equation (2.5) implies that every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contains $L$ must contain exactly one of the irreducible components $L_{i}$ . It follows that $\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}=\sum_{H|L_{1}\subset H}a_{H}+\ldots+\sum_{H|L_{k}\subset H}a_{H}$ (2.7) Applying Equation (2.4) to each $L_{i}$ together with Equations (2.7) and (2.6), implies that Equation (2.4) holds for $L$ . This finishes the proof that (i) implies (ii). Let us now show that (ii) implies (iii). Let $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a linear subspace and let $L^{\prime}$ be the common intersection of all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ . If there are no hyperplanes of the arrangement that contain $L$ then Equation (2.4) is trivially satisfied because the left hand side is equal to zero and $\codim L\geq 1$ . Since $L\subset L^{\prime}$ , we get that $L^{\prime}$ is non-empty and therefore $L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}$ . Applying Equation (2.4) to $L^{\prime}$ we obtain $\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}=\sum_{H|L^{\prime}\subset H}a_{H}<\codim L^{\prime}\leq\codim L.$ This finishes the proof that (ii) implies (iii). Since (iii) clearly implies (i), the lemma follows. ∎ As a direct consequence of Lemma 2.25 , We obtain $3$ slightly different but equivalent formulations of the klt condition. Corollary 2.26. Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted arrangement with weights $a_{H}>0$ . Then $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt if and only if any of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied: (i) Equation (2.4) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ ; (ii) Equation (2.4) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ ; (iii) Equation (2.4) holds for every non-empty and proper linear subspace $L\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . The next result will be crucial in Section 5. Lemma 2.27. If $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY then $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible. Proof. If the centre $T$ of the arrangement is non-empty then the klt condition applied to $L=T$ implies that $\sum_{H}a_{H}<n$ but this contradicts the CY condition $\sum_{H}a_{H}=n+1$ . Therefore, $\mathcal{H}$ is essential. If $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible then there are two linear subspaces $L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathcal{L}$ such that $L_{1}+L_{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ contains either $L_{1}$ or $L_{2}$ . Since $\mathcal{H}$ is essential, the subspaces $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ are disjoint, thus $\codim L_{1}+\codim L_{2}=n+1$ . It follows that $\displaystyle\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle=\sum_{H|L_{1}\subset H}a_{H}+\sum_{H|L_{2}\subset H}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle<\codim L_{1}+\codim L_{2}=n+1\,,$ which contradicts the CY condition. ∎ Lemmas 2.27 and 2.10 together yield the following: Corollary 2.28. If $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY then there are no non-zero holomorphic vector fields tangent to all the members of $\mathcal{H}$ . Recall that the multiplicity $m_{L}$ of a subspace $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is the number of hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ . The next results asserts that arrangements with no subspaces of relatively high multiplicity are irreducible. Corollary 2.29. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a non-empty arrangement such that $\forall\,L\in\mathcal{L}:\,\,m_{L}<\codim L\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{H}|}{n+1}.$ (2.8) Then $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible. Proof. Since for $L\in\mathcal{L}$ the multiplicity $m_{L}$ is always greater or equal than $\codim L$ , we must have $|\mathcal{H}|>n+1$ . Consider the CY weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ where all weights $a_{H}$ are equal to $(n+1)/|\mathcal{H}|$ . As $|\mathcal{H}|>n+1$ , we have $0<a_{H}<1$ . Equation (2.8) guarantees that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt. The result follows from Lemma 2.27 . ∎ 3 The resolution In Section 3.1 , we recall a canonical compactification $X$ of an arrangement complement that replaces $\mathcal{H}$ with a simple normal crossing divisor. This compactification is a particular instance of the wonderful models of subspace arrangements, introduced by De Concini and Procesi [DCP95]. We review the construction of $X$ as an iterated blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along linear subspaces and recall the notion of nested set. The upshot is that the common intersection $\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}D_{L}$ is non-empty, where $\{D_{L}\,|\,L\in\mathcal{S}\}$ is a collection of irreducible components of $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ , if and only if the set $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . In Section 3.2 , we discuss the Picard group of $X$ . We also calculate some intersection numbers in the cohomology ring $H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ . We will make use of the results on the Picard group and cohomology ring of $X$ later, in Sections 4 and 5, in calculations of parabolic Chern classes of our parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ and their products with the polarizations $P_{k}$ . In Section 3.3 , we introduce the polarizations $P_{k}$ on $X$ which are used in the stability Theorem 5.1 . 3.1 The minimal De Concini-Procesi model Before introducing De Concini and Procesi’s wonderful models, we recall, as a warmup, one of the standard constructions of the blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along a linear subspace. Let $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a linear subspace and let $P_{L}$ be a complementary subspace, that is $L+P_{L}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $L\cap P_{L}=\emptyset$ . The linear projection $\pr_{L}:\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus L\to P_{L}\,,$ sends a point $p\in\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus L$ to the intersection of $L+p$ with $P_{L}$ . The blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along $L$ is the map $B\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , where $B\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}\times P_{L}$ is the closure of the graph of $\pr_{L}$ and $\sigma$ is the restriction to $B$ of the projection to the first factor. Consider now a hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Let $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ be the arrangement complement and let $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ be the set of non-empty and proper irreducible subspaces. For each $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ choose a complementary subspace $P_{L}$ and define $\pr:(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}\to\prod_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}P_{L}\,,$ (3.1) with components the linear projections $\pr_{L}$ . Definition 3.1. Let $X\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}\times\prod P_{L}$ be the closure of the graph of (3.1) and let $\pi:X\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ (3.2) be the restriction to $X$ of the projection to the first factor. The resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ is the variety $X$ together with the map $\pi$ . For brevity, we will also refer to it simply as the resolution $X$ , omitting the map $\pi$ , or as the resolution $\pi$ , omitting the variety $X$ , depending on which aspect we wish to emphasize. Remark 3.2. De Concini and Procesi define a resolution $X_{\mathcal{G}}$ for any building set $\mathcal{G}$ of the poset $\mathcal{L}$ [DCP95, §2.3]. The irreducible subspaces $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ form a building set of $\mathcal{L}$ and any building set of $\mathcal{L}$ contains $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . Definition 3.1 corresponds to $X=X_{\mathcal{G}}$ with $\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . If $\mathcal{G}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{G}_{2}$ are building sets with $\mathcal{G}_{1}\subset\mathcal{G}_{2}$ , then $X_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}$ is obtained from $X_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}$ by performing a finite sequence of blowups along smooth subvarieties. For this reason, (3.2) is known as the minimal De Concini-Procesi model of $\mathcal{H}$ . The map $\pi$ is a bijection on the preimage of $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ with inverse $\imath(x)=(x,\pr(x))$ . By slight abuse of notation, suppressing $\imath$ , we can regard $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ as an open subset of $X$ and write $X\setminus(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H}).$ The main result that we are after is the next. Theorem 3.3 ([DCP95, §3.2 and §4.2]). The variety $X$ is smooth and the preimage of the arrangement $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ is a simple normal crossing divisor whose irreducible components are in one to one correspondence with the elements of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . More precisely, $D=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}D_{L},$ (3.3) where $D_{L}$ is the unique irreducible component of $D$ such that $\pi(D_{L})=L$ . Remark 3.4. It is shown in [DCP95, $\lx@sectionsign 1.6$ ] that $D_{L}$ is the closure of $\pi^{-1}(L^{\circ})$ where $L^{\circ}$ is the complement of the induced arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{L}$ . Moreover, [DCP95, $\lx@sectionsign 4.3$ ] shows that $D_{L}$ is biholomorphic to the product $D_{L}\cong X({\mathcal{H}^{L}})\times X({\mathcal{H}_{L}/L})$ where $X(\mathcal{K})$ denotes the minimal De Concini-Procesi model of the arrangement $\mathcal{K}$ . 3.1.1 Construction of $X$ as an iterated blowup We present a more hands-on description of $X$ as an iterated blowup. Before stating the result, we recall first the notion of blowup along a submanifold. If $N$ is a complex manifold and $S\subset N$ is a complex submanifold, then the blowup of $N$ along $S$ is a complex manifold $M$ together with a proper holomorphic map $M\xrightarrow{\sigma}N$ such that $\sigma:M\setminus E\cong N\setminus S$ is a biholomorphism and the restriction of $\sigma$ to $E$ is equivalent to the bundle projection $\mathbb{P}(N_{S})\to S$ , where $\mathbb{P}(N_{S})$ is the projectivization of the normal bundle $N_{S}$ of $S\subset N$ . Next, we recall the notions of proper transform and exceptional divisor in a slightly more general context. Let $M$ and $N$ be complex manifolds of the same dimension and let $f:M\to N$ be a proper holomorphic map of degree $1$ . Let $E\subset M$ be the set of critical points of $f$ and let $S=f(E)$ be the critical values, so that $f:M\setminus E\to N\setminus S$ is a biholomorphism. We recall the following notions. • If $V$ is a subvariety of $N$ with $V\not\subset S$ then the proper transform of $V$ by $f$ is the subvariety of $M$ obtained as the closure of $f^{-1}(V\setminus S)$ . • A divisor $D\subset M$ is $f$ -exceptional if $f(D)$ is an analytic subset of $N$ of codimension $\geq 2$ . In our case of interest, $f$ is a composition of blowups along complex submanifolds. Furthermore, the set of critical points is a (possibly reducible) $f$ -exceptional divisor. Example 3.5. The irreducible $\pi$ -exceptional divisors of the resolution (3.2) are precisely of the form $D_{L}$ with $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ of codimension $\geq 2$ . Notation 3.6. Let $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\setminus\mathcal{H}$ . In other words, $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ is the set of non-empty irreducible subspaces of codimension $\geq 2$ . Proposition 3.7 ([Li09, Proposition 2.13]). Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be a labelling of all the elements in $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ compatible with the inclusion relations, i.e., if $L_{i}\subset L_{j}$ then $i\leq j$ . Then the resolution (3.2) is equal to $X_{k}\xrightarrow{\pi_{k}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , where the maps $X_{i}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ for $1\leq i\leq k$ are defined inductively as follows: 1. $X_{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along $L_{1}$ and $\pi_{1}=\sigma_{1}$ ; 2. Let $i\geq 2$ and suppose that $\pi_{i-1}:X_{i-1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is defined. Let $\widetilde{L}_{i}$ be the proper transform of $L_{i}$ by $\pi_{i-1}$ . Then $\widetilde{L}_{i}$ is smooth and $\pi_{i}=\pi_{i-1}\circ\sigma_{i}$ where $\sigma_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i-1}$ is the blowup of $X_{i-1}$ along $\widetilde{L}_{i}$ . Proposition 3.7 guarantees that the end result $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is independent of the labelling, as long as it is compatible with inclusion relations. For example, we can order the elements of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ by increasing dimension $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{0},\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ , where the members of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{i}$ are taken in any order, c.f. [OT07, Theorem 4.2.4]. The following three examples illustrate the proposition. Example 3.8. If $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ then $X$ is obtained by blowing up the points $p\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ of multiplicity $m_{p}\geq 3$ . Example 3.9. If $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ then $X$ is obtained in two steps. • Step 1: blowup the points in $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{0}$ . • Step 2: blowup of the proper transforms $\widetilde{L}$ of the lines $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{1}$ . Here, if $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ are two irreducible lines meeting at a point $p$ , then, by Lemma 2.21, the intersection point $p$ must be irreducible. Therefore, the proper transforms $\widetilde{L}$ in Step 2 are mutually disjoint. Example 3.10. Let $P$ and $Q$ be two projective planes in $\mathbb{CP}^{4}$ meeting at a single point. Consider the arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{4}$ made of $6$ hyperplanes, $3$ of which intersect along $P$ and the other $3$ intersect along $Q$ . This arrangement contains no irreducible points and no irreducible lines. The resolution $X$ is obtained by blowing up one of the planes in $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{2}=\{P,Q\}$ and then blowing up the proper transform of the other plane. The result is independent of the order in which the two planes are blown up. In an affine chart where $P=\{0\}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}$ and $Q=\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\{0\}$ the resolution is the product $\Bl_{0}\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\Bl_{0}\mathbb{C}^{2}\xrightarrow{(\sigma,\sigma)}\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}$ where $\Bl_{0}\mathbb{C}^{2}\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbb{C}^{2}$ is the blowup of $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ at the origin. 3.1.2 Nested subsets of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and intersections of divisors $D_{L}$ Next, we analyse when a collection of divisors $D_{L}$ with $L$ ranging over a subset $\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ has non-empty intersection. To do this, we recall the following. Definition 3.11 ([DCP95, §2.4]). Let $\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ be a subset of irreducible subspaces. The set $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ if, for any $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ with $k\geq 2$ pairwise non-comparable (i.e. $L_{i}\not\subset L_{j}$ for $i\neq j$ ), their common intersection $\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}$ is non-empty and reducible. Remark 3.12. If $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ are pairwise non-comparable, then the intersections $\bigcap_{i\in I}L_{i}$ are non-empty and reducible for any subset $I\subset[k]$ with $|I|\geq 2$ . Example 3.13. If $L,M\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ then $\mathcal{S}=\{L,M\}$ is nested if and only if either one subspace is contained in the other or the intersection $L\cap M$ is non-empty and reducible. Example 3.14. Consider the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ of $4$ hyperplanes with defining equations $x_{1}=0\,,\,x_{2}=0\,,\,x_{1}+x_{2}=0$ , and $x_{3}=0$ . Then the set $\mathcal{S}$ of hyperplanes $x_{1}=0\,,\,x_{2}=0\,,\,x_{3}=0$ doesn’t form a nested set. Indeed, the intersection of $x_{1}=0$ with $x_{2}=0$ is not reducible (while all other intersections are reducible). Next, we provide an extension of Lemma 2.21 that will be useful later on. Recall that we write $\Irr(L)$ for the irreducible components of $L\in\mathcal{L}$ . Lemma 3.15. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ be pairwise non-comparable. If $L=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}$ is their common intersection, then $\Irr(L)=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\}$ . In particular, the subspaces $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ intersect transversely. Proof. Let $\Irr(L)=\{L_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,L_{p}^{\prime}\}$ . We want to show that $p=k$ and that, up to a relabel, $L_{i}=L_{i}^{\prime}$ for all $1\leq i\leq k$ . Since $L_{i}$ is irreducible, it must contain one (and only one) of the irreducible components of $L$ , say $L_{i}\supset L_{\sigma(i)}^{\prime}$ . Clearly, $L^{\prime}_{j}\subset\bigcap_{i\,|\,\sigma(i)=j}L_{i}:=M_{j}$ and $\bigcap_{j=1}^{p}M_{j}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}=L\,.$ Therefore, $\codim L\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p}\codim M_{j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{p}\codim L_{j}^{\prime}=\codim L\,.$ Thus, we must have $\codim M_{j}=\codim L_{j}^{\prime}$ for all $j$ , hence $\forall j\in[p]:\,\,L^{\prime}_{j}=M_{j}\,.$ On the other hand, since the elements of $\{L_{i}\,|\,\sigma(i)=j\}\subset\mathcal{S}$ are pairwise non-comparable, their common intersection $M_{j}$ must be reducible if $|\sigma^{-1}(j)|\geq 2$ . Since $M_{j}=L^{\prime}_{j}$ is irreducible, we must have $|\sigma^{-1}(j)|=1$ for all $j$ . We conclude that $\sigma$ is a bijection, so $p=k$ and we can relabel so that $L_{i}=L^{\prime}_{i}$ for all $i$ . ∎ The main point of introducing nested sets is that we have the following. Proposition 3.16 ([DCP95, §3.2]). Let $\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . The intersection $\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}D_{L}$ is non-empty if and only if $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . In particular, from Example 3.13, we have. Corollary 3.17. Two divisors $D_{L}$ and $D_{M}$ intersect if and only if one of the following two cases happens: (i) $L\subset M$ or $M\subset L$ ; (ii) $L\cap M$ is non-empty and reducible. 3.1.3 Example: tetrahedron in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ Let $p_{1},\ldots,p_{5}\in\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ be five points in general linear position, i.e., no $3$ points lie on a line and no $4$ points lie on a plane. We can represent these five points as the four vertices of a tetrahedron in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ together with its barycentre. Each triplet of points determines a plane, giving rise to a collection of $10$ planes $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ -see Figure 1 . $p_{1}$ $p_{3}$ $p_{4}$ $p_{2}$ $p_{5}$ $H_{123}$ $L_{12}$ Figure 1: The arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ of $10$ planes in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ spanned by triplets of $5$ points in general linear position. The irreducible subspaces of the arrangement are $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{2}=\{H_{ijk}=\overline{p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}}\},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{1}=\{L_{ij}=\overline{p_{i}p_{j}}\},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{0}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{5}\}.$ The resolution $\pi=\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2}$ is constructed in two steps. • Step 1: $X_{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ is the blowup at the five points of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{0}$ . • Step 2: $X\xrightarrow{\sigma_{2}}X_{1}$ is the blowup at the $10$ disjoint projective lines $\widetilde{L}$ , where $\widetilde{L}$ are the proper transforms of the elements $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{1}$ . The preimage of the arrangement is a normal crossing divisor made of $25$ irreducible components $\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})=\underbrace{\left(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{2}}D_{H}\right)}_{10\text{ divisors }\cong\Bl_{4}\mathbb{P}^{2}}\bigcup\underbrace{\left(\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{1}}D_{L}\right)}_{10\text{ divisors }\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}\bigcup\underbrace{\left(\bigcup_{p\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{0}}D_{p}\right)}_{5\text{ divisors }\cong\Bl_{4}\mathbb{P}^{2}}.$ Remark 3.18. More generally, the braid or $A_{n+1}$ -arrangement in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the collection of $\binom{n+2}{2}$ hyperplanes spanned by $n$ out of $n+2$ points in general linear position. The arrangement complement $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ is naturally identified with the configuration space $\mathcal{M}_{0,n+3}$ of $n+3$ marked points in the Riemann sphere modulo Möbius transformations. It is well known that the minimal De Concini-Procesi model (3.2) agrees with the Deligne-Mumford-Knudsen compactification $\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n+3}$ , see [Kap93, Theorem 4.3.3]. Remark 3.19. The action of the symmetric group $S_{6}$ on $X=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,6}$ is explained in [Hun06, Chapter 3]. Continuing with the above notation, $X\xrightarrow{\sigma_{2}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}.$ Contracting the ten projective lines $\widetilde{L}\subset X_{1}$ , which have normal bundle $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus 2}$ , produces a $3$ -fold with ten ordinary double point singularities which embeds in $\mathbb{CP}^{5}$ as the Segre cubic $\Bigl\{\sum_{i=0}^{5}z_{i}=0,\hskip 5.69054pt\sum_{i=0}^{5}z_{i}^{3}=0\Bigr\}$ and $S_{6}$ acts by permuting the coordinates. 3.2 Picard group and cohomology of $X$ In this section we calculate the Picard group of the resolution $X$ of Section 3.1. We also calculate some cup products in the integer cohomology of $X$ that will be needed in the next sections. A set of generators and relations for the ring $H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ is given in [DCP95, §5]. We provide quick and direct proofs of the formulas that we need rather than algebraic manipulation of the relations there. If $X$ is a projective manifold, we write $\Pic(X)$ for the group of isomorphisms classes of holomorphic -or equivalently algebraic- line bundles on $X$ . Given a divisor $D\subset X$ , we denote by $\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)$ the line bundle on $X$ defined by the rank $1$ locally free sheaf of rational functions on $X$ with at most simple poles along $D$ . We write $[D]$ for the class of $\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)$ in $\Pic(X)$ . We will use the following standard result. Lemma 3.20. Let $X$ be a nonsingular variety and let $Y$ be a nonsingular subvariety of codimension $r\geq 2$ . Let $\pi:\widetilde{X}\to X$ be the blowup of $X$ along $Y$ . (i) If $Y^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(Y)$ is the exceptional divisor then $\Pic(\widetilde{X})=\pi^{*}\left(\Pic(X)\right)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cdot[Y^{\prime}].$ (3.4) (ii) If $D\subset X$ is a divisor with $Y\not\subset D$ and $\widetilde{D}\subset\widetilde{X}$ is the proper transform of $D$ then $\pi^{*}([D])=[\widetilde{D}].$ (3.5) Proof. (i) This is Exercise 8.5 of Chapter II in [Har13]. (ii) Let $s$ be a section of $\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)$ with $D=s^{-1}(0)$ . Since $Y\not\subset D$ , the zero set of the pullback section $\pi^{*}s$ is equal to $\widetilde{D}$ , providing a trivialization of $\pi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D))\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}(-\widetilde{D})$ . Taking isomorphism classes gives (3.5). ∎ In the rest of the section we let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an arrangement and let $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ as in Definition 3.1 . Recall that we write $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\setminus\mathcal{H}$ . Proposition 3.21. The Picard group of the resolution $X$ is the free abelian group generated by the classes $[D_{L}]$ for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ together with the class of $\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ . Proof. We begin by recalling the construction of $X$ by a sequence of blowups as in Proposition 3.7 . The upshot is that $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is equal to $X_{k}\xrightarrow{\pi_{k}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ where $X_{i}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ are constructed inductively as follows. Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be a labelling of the elements of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ such that if $L_{i}\subset L_{j}$ then $i\leq j$ . Start with $X_{0}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $\pi_{0}=$ the identity map. Then $\pi_{i}=\pi_{i-1}\circ\sigma_{i}$ for $1\leq i\leq k$ , where $X_{i}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{i}}X_{i-1}$ is the blowup of $X_{i-1}$ along $\widetilde{L}_{i}$ and $\widetilde{L}_{i}$ is the proper transform of $L_{i}$ by $\pi_{i-1}$ . By construction, each $X_{i}$ contains exactly $i$ irreducible $\pi_{i}$ -exceptional divisors $D^{i}_{j}$ with $\pi_{i}(D^{i}_{j})=L_{j}$ for $1\leq j\leq i$ . Moreover, if $j\leq i-1$ then $D^{i}_{j}=\widetilde{D}^{i-1}_{j}$ is the proper transform of $D^{i-1}_{j}$ by $\sigma_{i}$ while $D^{i}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{L}_{i})$ is the $\sigma_{i}$ -exceptional divisor. Claim. The Picard group of $X_{i}$ is a free abelian group of rank $i+1$ generated by the $\pi_{i}$ -exceptional divisors $[D^{i}_{j}]$ for $1\leq j\leq i$ together with the class of $\pi_{i}^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\right)$ . We prove the claim by induction on $i$ . The case $i=0$ follows from the fact that $\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ is generated by $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ . Suppose that $1\leq i\leq k$ and assume that the Picard group of $X_{i-1}$ is the free abelian group generated by the $\pi_{i-1}$ -exceptional divisors $[D^{i-1}_{j}]$ for $1\leq j\leq i-1$ together with the class of $\pi_{i-1}^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\right)$ . By Lemma 3.20 (i) the Picard group of $X_{i}$ is the free abelian group generated by $\sigma_{i}^{*}(\pi_{i-1}^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)))=\pi_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))$ together with $\sigma_{i}^{*}([D^{i-1}_{j}])$ for $1\leq j\leq i-1$ and $[\sigma_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{L}_{i})]=[D^{i}_{i}]$ . Furthermore, if $\widetilde{L}_{i}\subset D^{i-1}_{j}$ for some $1\leq j\leq i-1$ then $L_{i}=\pi_{i-1}(\widetilde{L}_{i})\subset\pi_{i-1}(D^{i-1}_{j})=L_{j}$ which contradicts the inclusion preserving property of the labels. Thus, if $1\leq j\leq i-1$ then $\widetilde{L}_{i}\not\subset D^{i-1}_{j}$ and by Lemma 3.20 (ii) $\sigma_{i}^{*}([D^{i-1}_{j}])=[\widetilde{D}^{i-1}_{j}]=[D^{i}_{j}]$ . This finishes the proof of the claim. Having proved the claim, the statement follows since the divisors $D^{k}_{j}$ for $1\leq j\leq k$ are precisely the divisors of the form $D_{L}$ for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ . ∎ For the classes $D_{H}$ with $H\in\mathcal{H}$ we have the following. Lemma 3.22. If $H\in\mathcal{H}$ then $[D_{H}]=[\pi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))]-\sum_{L|L\subsetneq H}[D_{L}]$ (3.6) where the sum runs over all $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ properly contained in $H$ . Proof. Let $s$ be a section of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ with $s^{-1}(0)=H$ . The zero set of $\pi^{*}s$ is equal to $\pi^{-1}(H)=\bigcup_{L|L\subset H}D_{L}$ so $\pi^{*}(s)$ gives a trivialization of the tensor product of $\pi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))$ with $\mathcal{O}_{X}\left(-\sum D_{L}\right)$ where the sum runs over all irreducible subspaces $L$ contained in $H$ (including $L=H$ ). Splitting the sum as $H$ plus the sum pf all irreducible subspaces which are properly contained in $H$ and taking classes in $\Pic(X)$ gives (3.6). ∎ Now we switch gears and discuss the integer cohomology of $X$ . Lemma 3.23. The first Chern class is an isomorphism between $\Pic(X)$ and $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ . Proof. Since $X$ is Kähler and simply connected, it follows that $b^{0,1}(X)=\dim H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0.$ On the other hand, if $\alpha$ is a holomorphic $2$ -form on $X$ then by Hartogs it defines a holomorphic $2$ -form on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , so $\alpha=0$ . It follows that $b^{2,0}(X)=0$ and, since $X$ is Kähler, we have $b^{0,2}(X)=\dim H^{2}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0.$ Consider the exponential sequence $0\to\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\exp}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}\to 0$ . The long exact sequence in cohomology gives us $0\to H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})\xrightarrow{c_{1}}H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})\to 0,$ showing that $c_{1}$ is an isomorphism between $\Pic(X)$ and $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ . ∎ Notation 3.24. For $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ we write $\gamma_{L}=c_{1}(D_{L}).$ (3.7) Equivalently, $\gamma_{L}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ is the Poincaré dual of the divisor $D_{L}\subset X$ . Definition 3.25. We write $h$ for the generator of $H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\mathbb{Z})$ given by the hyperplane class $h=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)).$ (3.8) So $\pi^{*}h$ is the Poincaré dual of the proper transform $\widetilde{Q}\subset X$ of a generic hyperplane $Q\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ that intersects transversely all the members of $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . Corollary 3.26. $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ is the free abelian group generated by the classes $\gamma_{L}$ with $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ together with $\pi^{*}h$ . Proof. This follows from Proposition 3.21 together with Lemma 3.23 . ∎ Corollary 3.27. For $H\in\mathcal{H}$ we have $\gamma_{H}=\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L|L\subsetneq H}\gamma_{L}$ (3.9) where the sum runs over all $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ properly contained in $H$ . Proof. This follows from Equation (3.6) by taking the first Chern class. ∎ If $\alpha\in H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ and $\beta\in H^{j}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ then $\alpha\cdot\beta\in H^{i+j}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ (3.10) is their cup product. In the rest of the section we calculate diverse cup products between the classes $\gamma_{L}$ and $\pi^{*}h$ . Remark 3.28. If $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are the Poincaré duals of submanifolds $A$ and $B$ with transverse intersection, then $\alpha\cdot\beta$ is the Poincaré dual of $A\cap B$ . In particular, if $A\cap B=\emptyset$ then $\alpha\cdot\beta=0$ . We need the following relation between the pullback of the Poincaré dual of a submanifold and the Poicaré dual of its proper transform. Lemma 3.29. Let $X$ be a nonsingular variety and let $Y$ be a nonsingular subvariety. Let $\pi:\widetilde{X}\to X$ be the blowup of $X$ along $Y$ . Suppose that $V\subset X$ is a nonsingular subvariety of codimension $r$ that is transversal to $Y$ . Let $\widetilde{V}\subset\widetilde{X}$ be the proper transform of $V$ . Then $\gamma_{\widetilde{V}}=\pi^{*}\gamma_{V}\,,$ (3.11) where $\gamma_{V}\in H^{2r}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ and $\gamma_{\widetilde{V}}\in H^{2r}(\widetilde{X},\mathbb{Z})$ are the Poincaré duals of $V$ and $\widetilde{V}$ The proof of Lemma 3.29 is standard and we omit it, see [Ful13, Corollary 6.7.2]. Note that, under the hypothesis of Lemma 3.29, the proper transform $\widetilde{V}$ is nonsingular and is equal to the blowup of $V$ along $Y\cap V$ . Let $P$ be a linear subspace $P\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of dimension $k$ . We say that $P$ is generic if it intersects transversely the elements of $\mathcal{L}$ . Concretely, if $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is such that $\codim L>k$ then $P\cap L=\emptyset$ , while if $\codim L\leq k$ then $P+L=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Clearly, the generic linear subspaces $P\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of dimension $k$ make an open dense subset of the Grassmannian of $k$ -planes in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Lemma 3.30. The class $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-k}$ is the Poincaré dual of the proper transform $\widetilde{P}$ of a generic $k$ -plane $P\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Proof. We use the description of $X$ by a sequence of blowups given in Proposition 3.7 . The generic assumption implies that the proper transform $\widetilde{P}_{i}\subset X_{i}$ under $X_{i}\xrightarrow{\pi_{i}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is transversal to $\widetilde{L}_{i+1}$ for all $i$ . Then the statement follows by repeated application of Lemma 3.29 . ∎ Lemma 3.31. Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be a set of not necessarily distinct irreducible subspaces and assume that the common intersection $L=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}$ has codimension $r$ . If $k<r$ then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-k}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{L_{i}}=0.$ (3.12) Proof. By Lemma 3.30, the class $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-k}$ is Poincaré dual of the proper transform $\widetilde{P}$ of a generic $k$ -plane $P\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . If $k<r$ then $P$ does not intersect $L$ . Therefore, $\widetilde{P}$ does not intersect $\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}D_{L_{i}}$ and the result follows from Remark 3.28 . ∎ Corollary 3.32. Let $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $\codim L=r$ . (i) If $r>1$ then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}=0$ . (ii) If $r>2$ then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}=0$ . Lemma 3.33. For every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ we have $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{H}=1.$ (3.13) Proof. It follows from Equation (3.9) together with Corollary 3.32 (i) that $\displaystyle(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{H}$ $\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\left(\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L|L\subsetneq H}\gamma_{L}\right)$ $\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}h)^{n}=1$ where the last equality holds because $h^{n}=1$ and $\pi$ has degree $1$ . ∎ Lemma 3.34. For every $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $\codim L=2$ we have $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}=-1.$ (3.14) Proof. Let $P$ be a generic $2$ -plane that intersects $L$ transversely at a point $p\in L^{\circ}$ . The class $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}$ is the Poincaré dual of the proper transform $\widetilde{P}\subset X$ of $P$ . In a neighbourhood of $p$ , we can identify the resolution $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with the blowup of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ along $L$ so that: • $D_{L}=\mathbb{P}(N_{L})$ is the exceptional divisor, where $\mathbb{P}(N_{L})$ is the projectivization of the normal bundle $N_{L}$ of $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ ; • the class $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ is the Poincaré dual of a curve $C=\pi^{-1}(p)$ which is a fibre of the bundle projection $\mathbb{P}(N_{L})\xrightarrow{\pi}L$ . Since $N_{D_{L}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(N_{L})}(-1)$ is the tautological bundle on $\mathbb{P}(N_{L})$ that restricts to $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)$ on each fibre, we have $C\cdot\gamma_{L}=\deg(N_{D_{L}}|_{C})=-1$ and Equation (3.14) follows. ∎ Lemma 3.35. If $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ and $H\in\mathcal{H}$ contains $L$ , then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{H}=1\,.$ (3.15) Proof. By Equation (3.9), the product $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{H}$ is equal to $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\left(\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L^{\prime}|L^{\prime}\subsetneq H}\gamma_{L^{\prime}}\right).$ (3.16) By Corollary 3.32 (i) the product $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ is zero. By Lemma 3.31, if $L^{\prime}\neq L$ then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{L^{\prime}}=0$ . It follows that (3.16) is equal to $-(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}$ and the result follows from Equation (3.14). ∎ Definition 3.36. For $H\in\mathcal{H}$ let $B_{H}$ be the number of irreducible subspaces of codimension $2$ contained in $H$ minus $1$ , i.e., $B_{H}=\left|\{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}\,|\,L\subset H\}\right|-1\,.$ (3.17) Lemma 3.37. For every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ we have $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{H}^{2}=-B_{H}\,.$ (3.18) Proof. By the above results, we have $\displaystyle(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{H}^{2}$ $\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\left(\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L|L\subsetneq H}\gamma_{L}\right)^{2}$ $\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\left((\pi^{*}h)^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset H\\ \codim L=2\end{subarray}}\gamma_{L}^{2}\right)$ $\displaystyle=1-\left|\{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}\,|\,L\subset H\}\right|\,.$ The first equality uses Equation (3.9) . The second equality gets rid of the parenthesis terms $\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{L^{\prime}}$ , $\pi^{*}h\cdot\gamma_{L}$ , and $\gamma_{L}^{2}$ if $\codim L\geq 3$ ; by using Equation (3.12) together with items (i) and (ii) of Corollary 3.32 respectively. Finally, the third equality follows from Equation (3.14). ∎ 3.3 Polarization Let $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ as in Definition 3.1 . Recall that we write $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\setminus\mathcal{H}$ for the subset of irreducible subspaces of codimension $\geq 2$ . Lemma 3.38. We can choose integers $b_{L}>0$ for each $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}$ such that $P_{k}=k\cdot\pi^{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\big)-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}b_{L}\cdot D_{L}$ (3.19) is an ample divisor on $X$ for all $k\gg 1$ . Proof. This is standard and can be proved inductively by considering a single blowup along a smooth subvariety, see item (ii) of Corollary 4.1.4 in [Laz17]. ∎ Notation 3.39. Fix positive integers $b_{L}$ such that $P_{k}$ as in Lemma 3.38 is an ample line bundle on $X$ for all $k\gg 1$ . We refer to $b_{L}$ as the polarization coefficients. The next bound on the volumes of exceptional divisors and proper transforms of hyperplanes will be useful later in the proof of the main result. Lemma 3.40. Let $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and let $\vol_{P_{k}}(D_{L})=c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ be the volume of $D_{L}$ with respect to $P_{k}$ . Then $\vol_{P_{k}}(D_{L})=\begin{cases}f(k^{n-2})&\textup{ if }\codim L\geq 2,\\ k^{n-1}+f(k^{n-2})&\textup{ if }\codim L=1,\end{cases}$ (3.20) where $f=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}C_{j}k^{j}$ is a polynomial in $k$ of degree at most $n-2$ . Moreover, there is $\Lambda>0$ that depends only on $\mathcal{H}$ and the polarization coefficients such that $|C_{j}|\leq\Lambda$ for all $j$ . Proof. Write $c_{1}(P_{k})=k\cdot\pi^{*}h-e$ with $e=\sum_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}b_{L^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma_{L^{\prime}}$ and use the binomial expansion to obtain $\vol_{P_{k}}(D_{L})=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}C_{j}\cdot k^{j}$ where $C_{j}=\binom{n-1}{j}\cdot(-e)^{n-1-j}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{j}\cdot\gamma_{L}.$ (3.21) By Corollary 3.32 (i) and Lemma 3.33, the leading coefficient $C_{n-1}=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ is $C_{n-1}=\begin{cases}0\,\,&\text{ if }\,\,\codim L\geq 2,\\ 1\,\,&\text{ if }\,\,L=H\in\mathcal{H}\end{cases}$ and (3.20) follows from this. It follows from (3.21) that we can bound $|C_{j}|$ in terms of $\max b_{L}$ and the maximum absolute value of the top products $(\pi^{*}h)^{j}\cdot\prod_{L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\gamma_{L^{\prime}}$ with $\mathcal{S}$ a set of not necessarily distinct $n-j$ irreducible subspaces $L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ , which only depends on $\mathcal{H}$ . ∎ Lemma 3.41. The following holds $c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\pi^{*}h=k^{n-1}+f(k^{n-2})$ where $f(k^{n-2})$ is as in Lemma 3.40 . Proof. The same proof of Lemma 3.40 applies by replacing $\gamma_{L}$ with $\pi^{*}h$ . ∎ Remark 3.42. An alternative differential-geometric proof of Lemma 3.40 can be done as follows.
