Let $\epsilon=1/k$ and take a Kähler metric $\omega_{\epsilon}$ in the cohomology class of $(1/k)P_{k}$ given by $\omega_{\epsilon}=\pi^{*}\omega_{\FS}+\epsilon\cdot\eta$ where $\omega_{\FS}$ is the Fubini-Study metric normalized so that $[\omega_{\FS}]\in c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))$ and $\eta$ is a fixed real and closed $(1,1)$ -form. The form $\eta$ depends only on the polarization coefficients and it is positive when restricted to the kernel of $\pi_{*}$ , it can be constructed as in the proof of Proposition 3.24 in [Voi07a]. If $\codim L\geq 2$ , then $\pi^{*}\omega_{\FS}$ is degenerate along $TD_{L}$ and we get that $\int_{D_{L}}\omega_{\epsilon}^{n-1}=O(\epsilon).$ Similarly, $\int_{D_{H}}\omega_{\epsilon}^{n-1}=1+O(\epsilon)$ for every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . 4 The parabolic bundle In Section 4.1 , we present background material from the theory of parabolic bundles. In Section 4.2 , we define a natural parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $(X,D)$ , where $X$ is the minimal De Concini-Procesi model of $\mathcal{H}$ and $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ . The underlying vector bundle is the pullback tangent bundle $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}(T\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ . The parabolic structure is defined by the filtrations of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}$ for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ given by the subbundles $\pi^{*}(TL)\subset\mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}$ together with weights $a_{L}$ . In Section 4.3 , we show that the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian. In Sections 4.4 and 4.5 , we calculate the first and second parabolic Chern characters of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ . Specifically, we show that $\parch_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0$ and give a formula for $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ in terms of the poset $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . 4.1 General theory Let $X$ be a complex manifold and let $D$ be a simple normal crossing divisor with irreducible decomposition $D=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}$ . Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a holomorphic vector bundle on $X$ . Definition 4.1. A parabolic structure $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $\mathcal{E}$ is given by a collection of locally free subsheaves $\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}$ indexed by $i\in I$ and $a\in(0,1]$ such that the following holds. (i) Support/Increasing: for $0<a^{\prime}<a\leq 1$ we have inclusions of $\mathcal{O}_{X}$ -modules $\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})\subset\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}\,,$ where $\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})$ is the subsheaf of holomorphic sections of $\mathcal{E}$ that vanish along $D_{i}$ . (ii) Semi-continuity: for any given $a\in(0,1)$ there is $\epsilon>0$ such that $\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a+\epsilon}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}$ . (iii) Filtration in the category of vector bundles on $D$ : the quotient sheaves $F^{i}_{a}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}|_{D_{i}}\,\,\big/\,\,\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})|_{D_{i}}$ (4.1) define an increasing filtration of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}$ by vector subbundles. In this case, we say that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . Remark 4.2. Since the sheaves $\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}$ and $\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})$ are locally free $\mathcal{O}_{X}$ -modules, the restrictions $\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}|_{D_{i}}$ and $\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})|_{D_{i}}$ are locally free $\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}$ -modules. The quotient (4.1) is a subsheaf of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}=\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}\,\,\big/\,\,\mathcal{E}(-D_{i})|_{D_{i}}.$ Item (iii) requires that the $F^{i}_{a}$ are locally free $\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}$ -modules and that for $a^{\prime}<a$ the inclusions of $\mathcal{O}_{D_{i}}$ -modules $F^{i}_{a^{\prime}}\subset F^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}$ are inclusions of vector subbundles. Remark 4.3. If we extend the index $a\in(0,1]$ to $a\in\mathbb{R}$ by requiring that $\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a-1}=\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}(-D_{i})$ and define $\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a_{i}}$ for $\mathbf{a}=(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}$ . Then the collection of sheaves $\mathcal{E}_{\mathbf{a}}$ form a parabolic bundle as defined in [IS06, §2.1]. If $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ satisfies items (i) and (ii) of Definition 4.1 then $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is a c-parabolic sheaf as defined in [Moc06, §3.1.1] where $\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{1}$ is the vector in $\mathbb{R}^{I}$ with all entries equal to $1$ . Item (iii) is taken from [Moc06, Definition 3.12]. Notation 4.4. Write $F^{i}_{<a}=\bigcup_{a^{\prime}<a}F^{i}_{a^{\prime}}\,.$ By item (iii) of Definition 4.1 , $F^{i}_{<a}$ is a vector subbundle of $F^{i}_{a}$ . Definition 4.5. For $i\in I$ and $a\in(0,1]$ , we let $\Gr^{i}_{a}=F^{i}_{a}\,\big/\,F^{i}_{<a}\,.$ (4.2) Thus, we have an exact sequence of vector bundles on $D_{i}$ $0\to F^{i}_{<a}\to F^{i}_{a}\to\Gr^{i}_{a}\to 0\,.$ The $\Gr^{i}_{a}$ are the graded components of the filtration $F^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}$ . Definition 4.6. For $i\in I$ , let $\wt(i)\subset(0,1]$ be the finite set of weights of the filtration $F^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}|_{D_{i}}$ given by $\wt(i)=\{a\,\,|\,\,\Gr^{i}_{a}\neq 0\}\,,$ (4.3) and let $\lambda_{i}=\sum_{a\in\wt(i)}a\cdot\rk(\Gr^{i}_{a})\,,$ (4.4) where $\rk(\Gr^{i}_{a})$ is the rank of the vector bundle $\Gr^{i}_{a}$ on $D_{i}$ . Definition 4.7 ([Moc06, §3.1.2]). The parabolic first Chern class of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is the element of $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})$ given by $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=c_{1}(\mathcal{E})-\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{i}\cdot c_{1}(D_{i})\,.$ (4.5) To define parabolic second Chern character, we must introduce an extra compatibility condition. Definition 4.8 ([Moc06, Definition 3.12]). Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ as in Definition 4.1 . We say that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian, if for every subset $J=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset I$ such that the intersection $D_{J}=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}$ is non-empty, the following holds. • (Compatibility condition.) There is a decomposition of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{J}}$ locally on $D_{J}$ as a direct sum of subbundles $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ indexed by $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{J}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}U_{\mathbf{a}}$ such that for all $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in(0,1]^{k}$ we have $\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}\left.F^{i_{j}}_{a_{j}}\right|_{D_{J}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ (4.6) where the sum is over all $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{k})$ such that $a^{\prime}_{j}\leq a_{j}$ for all $1\leq j\leq k$ . Remark 4.9. If $k=1$ and $D_{J}=D_{i}$ , then (4.6) is satisfied by taking complements, locally on $D_{i}$ , to the subbundle $F^{i}_{<a}\subset F^{i}_{a}$ ; so that $F^{i}_{a}=F^{i}_{<a}\oplus U_{a}$ with $U_{a}\cong\Gr^{i}_{a}$ . Remark 4.10. The term locally abelian is taken from [IS06, §2.1]. Next, we reformulate the locally abelian condition in terms of local frames spanning the filtrations $F^{i}_{a}$ . To do this, we introduce some notation. Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ and let $J=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset I$ be such that the intersection $D_{J}=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}$ is non-empty. For each $j\in J$ , we have a filtration of $\left.\mathcal{E}\right|_{D_{J}}$ given by the subbundles $\left.F^{j}_{a}\right|_{D_{J}}$ . Altogether, we have $k=|J|$ different filtrations on $D_{J}$ . Notation 4.11. For $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , we write $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ for the fibrewise intersection (see Remark 4.12 below) $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}\left.F^{i_{j}}_{a_{j}}\right|_{D_{J}}\,.$ If $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})$ and $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{k})$ are vectors in $\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , we write $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}$ if $a^{\prime}_{i}\leq a_{i}$ for all $i$ and $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{a}$ . Similarly, we write $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ for the fibrewise sum $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}:=F^{i_{1}}_{<a_{1}}\cap F^{i_{2}}_{a_{2}}\cap\ldots\cap F^{i_{k}}_{a_{k}}\,\,+\,\,\ldots\,\,+\,\,F^{i_{1}}_{a_{1}}\cap F^{i_{2}}_{a_{2}}\cap\ldots\cap F^{i_{k}}_{<a_{k}}\,,$ where we have omitted all the restrictions $|_{D_{J}}$ on the right hand side. Remark 4.12. Without compatibility conditions on $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ , the above $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ and $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ are merely subsets of the total space of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{J}}$ which intersect the fibres along linear subspaces; however the dimension of these subspaces might vary. By definition, we have an inclusion of sets $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\subset F_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Lemma 4.13. Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . Then $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian if and only if for every non-empty intersection $D_{J}=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}$ the following conditions hold. (i) For every point $p\in D_{J}$ the $k$ -tuple of filtrations $\{\mathcal{F}^{j}_{p}\,|\,j\in J\}$ of the fibre $\mathcal{E}|_{p}$ given by $\mathcal{F}^{j}_{p}=\{F^{j}_{a}|_{p}\,,\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}$ , are compatible as in Definition A.8 . (ii) For any $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , the intersection $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ is a vector bundle on $D_{J}$ ; and the sum $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ is a vector subbundle of $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ .33 3 For generic values of $\mathbf{a}$ , e.g. if $\mathbf{a}$ does not belong to the finite set $\prod_{j=1}^{k}\wt(i_{j})$ , the subbundle $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ is actually equal to $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Proof. Suppose that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian. We want to prove items (i) and (ii). Item (i) is immediate, since Definition 4.8 implies Definition A.8 by restricting to fibres. To show (ii), note that Equation (4.6) implies that $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\oplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ is a vector bundle. Similarly, $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ is a vector subbundle of $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ , proving (ii). Conversely, suppose that items (i) and (ii) hold. Fix $p\in D_{J}$ , by item (i) we can find a direct sum decomposition $\mathcal{E}|_{p}=\oplus_{\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}}|_{p}$ of $\mathcal{E}|_{p}$ such that $\left.F_{\mathbf{a}}\right|_{p}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}\left.F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\right|_{p}\right)\oplus\left.U_{\mathbf{a}}\right|_{p}\,.$ (4.7) By item (ii), we can locally extend $U_{\mathbf{a}}|_{p}$ to a vector subbundle $U_{\mathbf{a}}\subset F_{\mathbf{a}}$ such that Equation (4.7) holds for all points in a neighbourhood of $p$ . By construction, $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ for any $\mathbf{a}$ . On the other hand, since the subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}|_{p}$ form a direct sum at $p$ , they also form a direct sum near $p$ . This shows that $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\oplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ and therefore $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian. ∎ Definition 4.14. Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . Let $S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\}$ be a local frame of sections of $\mathcal{E}$ defined on an open set of $D_{J}=\cap_{j\in J}D_{j}$ . The frame $S$ is adapted to $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ , if for every $j\in J$ and $a\in\mathbb{R}$ , the subset $S^{j}_{a}\subset S$ given by $S^{j}_{a}=\{s\in S\,\,|\,\,s\textup{ is a section of }F^{j}_{a}\}$ is a frame of $F^{j}_{a}$ . The main characterization of the local abelian condition that we are after is given by the next. Lemma 4.15. Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . Then $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian if and only if for every non-empty intersection $D_{J}$ and every $p\in D_{J}$ , there is a frame of sections of $\mathcal{E}$ defined on a neighbourhood of $p$ in $D_{J}$ that is adapted to $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ . Proof. If $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian, then we can decompose $\mathcal{E}=\oplus_{\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}}$ locally on $D_{J}$ as in Definition 4.8 . Choose a frame $S_{\mathbf{a}}$ for each component $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ and let $S=\cup_{\mathbf{a}}S_{\mathbf{a}}$ . It is easy to see that $S$ is adapted to $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ . Conversely, suppose that such adapted frames exist. To show that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian, we verify items (i) and (ii) of Lemma 4.13 . Item (i) follows immediately from Lemma A.15 . To show item (ii), take a local trivialization of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{J}}$ given by an adapted frame. In such a trivialization, the intersections $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ and sums $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ are constant, hence they are vector bundles. This proves (ii) and finishes the proof of the lemma. ∎ Definition 4.16. Suppose that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is a locally abelian parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . For each pair $i,j\in I$ , let $D_{ij}=D_{i}\cap D_{j}$ . For $a,b\in(0,1]$ , let $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ be given by $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}=\left(\left.F^{i}_{a}\right|_{D_{ij}}\cap\left.F^{j}_{b}\right|_{D_{ij}}\right)\,\big/\,\left(\left.F^{i}_{<a}\right|_{D_{ij}}\cap\left.F^{j}_{b}\right|_{D_{ij}}+\left.F^{i}_{a}\right|_{D_{ij}}\cap\left.F^{j}_{<b}\right|_{D_{ij}}\right)\,.$ (4.8) By Lemma 4.13 (ii), $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ is a vector bundle on $D_{ij}$ . The quotient $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ is zero unless $a\in\wt(i)$ and $b\in\wt(j)$ . It is helpful to picture the $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ inside the unit square $(0,1]^{2}$ . The $F^{i}_{a}$ are represented by intersections of the square with left half planes $x\leq a$ for $a\in\wt(i)$ ; similarly the $F^{j}_{b}$ are intersections of the square with lower half planes $y\leq b$ for $b\in\wt(j)$ . The $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ correspond to the smallest sub-rectangles of the square obtained as intersections of half spaces, indexed by the coordinates $(a,b)$ of their upper right corners. Definition 4.17 ([Moc06, §3.1.5]). Suppose that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is a locally abelian parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . The parabolic second Chern character of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is the element of $H^{4}(X,\mathbb{R})$ given by $\displaystyle\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ $\displaystyle=\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})-\sum_{i}\sum_{a}a\cdot\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{i}_{a})\big)$ (4.9) $\displaystyle+\,\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{a}a^{2}\cdot\rk(\Gr^{i}_{a})\cdot c_{1}(D_{i})^{2}$ $\displaystyle+\,\,\sum_{i<j}\sum_{a,b}ab\cdot\rk(\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b})\cdot c_{1}(D_{i})\cdot c_{1}(D_{j})\,.$ The terms in Equation (4.9) have the following meaning: $\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})$ denotes the second Chern character of the vector bundle $\mathcal{E}$ given by $\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})=\frac{1}{2}\big(c_{1}(\mathcal{E})^{2}-2c_{2}(\mathcal{E})\big)\,;$ $\imath$ is the inclusion $D_{i}\subset X$ and $\imath_{*}$ is the associated Gysin map in cohomology $\imath_{*}:H^{2}(D_{i},\mathbb{Z})\to H^{4}(X,\mathbb{Z})\,.$ Finally, $\rk(\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b})$ denotes the rank of the vector bundle $\Gr^{i,j}_{a,b}$ . Stable bundles and Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality Next, we define (slope) stability for parabolic bundles. To do this, we must consider saturated subsheaves (see Definition A.51). We recall the way a parabolic bundle induces a parabolic structure on subsheaves. Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ as in Definition 4.1 and suppose that $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ is a saturated subsheaf. Definition 4.18. The induced parabolic structure $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is defined by the collection of subsheaves $\mathcal{V}^{i}_{a}\subset\mathcal{V}$ indexed by $i\in I$ and $a\in(0,1]$ given by $\mathcal{V}^{i}_{a}=\mathcal{V}\bigcap\mathcal{E}^{i}_{a}.$ Remark 4.19. Since $\mathcal{V}$ is saturated, by Corollary A.53 there is an analytic subset $Z\subset X$ with $\codim Z\geq 2$ such that $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ on $X\setminus Z$ . In particular, on the complements $D_{i}\setminus Z$ we have an increasing filtration of the vector bundle $\mathcal{V}|_{D_{i}}$ by vector subbundles $V^{i}_{a}=F^{i}_{a}\bigcap\mathcal{V}|_{D_{i}}$ . Let $\lambda^{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=\sum_{a\in(0,1]}a\cdot\rk\left(V^{i}_{a}\,/\,V^{i}_{<a}\right),$ (4.10) where $\rk\left(V^{i}_{a}\,/\,V^{i}_{<a}\right)$ is the rank of the quotient vector bundle on $D_{i}\setminus Z$ . Definition 4.20. The parabolic first Chern class of $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is the element $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{*})$ of $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})$ defined as $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{*})=c_{1}(\mathcal{V})-\sum_{i\in I}\lambda^{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\cdot c_{1}(D_{i})\,,$ where $c_{1}(\mathcal{V})$ is as in Definition A.56 . To define slope stability, we fix a polarization, given by an ample line bundle $P$ on $X$ . The parabolic degree of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is defined as $\pardeg_{P}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=c_{1}(P)^{n-1}\cdot\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\,,$ where $n$ is the dimension of $X$ . Similarly, the parabolic degree of the induced parabolic structure $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is defined as $\pardeg_{P}(\mathcal{V}_{*})=c_{1}(P)^{n-1}\cdot\parc_{1}(\mathcal{V}_{*})\,.$ Remark 4.21. If $\deg_{P}(\mathcal{V})=c_{1}(P)^{n-1}\cdot c_{1}(\mathcal{V})$ is the degree of $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ , then $\pardeg_{P}(\mathcal{V}_{*})=\deg_{P}(\mathcal{V})-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\mathcal{V}}\cdot\vol_{P}(D_{i})$ where $\vol_{P}(D_{i})=c_{1}(P)^{n-1}\cdot c_{1}(D_{i})>0$ is the volume of $D_{i}\subset X$ with respect to $P$ . Since $\lambda^{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\geq 0$ , $\pardeg_{P}(\mathcal{V}_{*})\leq\deg_{P}(\mathcal{V})\,.$ (4.11) If the induced parabolic structure is non-trivial, in the sense that $\lambda_{i}^{\mathcal{V}}$ are not all zero, then strict inequality holds in (4.11). A main notion we need is the next. Definition 4.22 ([Moc06, §3.1.3]). Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be a parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ with $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0$ and let $P$ be an ample line bundle on $X$ . We say that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is stable with respect to $P$ , or $P$ -stable for short, if for every non-zero and proper saturated subsheaf $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ we have $\pardeg_{P}(\mathcal{V}_{*})<0\,.$ (4.12) Remark 4.23. Our $P$ -stable parabolic bundles are referred as $\mu_{L}$ -stable in [Moc06]. The main result we need is the next version of the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality. Theorem 4.24 ([Moc06, Theorem 6.5]). Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be locally abelian parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ . Suppose that $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0$ and that $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is $P$ -stable. Then the following inequality holds: $c_{1}(P)^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\leq 0\,.$ (4.13) Remark 4.25. Mochizuki proves Theorem 4.24 in the more general case where $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is only locally abelian in codimension $2$ , meaning that the compatibility condition in Definition 4.8 holds outside a set of codimension $\geq 3$ . 4.2 Weights and filtration Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted arrangement as in Theorem 1.1 . Let $X\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the resolution of $\mathcal{H}$ and let $\mathcal{E}$ be the pullback tangent bundle $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}(T\mathbb{P}^{n})\,.$ (4.14) We use the weights $a_{H}$ to define a natural parabolic structure on $\mathcal{E}$ whose filtered subsheaves are indexed by the irreducible components of the simple normal crossing divisor $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ . Definition 4.26. The weight $a_{L}$ at an irreducible subspace $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ is $a_{L}=(\codim L)^{-1}\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}.$ (4.15) Note that $0<a_{L}<1$ because of the klt condition (1.1). Recall that the irreducible components of $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ are of the form $D_{L}$ , where $\pi(D_{L})=L$ and $L$ is a non-empty proper irreducible subspace. For $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ let $\mathcal{E}^{L}$ be the subsheaf of $\mathcal{E}$ generated by sections tangent to $\pi^{*}TL$ when restricted to $D_{L}$ .44 4 Alternatively, $\mathcal{E}^{L}$ is the pullback of the subsheaf of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ generated by vector fields tangent to $L$ . Set $\mathcal{E}^{L}_{a}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{E}^{L}&\mbox{ for }0<a<a_{L}\,,\\ \mathcal{E}&\mbox{ for }a_{L}\leq a\leq 1\,.\end{cases}$ (4.16) Lemma 4.27. The collection of subsheaves $\mathcal{E}^{L}_{a}\subset\mathcal{E}$ given by Equation (4.16) define a parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ as in Definition 4.1 . Proof. First of all, the sheaves $\mathcal{E}^{L}$ are locally free, for if $p\in D_{L}$ then we can pick a frame of sections $s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}$ of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ in a neighbourhood of $\pi(p)$ of which the first $d=\dim L$ generate $TL$ . Then $\mathcal{E}^{L}$ is freely generated near $p$ by the sections $\pi^{*}s_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}s_{d},\,\,z\cdot\pi^{*}s_{d+1},\ldots z\cdot\pi^{*}s_{n}\,$ where $z$ is a local defining equation of $D_{L}=\{z=0\}$ . Alternatively, the sections $s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}$ give a splitting $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}=V\oplus W$ in a neighbourhood of $\pi(p)$ where $V|_{L}=TL$ and $W|_{L}$ is isomorphic to the normal bundle of $L$ . Then $\mathcal{E}^{L}$ is locally isomorphic to the direct sum of $\pi^{*}V$ and $\pi^{*}W(-D_{L})$ . Let us now verify that $\mathcal{E}^{L}_{a}$ satisfy properties (i), (ii), and (iii) in Definition 4.1 . Items (i) support and (ii) semi-continuity are immediate from Equation (4.16). Finally, item (iii) follows from $\mathcal{E}^{L}|_{D_{L}}\,\big/\,\mathcal{E}(-D_{L})|_{D_{L}}=\pi^{*}(TL),$ which implies that the quotient $\mathcal{O}_{D_{L}}$ -modules $F^{L}_{a}=\mathcal{E}_{a}^{L}|_{D_{L}}\,\big/\,\mathcal{E}(-D_{L})|_{D_{L}}$ are given by $F^{L}_{a}=\begin{cases}\pi^{*}(TL)&\text{ if }a<a_{L},\\ \mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}&\text{ if }a\geq a_{L}.\end{cases}$ (4.17) This shows that $F^{L}_{a}$ is an increasing filtration of $\mathcal{E}|_{D_{L}}$ by vector subbundles. ∎ Definition 4.28. The parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is defined by the increasing filtrations $\mathcal{E}^{L}_{a}$ with $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and $a\in(0,1]$ given by Equation (4.16). 4.3 Locally abelian property Theorem 4.29. The parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $(X,D)$ is locally abelian. Proof. We use the characterization of the locally abelian property in terms of adapted frames given by Lemma 4.15 . Outside $D=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{H})$ there is nothing to prove. Take $\bar{p}\in D$ and let $\mathcal{S}$ be the set of all irreducible subspaces $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ such that $\bar{p}\in D_{L}$ . Let $D_{\mathcal{S}}$ be their common intersection $D_{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}D_{L}$ , so that $\bar{p}\in D_{\mathcal{S}}$ and $\bar{p}\notin D_{L^{\prime}}$ for $L^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{S}$ . Since the intersection $D_{\mathcal{S}}$ is non-empty, by Proposition 3.16 , the set $\mathcal{S}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . By Lemma 3.15 , $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set of projective subspaces as in Definition A.32. Let $M=\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}L$ be the projective subspace of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as the common intersection of the members of $\mathcal{S}$ . Let $p=\pi(\bar{p})\in M$ . By Lemma A.36 (ii), there is a frame of vector fields $e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}$ of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ defined on a neighbourhood of $p\in U\subset M$ such that for every $q\in U$ and $L\in\mathcal{S}$ , the vectors $e_{i}(q)$ that belong to $T_{q}L$ form a basis of $T_{q}L$ . The pullbacks $\pi^{*}e_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}e_{n}$ form a frame of $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}(T\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ on $\pi^{-1}(U)$ . Since $\pi(D_{\mathcal{S}})\subset M$ , the preimage $\pi^{-1}(U)$ contains an open neighbourhood $\bar{U}\subset D_{\mathcal{S}}$ of $\bar{p}$ . By construction, the frame $\pi^{*}e_{1},\ldots,\pi^{*}e_{n}$ is adapted -as per Definition 4.14- to the parabolic structure $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ . This finishes the proof of the theorem. ∎ 4.4 The parabolic first Chern class In this section we show that $\parch_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0$ . We begin with a preliminary formula. Lemma 4.30. The parabolic first Chern class of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is given by $\parc_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=c_{1}(\mathcal{E})-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}\,.$ (4.18) Proof. By Equation (4.17), the graded terms $\Gr^{L}_{a}=F^{L}_{a}\big/F^{L}_{<a}$ are $\Gr^{L}_{a}=\begin{cases}\pi^{*}\left(T\mathbb{CP}^{n}|_{L}\big/TL\right)&\text{ if }a=a_{L},\\ 0&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}$ Therefore $\rk(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})=\codim L$ and Equation (4.18) follows from the formula for $\parch_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ given in Definition 4.7 . ∎ Lemma 4.31. In $H^{2}(X,\mathbb{R})$ the following identity holds: $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}=s\cdot\pi^{*}h\,,$ (4.19) where $s=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}$ is the sum of all hyperplane weights. Proof. We split the left hand side of Equation (4.19) as a sum over hyperplanes and irreducible subspaces of codimension $\geq 2$ , $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\cdot\gamma_{H}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}\,.$ (4.20) On the other hand, Equation (3.9) implies that $\displaystyle\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\cdot\gamma_{H}$ $\displaystyle=s\cdot\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}\left(\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\right)\cdot\gamma_{L}$ (4.21) $\displaystyle=s\cdot\pi^{*}h-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}\,,$ where the second equality follows from the definition of $a_{L}$ given by Equation (4.15). Equation (4.19) follows from Equations (4.20) and (4.21). ∎ Lemma 4.32. The parabolic first Chern class of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is zero $\parch_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=0.$ (4.22) Proof. Since $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}T\mathbb{P}^{n}$ , we have $c_{1}(\mathcal{E})=(n+1)\cdot\pi^{*}h.$ It follows from Equations (4.18) and (4.19) that $\parch_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=\big(n+1-s\big)\cdot\pi^{*}h.$ The lemma follows by using the CY condition (1.2) which requires $s=n+1$ . ∎ 4.5 The parabolic second Chern character By Equation (4.9), the parabolic second Chern character of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is given by $\begin{gathered}\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})\big)\\ +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(L,M)}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\nu_{L,M}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,.\end{gathered}$ (4.23) The last sum runs over all ordered pairs $(L,M)$ of (not necessarily distinct) elements in $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $D_{L}\cap D_{M}\neq\emptyset$ , and $\nu_{L,M}=\begin{cases}\rk\big(\Gr^{L,M}_{a_{L},a_{M}}\big)&\textup{ if }L\neq M\,,\\ \rk\big(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}}\big)=\codim L&\textup{ if }L=M\,,\end{cases}$ where $\Gr^{L,M}_{a_{L},a_{M}}$ is given by formula (4.8). Lemma 4.33. For every $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ the following identity holds: $\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})\big)=\codim L\cdot\pi^{*}h\cdot\gamma_{L}\,.$ (4.24) Proof. On $D_{L}$ we have $\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}}=\pi^{*}\big(T\mathbb{CP}^{n}|_{L}\big/TL\big)=\pi^{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)|_{L}^{\oplus r}\big)\,,$ where $r=\codim L$ . This implies that $c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})=\imath^{*}\alpha\,,$ where $\alpha=r\cdot\pi^{*}h\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})$ and $\imath^{*}$ is the pullback by the inclusion map of $D_{L}\subset X$ . The projection formula $\imath_{*}\left(\imath^{*}\alpha\right)=\alpha\cdot\gamma_{L}$ -see Equation 1.6 in [Voi07b]- implies that $\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})\big)=\imath_{*}\big(\imath^{*}\alpha\big)=\alpha\cdot\gamma_{L}=r\cdot\pi^{*}h\cdot\gamma_{L}\,.$ This finishes the proof. ∎ Lemma 4.34. Let $L$ and $M$ be irreducible subspaces with $D_{L}\cap D_{M}\neq\emptyset$ . Then $\nu_{L,M}=\codim(L+M)\,.$ (4.25) Proof. Since $D_{L}\cap D_{M}\neq\emptyset$ , the subspace $N=L\cap M$ is non-empty and $\pi(D_{L}\cap D_{M})\subset N.$ Since $F^{L}_{<a_{L}}=\pi^{*}TL$ , $F^{L}_{a_{L}}=\pi^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , and similarly for $M$ ; on $D_{L}\cap D_{M}$ the vector bundle $\Gr^{L,M}_{a_{L},a_{M}}$ (given by formula (4.8)) is equal to $\Gr^{L,M}_{a_{L},a_{M}}=\pi^{*}\left(T\mathbb{P}^{n}\big/\big(TL|_{N}+TM|_{N}\big)\right)$ and the result follows. ∎ Split the terms that define $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ in Equation (4.23) as $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=\textup{A}+\textup{B}$ (4.26) with $\textup{A}=\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})\big)$ and $\textup{B}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{(L,M)}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\nu_{L,M}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,.$ Lemma 4.35. The term A in $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ is equal to $\textup{A}=-\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}.$ (4.27) Proof. By definition, the second Chern class of the vector bundle $\mathcal{E}$ is $\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})=\frac{1}{2}\big(c_{1}(\mathcal{E})^{2}-2c_{2}(\mathcal{E})\big).$ Euler’s exact sequence implies that $c_{k}(T\mathbb{CP}^{n})=\binom{n+1}{k}\cdot h^{k}$ and we get that the second Chern character of $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}\big(T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\big)$ is $\ch_{2}(\mathcal{E})=\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}\,.$ (4.28) On the other hand, it follows from Equations (4.24) and (4.19) that $\displaystyle\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\imath_{*}\big(c_{1}(\Gr^{L}_{a_{L}})\big)$ $\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}\right)\cdot\pi^{*}h$ (4.29) $\displaystyle=s\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}.$ Taking the difference of Equations (4.28) and (4.29) gives us $\textup{A}=\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-s\right)\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}.$ The result follows from the CY condition (1.2) which requires $s=n+1$ . ∎ Lemma 4.36. The term B in $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ is equal to $\textup{B}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}\,+\,\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,,$ (4.30) where the second sum runs over all pairs of irreducible subspaces with $L\subsetneq M$ . Proof. Clearly, $\textup{B}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\nu_{L}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}\,\,+\,\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\neq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\nu_{L,M}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,,$ (4.31) where the second sum runs over all pairs $(L,M)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $L\neq M$ and $D_{L}\cap D_{M}\neq\emptyset$ . By Proposition 3.16 , the set $\mathcal{S}=\{L,M\}$ is nested relative to $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ , so one of the following must happen: (i) one subspace is contained in the other; (ii) the intersection $L\cap M$ is non-empty and reducible. If (ii) occurs then, by Lemma 2.21 , $L+M=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $\nu_{L,M}=\codim(L+M)=0$ . Therefore, we can assume that for all pairs $(L,M)$ occurring in the second sum in Equation (4.31) we have either $L\subset M$ or $M\subset L$ . Since every pair of irreducible subspaces $L,M$ with one contained in the other contributes twice to the sum as $(L,M)$ and $(M,L)$ ; and since $\nu_{L,M}=\codim(L+M)$ , we have $\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\neq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\nu_{L,M}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}=\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,.$ (4.32) Finally, Equation (4.30) follows from Equations (4.31) and (4.32). ∎ Lemmas 4.35 and 4.36 together give us the next. Corollary 4.37. The parabolic second Chern character of $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is given by $\begin{gathered}\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=-\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}\\ +\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}+\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,,\end{gathered}$ (4.33) where the last sum is over all pairs $(L,M)\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ with $L\subsetneq M$ . 5 Stability of the parabolic bundle In this section, we show that the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ that we defined in Section 4.2 is slope stable. To state a precise result, fix polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ , as in Lemma 3.38 , so that $P_{k}$ is an ample line bundle on $X$ for all $k\gg 1$ . In this section, we prove the following stability theorem. Theorem 5.1. The parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is $P_{k}$ -stable for all $k\gg 1$ . More precisely, there is $k_{0}$ , depending only on $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ and the polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ , such that for every non-zero and proper saturated subsheaf $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ , we have $\forall k>k_{0}:\,\,\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})<0\,,$ (5.1) where $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is the parabolic structure on $\mathcal{V}$ induced from $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ . In Section 5.1 , we establish some auxiliary results. In Section 5.2 , we prove the key estimate needed to show stability. This boils down to analysing the tangencies between $\mathcal{H}$ and distributions $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $c_{1}(\mathcal{V})\geq 0$ , as described in Proposition 5.10 . In Section 5.3 , we prove the stability Theorem 5.1 . In Section 5.4 , we prove our main Theorem 1.1 . We derive Theorem 1.1 as a consequence of Theorem 5.1 together with Mochizuki’s version of the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality for parabolic bundles [Moc06, Theorem 6.5]. 5.1 Auxiliary results The next result is an analogue of [Pan09, Lemma 7.7]. Lemma 5.2. Let $M\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a non-empty linear subspace, then $\sum_{H|M\not\subset H}a_{H}>\dim M+1.$ (5.2) Proof. The CY and klt conditions together give us $\displaystyle\sum_{H|M\not\subset H}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle=n+1-\sum_{H|M\subset H}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle>n+1-\codim M=\dim M+1\,,$ where we used Corollary 2.26 (iii) in the inequality step. ∎ Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a saturated subsheaf of $\mathcal{E}$ . By Corollary 3.26 we can write $c_{1}(\mathcal{V})=\imath\cdot\pi^{*}h+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}d_{L}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ (5.3) for unique integers $\imath$ and $d_{L}$ . The next result is an analogue of [Pan09, Lemma 7.6]. Lemma 5.3. Let $\imath$ and $d_{L}$ be as in Equation (5.3). Then $\imath\leq r\,,$ (5.4) where $r$ is the rank of $\mathcal{V}$ , and $\forall L:\,\,d_{L}\leq n-\imath\,.$ (5.5) Proof. To show that $\imath\leq r$ , to take a generic line $Q$ in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ which does not intersect any of the irreducible subspaces of the arrangement of codimension $\geq 2$ and such that $\mathcal{V}|_{Q}$ is a vector subbundle of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}|_{Q}$ which does not contain $TQ$ . The same argument used to prove Lemma A.58 in the Appendix shows that Equation (5.4) holds. To establish the bound on the coefficients $d_{L}$ , we argue similarly. Given $L$ , take a projective line $Q\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ such that: (i) $Q$ meets $L$ at single point; (ii) $Q\cap M=\emptyset$ for all $M\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\{L\}$ with $\codim M\geq 2$ ; (iii) $\mathcal{V}|_{\widetilde{Q}}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}|_{\widetilde{Q}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)^{\oplus(n-1)}\,,$ where $\widetilde{Q}$ is the proper transform of $Q$ . It is clear that we can take a line $Q$ with the above three properties. Item (iii) implies that $\deg(\mathcal{V}|_{\widetilde{Q}_{L}})\leq n.$ (5.6) On the other hand, items (i) and (ii) together with Equation (5.3) give us $\deg(\mathcal{V}|_{\widetilde{Q}_{L}})=\imath+d_{L}\,.$ (5.7) Equations (5.6) and (5.7) $\imath+d_{L}\leq n$ which is equivalent to Equation (5.5). ∎ Next, we discuss tangencies between hyperplanes and distributions. Definition 5.4. Let $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a distribution and let $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a hyperplane. We say that $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to $H$ (or that $H$ is tangent to $\mathcal{V}$ ), if for every point $x\in H\cap U$ -where $U$ is the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ - we have $\mathcal{V}_{x}\subset T_{x}H\,,$ where $\mathcal{V}_{x}$ denotes the fibre at $x$ of the vector subbundle $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ on $U$ . We denote by $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ the collection of all hyperplanes tangent to $\mathcal{V}$ , $\Tan(\mathcal{V})=\{H\,\,|\,\,H\text{ is tangent to }\mathcal{V}\}\,.$ We analyse the set $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ for distributions with non-negative index (see Definition A.57). Our next result is an analogue of [Pan09, Lemma 7.4]. The proof relies on results from Appendix A.4 . Proposition 5.5. Let $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a distribution of index $\imath\geq 0$ . (i) If $\imath>0$ then there is a linear subspace $M\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $\dim M\geq\imath-1$ (5.8) such that any hyperplane $H\in\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ contains $M$ . (ii) Suppose that $\imath=0$ and that $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to the $n+1$ coordinate hyperplanes. Then there is a non-zero holomorphic vector field $Y$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ that is tangent to all the hyperplanes in $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ . Proof. Let $r$ be the rank of $\mathcal{V}$ and let $d=r-\imath$ be its degree. By Corollary A.67 , the distribution $\mathcal{V}$ defines an $(r+1)$ -vector field $\mathbf{v}$ on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with homogeneous polynomial coefficients of degree $d+1$ . By Lemma A.76 , a hyperplane $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ belongs to $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ if and only if the corresponding linear hyperplane $H^{\mathbf{c}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ belongs to $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ , the set of linear hyperplanes in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ that are tangent to $\mathbf{v}$ (Definitions A.70 and A.74). (i) By Proposition A.75 , there is a linear subspace $M^{\mathbf{c}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with $\dim M^{\mathbf{c}}\geq\imath$ that is contained in all hyperplanes in $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ . Item (i) follows by taking $M=\mathbb{P}(M^{\mathbf{c}})$ . (ii) Since $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to the coordinate hyperplanes, the multivector field $\mathbf{v}$ belongs to the subspace $T_{r+1}^{\circ}$ from Definition A.77 . By Proposition A.82 , there is a linear vector field $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}$ on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ that is tangent to all the hyperplanes in $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ and such that $e\wedge\mathbf{v}^{\prime}$ is non-zero. We let $Y$ be the projection of $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}$ down to $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Then $Y$ is tangent to all elements in $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ and, since $e\wedge\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\neq 0$ , the vector field $Y$ is non-zero. ∎ Example 5.6. Let $M\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a linear subspace with $\dim M=r-1$ for some $1\leq r\leq n-1$ . The collection of all $r$ -dimensional subspaces that contain $M$ defines a distribution $\mathcal{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{\oplus r}$ of index $\imath=r$ . A hyperplane $H$ belongs to $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ if and only if $H\supset M$ . In particular, $\bigcap_{H\in\Tan(\mathcal{V})}H=M\,.$ This example shows that the inequality (5.8) is sharp. Remark 5.7. The proof of item (ii) of Proposition A.82 shows that the vector field $Y$ is tangent to $\mathcal{V}$ , meaning that $Y(x)\in\mathcal{V}_{x}$ for all points $x$ in the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ . The next example shows that, in general, distributions of index zero do not necessarily admit non-zero tangent vector fields. Example 5.8. Let $f$ and $h$ be homogeneous polynomials in $\mathbb{C}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{3}]$ with $\deg h=1$ and $f$ generic with $\deg f=3$ . The $1$ -form on $\mathbb{C}^{4}$ given by $\omega=3fdh-hdf$ has homogeneous polynomial coefficients of degree $3$ and the contraction of $\omega$ with the Euler vector field on $\mathbb{C}^{4}$ is identically zero. The $1$ -form $\omega$ defines a codimension $1$ distribution $\mathcal{V}$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ of rank $r=2$ and degree $d=2$ (see Remark A.69). The index of $\mathcal{V}$ is equal to $\imath=r-d=0$ . The distribution $\mathcal{V}$ is integrable. The leaves of the foliation defined by $\mathcal{V}$ make a pencil of cubic surfaces in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ given by $\{\lambda_{1}\cdot f+\lambda_{2}\cdot h^{3}=0\}$ with $[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ . A holomorphic vector field $Y$ tangent to $\mathcal{V}$ must also be tangent to the smooth cubic surface $\{f=0\}$ and therefore it must vanish. 5.2 Key estimate Let $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a distribution and let $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ be the set of all hyperplanes tangent to $\mathcal{V}$ . Definition 5.9. We say that a hyperplane $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is transverse to $\mathcal{V}$ if $H\notin\Tan(\mathcal{V})\,.$ (5.9) We write this condition as $H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}$ . The next result lies at the core of the proof of Theorem 5.1 . Proposition 5.10. Suppose that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is a weighted arrangement that is klt and CY. Then there is $\delta>0$ such that, for any distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with non-negative index $\imath\geq 0$ , we have $\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\geq\imath+\delta\,,$ (5.10) where the sum is over all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that are transverse to $\mathcal{V}$ . Proof. Take $\delta=\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}$ (5.11) with $\delta_{1}=\min\limits_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\delta_{2}=\min_{L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}}\left(\codim L-\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\right)\,,$ where the minimum in the definition of $\delta_{2}$ is taken over all non-empty linear subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $\codim L\geq 2$ . The klt condition implies that $\delta>0$ . We prove that Equation (5.10) holds with the above $\delta$ . We analyse the cases $\imath=0$ and $\imath>0$ separately. • Case $\imath=0$ . We want to show that there is at least one hyperplane $H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}$ such that $\mathcal{V}$ is not tangent to $H^{\prime}$ . We proceed by contradiction and suppose that $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to every hyperplane in $\mathcal{H}$ . Then, since $\mathcal{H}$ contains $n+1$ linearly independent hyperplanes, by Proposition 5.5 there is a non-zero holomorphic vector field $Y$ that is tangent to all the members of $\mathcal{H}$ , but this contradicts Corollary 2.28 . We conclude that there is $H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}$ such that $\mathcal{V}$ is not tangent to $H^{\prime}$ ; therefore $\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\geq a_{H^{\prime}}\geq\delta_{1}=\imath+\delta_{1}.$ (5.12) • Case $\imath>0$ . By Proposition 5.5 there is a subspace $M\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $\dim M\geq\imath-1$ such that if $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to $H$ then $H\supset M$ . In particular, if $M\not\subset H$ then $H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}$ and hence $\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\geq\sum_{H|M\not\subset H}a_{H}.$ Using the CY condition, we have $\displaystyle\sum_{H|M\not\subset H}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle=n+1-\sum_{H|M\subset H}a_{H}$ $\displaystyle=n+1-\codim M+\left(\codim M-\sum_{H|M\subset H}a_{H}\right)$ $\displaystyle\geq\imath+\delta_{2}\,,$ where the last inequality uses the bound $\dim M\geq\imath-1$ and the definition of $\delta_{2}$ . We conclude that $\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\geq\imath+\delta_{2}.$ (5.13) The statement follows from Equations (5.11), (5.12), and (5.13). ∎ Remark 5.11. The number $\delta>0$ in Proposition 5.10 depends only on the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ -as given by Equation (5.11)- and not on the distribution $\mathcal{V}$ . 5.3 Proof of Theorem 5.1 Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a saturated subsheaf of $\mathcal{E}$ . We want to show that there is $k_{0}$ , independent of $\mathcal{V}$ , such that $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})<0$ for all $k\geq k_{0}$ . Let $\imath$ be ‘the index’ of $\mathcal{V}$ , as defined by Equation (5.3). Notation 5.12. We denote by $O(k^{n-2})$ a polynomial in $k$ of degree at most $n-2$ , say $\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}C_{j}k^{j}$ , such that there is a positive number $K=K(\mathcal{H},b_{L})$ , depending only on $\mathcal{H}$ and the polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ , such that $|C_{j}|\leq K$ for all $j$ . Lemma 5.13. Suppose that $\imath<0$ . Then there is $k_{0}$ , that depends only on the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ and the polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ , such that $\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})<0$ for all $k>k_{0}$ . Proof. By Equation (5.3), we have $\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})=\imath\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\pi^{*}h+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}d_{L}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}\,.$ By Lemmas 3.40 and 3.41, we have $c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}=f_{L}$ with $f_{L}=O(k^{n-2})$ and $c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\pi^{*}h=k^{n-1}+f_{0}$ with $f_{0}=O(k^{n-2})$ . Together with the bound $d_{L}\leq n-\imath$ from Lemma 5.3, we obtain $\displaystyle\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})$ $\displaystyle=\imath\cdot k^{n-1}+\imath\cdot f_{0}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}d_{L}\cdot f_{L}$ $\displaystyle\leq\imath\cdot\left(k^{n-1}+f_{0}-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}f_{L}\right)+n\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}f_{L}$ Dividing by $k^{n-1}$ , we get $\frac{1}{k^{n-1}}\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})\leq\,\,\imath\cdot\textup{A}\,\,+\,\,\textup{B}$ with $\textup{A}=1+O(\epsilon)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\textup{B}=O(\epsilon),$ where $O(\epsilon)$ denotes a polynomial in the variable $\epsilon=1/k$ with zero constant term whose coefficients are uniformly bounded in absolute value in terms of the arrangement and the fixed integers $b_{L}$ . Thus, if we choose $k_{0}$ sufficiently big so that, say $\textup{A}>1/2$ and $\textup{B}<1/3$ for all $k\geq k_{0}$ , then $\frac{1}{k^{n-1}}\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})\leq\imath\cdot\textup{A}+\textup{B}<-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{3}<0$ for all $\imath\leq-1$ . ∎ The saturated subsheaf $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ induces in a natural way a distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Concretely, $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}=\pi^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ outside a codimension $2$ analytic subset $Z\subset X$ . The analytic subset $W\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ given by $W=\pi(Z)\cup\left(\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}L\right)$ has codimension $\geq 2$ and $\pi$ restricts to a biholomorphism between $\pi^{-1}(U)$ and $U$ , where $U=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus W$ . On the open set $U$ , the push-forward sheaf $\pi_{*}\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , thus defining a distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . By slight abuse of notation, we shall also write $\mathcal{V}$ for the distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Lemma 5.14. If $\imath\geq 0$ , then $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})\leq\left(\imath-\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\right)\cdot k^{n-1}+O(k^{n-2})\,.$ (5.14) Proof. The induced parabolic bundle $\mathcal{V}_{*}$ is given by the filtration $\mathcal{V}^{L}_{a}=\mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{E}^{L}_{a}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}^{L}\,&\text{ for }\,0<a<a_{L},\\ \mathcal{V}\,&\text{ for }a_{L}\leq a\leq 1;\end{cases}$ where $L$ ranges over all elements in $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ and $\mathcal{V}^{L}$ is the sheaf of sections of $\mathcal{V}$ which are tangent to $\pi^{*}(TL)$ when restricted to $D_{L}$ . In particular, if on the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ (c.f. Remark 4.19), the restriction of $\mathcal{V}$ to $D_{L}$ is contained in $\pi^{*}(TL)$ , then the filtration $\mathcal{V}^{L}_{a}$ is trivial, in the sense that $\mathcal{V}^{L}_{a}=\mathcal{V}$ for all $0<a\leq 1$ . If $L=H$ is a hyperplane, then the quotient $\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{H}$ is non-zero if and only if $\mathcal{V}|_{D_{H}}\not\subset\pi^{*}(TH)\,.$ This is equivalent to $H$ being transverse to the distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ as in Definition 5.9. It follows that $\begin{gathered}\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})=\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})\,\,-\\ \left(\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{H}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}r_{L}a_{L}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}\right)\,,\end{gathered}$ (5.15) where $r_{L}$ is the rank (possibly zero) of the quotient sheaf on $D_{L}$ given by $\mathcal{V}/\mathcal{V}^{L}$ . Same as in the proof of Lemma 5.13 , $\displaystyle\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})$ $\displaystyle=\imath\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\pi^{*}h+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}d_{L}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ $\displaystyle\leq\imath\cdot k^{n-1}+\imath\cdot f_{0}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}(n-\imath)\cdot f_{L}$ where $f_{0}=O(k^{n-2})$ and $f_{L}=O(k^{n-2})$ . Since $0\leq\imath\leq n$ , we get $\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})\leq\imath\cdot k^{n-1}+O(k^{n-2}).$ (5.16) On the other hand, since the weights $a_{H},a_{L}\in(0,1)$ and $0\leq r_{L}\leq n$ , $\begin{gathered}\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{H}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}r_{L}a_{L}\cdot c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-1}\cdot\gamma_{L}\\ =\left(\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}\right)\cdot k^{n-1}+O(k^{n-2}).\end{gathered}$ (5.17) The lemma follows from Equations (5.15), (5.16), and (5.17). ∎ Lemma 5.15. Let $p(k)$ be a polynomial with real coefficients $p(k)=c_{n-1}k^{n-1}+\ldots+c_{1}k+c_{0}.$ Suppose that $c_{n-1}=-\delta$ with $\delta>0$ and that $c_{n-2},\ldots,c_{0}\leq C$ for some $C>0$ . Then $p(k)<0$ for all positive integers $k>2(n-1)C/\delta$ . Proof. For $k\geq 1$ we have $p(k)\leq\left(-\delta+\frac{(n-1)C}{k}\right)k^{n-1}$ and the statement follows. ∎ Proof of Theorem 5.1. We divide the proof into cases according to the sign of $\imath$ . The case $\imath<0$ follows from the obvious inequality: $\pardeg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V}_{*})\leq\deg_{P_{k}}(\mathcal{V})$ (see Remark 4.21) together with Lemma 5.13. Therefore, we can assume that $\imath\geq 0$ . By Lemmas 5.14 and 5.15, it is enough to show that $c:=\imath-\sum_{H|H\pitchfork\mathcal{V}}a_{H}$ (5.18) is $<0$ . Since index of the distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is equal to $\imath\geq 0$ , by Proposition 5.10 we get that $c=-\delta$ for some $\delta>0$ that depends only on $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ . This concludes the proof of Theorem 5.1 . ∎ Remark 5.16. Tracing back the arguments in the proof of Theorem 5.1, we showed that we can take $k_{0}=C/\delta$ , where $C$ only depends on $\mathcal{H}$ and the polarization coefficients $b_{L}$ , and $\delta$ is as in Proposition 5.10 . 5.4 Proof of Theorem 1.1 Let $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ be the parabolic bundle on $(X,D)$ as defined in Section 4.2 and let $P_{k}$ be the polarization on $X$ as given by Lemma 3.38 . Consider the top product of the polarization $P_{k}$ with $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ given by $p(k)=c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\,.$ (5.19) The expression $p(k)$ defines a polynomial in $k$ of degree $n-2$ with real coefficients. The coefficients of $p(k)$ depend only on the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ and the fixed integers $b_{L}$ involved in the choice of polarization $P_{k}$ . More precisely, if we write $c_{1}(P_{k})=k\cdot\pi^{*}h-e$ with $e=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{\circ}}b_{L}\cdot\gamma_{L}$ , then $p(k)=C_{n-2}k^{n-2}+C_{n-1}k^{n-1}+\ldots+C_{0}\,,$ (5.20) where the coefficients $C_{j}$ are given by $C_{j}=\binom{n-2}{j}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{j}\cdot(-e)^{n-2-j}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\,.$ (5.21) Lemma 5.17. The coefficient $C_{n-2}$ in Equation (5.20) is given by $C_{n-2}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}\,,$ (5.22) where $B_{H}+1$ is the number of codimension $2$ irreducible subspaces contained in $H$ . Proof. By Equation (5.21) the coefficient $C_{n-2}$ is equal to $C_{n-2}=(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*}).$ (5.23) Equation (4.33) expresses $\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})$ as a sum of $3$ terms: $\begin{gathered}\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})=-\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{2}\\ +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\codim L\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}+\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\,.\end{gathered}$ Taking the corresponding products of the above $3$ terms with $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}$ we have $C_{n-2}=\textup{A}+\textup{B}+\textup{C}$ (5.24) where A, B, and C are given as follows • A is the easiest and is equal to $\textup{A}=-\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot(\pi^{*}h)^{n}=-\frac{n+1}{2}\,.$ (5.25) • B is the middle term involving self-intersections. We use Corollary 3.32 (ii) together with Equations (3.14) and (3.18) to obtain correspondingly: $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\codim L>2\,,\\ -1&\text{ if }\codim L=2\,,\\ -B_{H}&\text{ if }L=H\in\mathcal{H}\,.\end{cases}$ We conclude that $\displaystyle\textup{B}$ $\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}}a_{L}^{2}\cdot\codim L\cdot\left((\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}^{2}\right)$ (5.26) $\displaystyle=-\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,\,-\,\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}\,.$ • C involves mixed intersections for pairs $L\subsetneq M$ . It follows from Lemma 3.31 that if $\codim L>2$ then $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}=0.$ On the other hand, if $\codim L=2$ and $M=H$ is a hyperplane that contains $L$ then by Lemma 3.35 we get $(\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{H}=1.$ We conclude that $\displaystyle\textup{C}$ $\displaystyle=\sum_{L\subsetneq M}a_{L}a_{M}\cdot\codim M\cdot\left((\pi^{*}h)^{n-2}\cdot\gamma_{L}\cdot\gamma_{M}\right)$ (5.27) $\displaystyle=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}\cdot\left(\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\right)$ $\displaystyle=2\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,.$ The result follows from Equations (5.25), (5.26), and (5.27). ∎ Remark 5.18. Note that $C_{n-2}$ is independent of the integers $b_{L}$ involved in the choice of polarization, unlike the other coefficients $C_{j}$ . Proof of Theorem 1.1. Let $(\mathcal{H},\textbf{a})$ be klt and CY. By Theorem 5.1, the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ on $(X,D)$ is slope stable with respect to the polarization $P_{k}$ for all $k\gg 1$ . By Theorem 4.29 the parabolic bundle $\mathcal{E}_{*}$ is locally abelian, therefore we can apply Theorem 4.24 . By the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality (4.13), for all $k\gg 1$ we have $p(k)=c_{1}(P_{k})^{n-2}\cdot\parch_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{*})\leq 0\,.$ (5.28) In particular, the coefficient $C_{n-2}$ of the highest order term of $p(k)$ in Equation (5.20) must be non-positive, i.e., $C_{n-2}\leq 0\,.$ (5.29) Equation (1.3) follows from Equation (5.29) together with Lemma 5.17 . ∎ 6 The quadratic form and the stable cone In Section 6.1 , we define the quadratic form $Q:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}$ associated to $\mathcal{H}$ by extending the left hand side of Equation (1.3) from the affine hyperplane $\{\sum_{H}a_{H}=n+1\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ to the whole space $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ as a homogeneous degree $2$ polynomial. In Section 6.2 , we introduce the concept of stable weighted arrangements and the stable cone $C^{\circ}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ of the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ . In Section 6.3 , we introduce the semistable cone $C$ , the matroid polytope, and show that the quadratic form $Q$ is $\leq 0$ on $C$ (Theorem 6.29). In Section 6.4 , we provide links to GIT and stability of pairs. 6.1 The quadratic form of an arrangement Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a hyperplane arrangement. Let $s$ be the linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ that takes a vector $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ and sends it to the sum of its components, $s=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\,.$ (6.1) Recall that if $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ is an irreducible subspace of codimension $2$ then the weight $a_{L}$ at $L$ is given by $a_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\,.$ (6.2) Thus, we can think of $a_{L}$ as a linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ as well. Definition 6.1. The quadratic form $Q=Q(\mathbf{a})$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is the homogeneous polynomial of degree $2$ on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ given by $Q(\mathbf{a})=4(n+1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,-\,2(n+1)\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}\,-\,2\cdot s^{2}\,,$ (6.3) where $B_{H}+1$ is the number of irreducible codimension $2$ subspaces contained in $H$ . Taking common factor $4(n+1)$ , we have $Q(\mathbf{a})=4(n+1)\cdot\left(\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,-\,\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}\,-\,\frac{s^{2}}{2(n+1)}\right)\,.$ (6.4) Up to the constant dimensional factor $4(n+1)$ , the quadratic form $Q$ agrees with the left hand side of Equation (1.3) on the affine hyperplane $\{s=n+1\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ . We record this fact as a lemma. Lemma 6.2. If $s(\mathbf{a})=n+1$ , then $Q(\mathbf{a})=4(n+1)\left(\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,-\,\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}B_{H}\cdot a_{H}^{2}\,-\,\frac{n+1}{2}\,\right)\,.$ We can write down the symmetric matrix associated with the quadratic form $Q$ . To do this, label the hyperplanes, say $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\}\,,$ and let $a_{i}=a_{H_{i}}$ be the weight at the hyperplane $H_{i}$ . Then, we can write $Q(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{i}Q_{ii}\cdot a_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i<j}Q_{ij}\cdot 2a_{i}a_{j}\,.$ The coefficients $Q_{ij}$ can be easily calculated from Equation (6.3). Let $\sigma_{i}$ be the number of irreducible codimension $2$ subspaces contained in $H_{i}$ , i.e., $\sigma_{i}=B_{H_{i}}+1$ . Then $Q_{ij}=\begin{cases}-(n+1)\sigma_{i}+2n&\text{ if }i=j\,,\\ -2&\text{ if }i\neq j\text{ and }H_{i}\cap H_{j}\text{ is reducible },\\ \,\,n-1&\text{ if }i\neq j\text{ and }H_{i}\cap H_{j}\text{ is irreducible }\,.\end{cases}$ (6.5) By slight abuse of notation, we shall also write $Q$ for the symmetric matrix $(Q_{ij})$ . Remark 6.3. If $n=2$ then $Q_{ij}=-A_{ij}$ where $A_{ij}$ are the entries of symmetric matrix defined by Hirzebruch [Hir85, Equation (3)] regarding Höfer’s formula for the ‘proportionality’ $3c_{2}-c_{1}^{2}$ of coverings of the projective plane branched along line arrangements. For the record, we note the following property of the quadratic form: Lemma 6.4. The sum of the $i$ -th column (or $i$ -th row) of the matrix $(Q_{ij})$ is given by $\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{ij}=(n-1)\cdot N\,-\,(n+1)\cdot(t_{i}-1)\,,$ (6.6) where $t_{i}$ is the number of codimension $2$ subspaces $L\in\mathcal{L}$ contained in $H_{i}$ . Proof. Let $\mathcal{R}$ and $\mathcal{I}$ be the set of all reducible and irreducible codimension $2$ subspaces contained in $H_{i}$ and let $T=\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{I}$ . We write $\tau_{i}=|\mathcal{R}|$ and $\sigma_{i}=|\mathcal{I}|$ , so that $t_{i}=\tau_{i}+\sigma_{i}$ . It follows from Equation (6.5) that $\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}Q_{ij}$ $\displaystyle=2n-(n+1)\cdot\sigma_{i}-2\tau_{i}+(n-1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{I}}(m_{L}-1)$ $\displaystyle=2n-(n+1)\cdot t_{i}\,+\,(n-1)\cdot\tau_{i}+(n-1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{I}}(m_{L}-1)$ $\displaystyle=2n-(n+1)\cdot t_{i}\,+\,(n-1)\cdot\sum_{L\in T}(m_{L}-1)$ Since for every $j\neq i$ the intersection $H_{i}\cap H_{j}$ is a codimension $2$ subspace contained in $H_{i}$ $\sum_{L\in T}(m_{L}-1)=N-1\,.$ Therefore, $\sum_{j=1}^{n}Q_{ij}=2n-(n+1)\cdot t_{i}+(n-1)\cdot(N-1)$ which implies (6.6). ∎ Remark 6.5. It follows from Lemma 6.4 that the vector $\mathbf{1}$ with all components equal to $1$ is an eigenvector of $Q$ if and only if every hyperplane $H\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects the other hyperplanes of the arrangement along the same number of codimension $2$ subspaces. The next example shows that, for most arrangements, the quadratic form $Q$ is non-degenerate and indefinite. Example 6.6. Let $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\}$ be an arrangement in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ which is generic, in the sense that no $3$ distinct hyperplanes are linearly dependent. Suppose that the number of hyperplanes $N$ is $>n+1$ . The generic assumption implies that $\sigma_{i}=0$ for all $i$ . Equation (6.5) gives us $Q=2\cdot\begin{pmatrix}n&-1&\cdots&-1\\ -1&n&\cdots&-1\\ \cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\ -1&-1&\cdots&n\\ \end{pmatrix}\,.$ Let $\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}$ be the vector with all entries equal to $1$ . Then $\mathbf{1}$ is an eigenvector with $Q\cdot\mathbf{1}=2(n+1-N)\cdot\mathbf{1}\,.$ The assumption that $N>n+1$ implies that the eigenvalue is $<0$ . On the other hand, if $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})$ is a vector with $\sum_{i}a_{i}=0$ , i.e., $\mathbf{a}$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf{1}$ , then $Q\cdot\mathbf{a}=2(n+1)\cdot\mathbf{a}\,.$ This shows that the orthogonal complement $\mathbf{1}^{\perp}$ is an eigenspace of $Q$ with positive eigenvalue. Thus, the quadratic form $Q$ is non-degenerate of signature $(N-1,1)$ . Next, we provide an example for which the quadratic form $Q$ is negative semidefinite and has non trivial kernel. Example 6.7. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the braid (or $A_{n+1}$ ) arrangement consisting of the $\binom{n+2}{2}$ hyperplanes $H_{ij}=\{x_{i}=x_{j}\}$ for $1\leq i<j\leq n+2$ in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{C}^{n+2}\big/\{x_{1}=x_{2}=\ldots=x_{n+2}\}\right).$ The elements $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ are of the form $L=\{x_{i}=x_{j}=x_{k}\}$ for a triplet of distinct indices $i,j,k$ . The number of codimension $2$ irreducible subspaces contained in $H_{ij}$ is equal to $n=|[n+2]\setminus\{i,j\}|$ . Therefore, $B_{H}=n-1$ for all $H$ . Equation (6.3) gives us $Q=(n+1)\sum_{i<j<k}\left(a_{ij}+a_{ik}+a_{jk}\right)^{2}-(n^{2}-1)\cdot\sum_{i<j}a_{ij}^{2}-2\cdot\left(\sum_{i<j}a_{ij}\right)^{2}\,,$ (6.7) where $a_{ij}$ is the weight at $H_{ij}$ . This quadratic form is negative semidefinite and has non trivial kernel, as shown by the next. Lemma 6.8. The quadratic form (6.7) is negative semidefinite on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ with kernel the linear subspace of $K\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ parametrized by $a_{ij}=a_{i}+a_{j}$ with $(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+2})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2}$ . Proof. Identify $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ with $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ where $N=\binom{n+2}{2}$ with coordinates $(a_{ij})_{i<j}$ and basis vectors $\mathbf{e}_{ij}$ . If $Q$ is the symmetric matrix of the quadratic form, then we can write $Q\cdot\mathbf{a}=\sum_{i<j}\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{q}_{ij}\rangle\cdot\mathbf{e}_{ij}\,,$ (6.8) where $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ is the Euclidean product on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and the vectors $\mathbf{q}_{ij}$ are the rows of the matrix $Q$ given by Equation (6.5) $(\mathbf{q}_{ij})_{kl}=\begin{cases}n(1-n)&\text{ if }\{k,l\}=\{i,j\}\,,\\ -2&\text{ if }\{k,l\}\cap\{i,j\}=\emptyset\,,\\ n-1&\text{ if }|\{k,l\}\cap\{i,j\}|=1\,.\end{cases}$ (6.9) Let $\mathbf{v}_{i}$ be the vector with components $(\mathbf{v}_{i})_{kl}=\begin{cases}1\,\,&\textup{ if }\,\,i\in\{k,l\}\\ 0\,\,&\textup{ if }\,\,i\notin\{k,l\}\end{cases}$ so that $\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{n+2}$ make a basis of the linear subspace $K$ . By Equation (6.9), $\mathbf{q}_{ij}=(n+1)\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{i}+\mathbf{v}_{j})-n(n+1)\mathbf{e}_{ij}-2\cdot\mathbf{1}\,,$ (6.10) where $\mathbf{1}$ is the vector with all entries equal to $1$ . By Equations (6.8) and (6.10), together with $\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{1}\rangle=n+1\,,\hskip 5.69054pt\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle=\begin{cases}n+1&\text{ if }i=j\,,\\ 1&\text{ if }i\neq j\,,\end{cases}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{ and }\mathbf{1}\in K\,,$ we deduce that $Q\cdot\mathbf{v}_{i}=0$ for all $i$ and $Q\cdot\mathbf{a}=-n(n+1)\cdot\mathbf{a}$ for all $\mathbf{a}\in K^{\perp}$ . Thus, the quadratic form $Q$ is negative semidefinite with kernel $K$ . ∎ Remark 6.9. For $n=2$ , [Hir85, §5] asserts that for all reflection line arrangements listed in Section 3 of that paper, the quadratic form $Q$ is negative semidefinite. In Theorem 7.29 , we provide an extension to higher dimensions. The calculation of the kernel of $Q$ for reflection arrangements is also related to the classification of Dunkl connections in [CHL05, §2.6]. Example 6.10. Let $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}$ be a product arrangement in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ (Definition 2.5) with $\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{2}}$ . Then, in an obvious notation, $\frac{Q}{n+1}=\frac{Q_{1}}{n_{1}+1}+\frac{Q_{2}}{n_{2}+1}+\frac{2s_{1}^{2}}{n_{1}+1}+\frac{2s_{2}^{2}}{n_{2}+1}-\frac{2s^{2}}{n+1}\,.$ In particular, $\frac{Q}{n+1}=\frac{Q_{1}}{n_{1}+1}+\frac{Q_{2}}{n_{2}+1}$ on the intersection $\{s_{1}=n_{1}+1\}\cap\{s_{2}=n_{2}+1\}$ . 6.2 The stable cone Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a hyperplane arrangement and let $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ be a weight vector with positive weights $a_{H}>0$ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . Let $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}$ be the rescaled vector $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{a}\,\,\text{ with }\,\,\lambda=\frac{n+1}{s}\,,$ (6.11) where $s$ is the total sum of the weights as in Equation (6.1). Definition 6.11. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if the rescaled weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ is klt. We have the following restatement of Theorem 1.1. Theorem 6.12. If $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is a stable weighted arrangement, then $Q(\mathbf{a})\leq 0$ . Proof. Since $Q$ is homogeneous, it suffices to show that $Q(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})\leq 0$ . The choice of rescaling factor $\lambda$ given by Equation (6.11) ensures that the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ is CY. On the other hand, the stability assumption requires that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ is klt. By Lemma 6.2 and Theorem 1.1 , $Q(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})\leq 0$ . ∎ Remark 6.13. If $n=1$ and $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable then $\mathcal{H}$ must have at least $3$ points. In this case, we take the convention that $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{-1}=\{\emptyset\}$ . Since $\emptyset$ is contained in all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , we have that $a_{\emptyset}=s/2$ and $B_{H}=1-1=0$ for all $H$ . Therefore, $Q=s^{2}/4-s^{2}/4=0$ and the statement of Theorem 6.12 is trivial. We provide $3$ slightly different but equivalent systems of linear inequalities that characterize the weights for which the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is stable. For an arbitrary non-empty and proper linear subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , consider the equation $\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}<\frac{\codim L}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\,.$ (6.12) Lemma 6.14. A weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ with positive weights $a_{H}>0$ is stable if and only if any of the following equivalent conditions is satisfied: (i) Equation (6.12) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ ; (ii) Equation (6.12) holds for every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ ; (iii) Equation (6.12) holds for every non-empty and proper linear subspace $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Proof. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if the rescaled CY weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ is klt. Corollary 2.26 implies that (i), (ii), and (iii) are equivalent. ∎ Definition 6.15. The stable cone $C^{\circ}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is the set of all weights $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{>0}$ such that the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable. Equivalently, $C^{\circ}$ is the cone over the set of weights $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{\mathcal{H}}$ for which the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY. By Lemma 6.14 , the stable cone $C^{\circ}$ is an open convex polyhedral cone; it is the subset of the positive octant defined by the linear inequalities (6.12). Next, we give two explicit examples of stable cones. Example 6.16. If $\mathcal{H}$ is normal crossing then the only irreducible subspaces are the hyperplanes of the arrangement, i.e., $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}=\mathcal{H}$ . By Lemma 6.14 (i), the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if $\forall H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,0<a_{H}<\frac{s}{n+1}\,.$ (6.13) Example 6.17. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the braid arrangement as in Example 6.7. The irreducible subspaces $L_{I}$ correspond to subsets $I\subset[n+2]$ with $2\leq|I|\leq n+1$ by letting $L_{I}=\{x_{i}=x_{j}\,\,\textup{ for }\,\,i,j\in I\}$ . In particular, $\codim L_{I}=|I|-1$ . By Lemma 6.14 (i), the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ with weights $a_{ij}>0$ is stable if and only if for every $I\subset[n+2]$ with $2\leq|I|\leq n+1$ we have $\sum_{i<j\,\,|\,\,i,j\in I}a_{ij}<\frac{|I|-1}{n+1}\cdot s\,.$ Theorem 6.12 can be restated as follows. Theorem 6.18. The quadratic form $Q$ is negative semidefinite on the stable cone, $C^{\circ}\subset\{Q\leq 0\}\,.$ (6.14) Remark 6.19. Of course, by continuity, $Q\leq 0$ on the closure $\overline{C^{\circ}}$ . For generic arrangements, we can check Theorem 6.18 by direct calculation, as shown in the next example. Example 6.20. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be generic with $|\mathcal{H}|>n+1$ as in Example 6.6 . Then $Q=2(n+1)\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}^{2}\,-\,2\cdot s^{2}\,.$ (6.15) is non-degenerate and it has signature $(|\mathcal{H}|-1,1)$ . By Lemma 6.14 , if $\mathbf{a}\in C^{\circ}$ then $a_{H}<s/(n+1)$ . Therefore, $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}^{2}<\frac{s}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}=\frac{s^{2}}{n+1}\,.$ (6.16) It follows from Equations (6.15) and (6.16) that $Q<0$ on $C^{\circ}$ . 6.3 The matroid polytope and the semistable cone We present a geometric description of the set of weights for which an arrangement is stable, involving standard constructions of polytopes associated to matroids. For a more in depth discussion and relations to toric geometry, see [Ale15] . Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a hyperplane arrangement. Definition 6.21. A basis $\mathcal{B}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is a subset $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}$ with $|\mathcal{B}|=n+1$ and $\bigcap_{H\in\mathcal{B}}H=\emptyset\,.$ The arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ has a basis if and only if $\mathcal{H}$ is essential. If $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis, then up to a linear change of coordinates, $\mathcal{B}$ can be identified with the set of $n+1$ coordinate hyperplanes in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Definition 6.22. The indicator function of a basis $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}$ is the vector $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ with components $(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})_{H}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }H\in\mathcal{B}\,,\\ 0&\text{ if }H\notin\mathcal{B}\,.\end{cases}$ Definition 6.23. The matroid polytope $P$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is the convex hull of the vectors $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}$ with $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}$ a basis. The matroid polytope $P$ is non-empty precisely when $\mathcal{H}$ is essential. Since for every indicator function of a basis $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}$ we have $s(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})=n+1$ , the matroid polytope is contained in the affine hyperplane $s=n+1$ , $P\subset\{s=n+1\}\,.$ Therefore, the dimension of $P$ is at most $|\mathcal{H}|-1$ . It follows from [BGW03, Theorem 1.12.9] that, if $\mathcal{H}$ is an essential arrangement, then $\dim P=|\mathcal{H}|-k\,,$ where $k$ is the number of factors in the decomposition of $\mathcal{H}$ as a product of irreducible arrangements $\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{H}_{k}$ . A dual description of the matroid polytope in terms of defining linear inequalities is given by the following result of Edmonds. Theorem 6.24 ([Sch03, Corollary 40.2d]). The matroid polytope $P$ is the subset of the affine hyperplane $\{s=n+1\}$ in $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ defined by the following inequalities: $\begin{gathered}\forall H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,a_{H}\geq 0\,,\\ \forall L\in\mathcal{L}:\,\,\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\leq\codim L\,.\end{gathered}$ (6.17) Let $P^{\circ}$ be the relative interior of the matroid polytope $P$ inside the hyperplane $\{s=n+1\}$ . Specifically, $P^{\circ}$ is the subset of $\{s=n+1\}$ of points for which the inequalities (6.17) are strict. In particular, $P^{\circ}$ is non-empty if and only if $P$ has dimension $|\mathcal{H}|-1$ . Corollary 6.25. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY if and only if $\mathbf{a}\in P^{\circ}\,.$ (6.18) Proof. By definition, $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt if and only if all the inequalities in (6.17) are strict. The result follows from Theorem 6.24 . ∎ Corollary 6.25 implies the next. Corollary 6.26. The stable cone $C^{\circ}$ is the open polyhedral cone given by $C^{\circ}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\cdot P^{\circ}\,.$ (6.19) Definition 6.27. The semistable cone $C\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ is the cone over the matroid polytope, $C=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot P\,.$ (6.20) Remark 6.28. The stable cone $C^{\circ}$ is the interior of the semistable cone $C$ , as a subset of $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ . If $C^{\circ}$ is non-empty then $C$ is equal to its closure, $C=\overline{C^{\circ}}$ . By Corollary B.2, this happens precisely when $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible. However, if the essential arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible, then $C^{\circ}$ is empty but $C$ is not. We can now state our main result in its more general form. Theorem 6.29. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an essential arrangement. Then the quadratic form $Q$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is $\leq 0$ on the semistable cone $C\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ . Proof. It suffices to show that $Q\leq 0$ on the matroid polytope $P$ of $\mathcal{H}$ . If $\mathcal{H}$ is reducible, say $\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}$ , then the matroid polytope of $\mathcal{H}$ is the product of the matroid polytopes of the respective factors, say $P=P_{1}\times P_{2}$ . By Example 6.10 , on the polytope $P$ , we have $\frac{Q}{n+1}=\frac{Q_{1}}{n+1}+\frac{Q_{2}}{n_{2}+1}\,.$ Therefore, to prove the theorem, we can assume that $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible. Suppose that $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible. As pointed out in Remark 6.28 , if the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is irreducible then the semistable cone $C$ is the closure of the stable cone $C^{\circ}$ . By Theorem 6.18 , the quadratic form $Q$ of $\mathcal{H}$ is $\leq 0$ on $C^{\circ}$ . By continuity, $Q\leq 0$ on $C$ . ∎ 6.4 Relation to GIT and stability of pairs In this section we justify the name stable cone, by providing links to Geometric Invariant Theory and stability of pairs. 6.4.1 Configurations of points It is useful to work dually with configurations of points rather than hyperplane arrangements. We set-up the basic definitions. Definition 6.30. A weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is a finite set $\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}$ of points $p_{i}\in\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ together with a weight vector $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}$ . Remark 6.31. Note that we require the weights $a_{i}$ to be positive. Informally, we can think of $a_{i}$ as the mass of the point $p_{i}$ . The main concept of interest is the following. Definition 6.32. A weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable, if for every non-empty and proper projective subspace $W\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ the following holds: $\sum_{i\,|\,p_{i}\in W}a_{i}<\frac{\dim W+1}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\,.$ (6.21) The sum poset of $\mathcal{P}$ is the finite set $\mathcal{U}$ of all proper non-empty subspaces $U\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ spanned by points in $\mathcal{P}$ , equipped with the partial order given by inclusion. The next result is dual to Lemma 6.14 , we omit its proof. Lemma 6.33. The weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if $\forall U\in\mathcal{U}:\,\,\sum_{i\,|\,p_{i}\in U}a_{i}<\frac{\dim U+1}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\,.$ (6.22) Next, we relate stability of weighted configurations of points to our previous notion of stable weighted arrangements. Notation 6.34. If $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is a linear subspace, we write $L^{\perp}\subset(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}$ for the (projectivization) of the annihilator of $L$ . In particular, $\codim L=\dim L^{\perp}+1\,.$ (6.23) Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a hyperplane arrangement. For each $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , there is a unique up to scalar multiplication linear function $\ell_{H}\in(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ such that $H=\{\ell_{H}=0\}$ . The annihilator $H^{\perp}$ is the uniquely defined point in $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}=\mathbb{P}((\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*})$ given by $H^{\perp}=[\ell_{H}]$ . This way, the arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ corresponds to a configuration of points $\mathcal{P}\subset(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}$ with $\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}=\{H^{\perp}\,|\,H\in\mathcal{H}\}\,.$ Recall that $\mathcal{L}$ is the poset of non-empty and proper subspaces $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained by intersecting members $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , equipped with the partial order given by reverse inclusion. Similarly, $\mathcal{U}$ is the poset of non-empty and proper subspaces $U\subset(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}$ obtained as sums of points $p\in\mathcal{P}$ , equipped with the partial order given by inclusion. The correspondence $L\mapsto U=L^{\perp}$ defines an isomorphism of posets between $\mathcal{L}$ and $\mathcal{U}$ . Let $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ be a weight vector with components $a_{H}>0$ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ . Let $m=|\mathcal{H}|$ and label the hyperplanes $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{m}\}$ . Write $\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}$ with $p_{i}=H_{i}^{\perp}$ and $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})$ with $a_{i}=a_{H_{i}}$ . Lemma 6.35. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable (as in Definition 6.11) if and only if the weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable (as in Definition 6.32). Proof. By Lemma 6.14 , the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if, for every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ we have $g_{L}(\mathbf{a})>0$ , where $g_{L}(\mathbf{a})=\frac{\codim L}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}-\sum_{i\,|\,L\subset H_{i}}a_{i}\,.$ By Lemma 6.33, the weighted configuration $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if, for every $U\in\mathcal{U}$ we have $f_{U}(\mathbf{a})>0$ , where $f_{U}(\mathbf{a})=\frac{\dim U+1}{n+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}-\sum_{i\,|\,p_{i}\in U}a_{i}\,.$ If $U=L^{\perp}$ for some $L\in\mathcal{L}$ , then (6.23) asserts that $\dim U+1=\codim L$ . On the other hand, $L\subset H_{i}$ if and only if $p_{i}\in U$ . Therefore, $g_{L}(\mathbf{a})=f_{U}(\mathbf{a})\,.$ Using this, the lemma follows from the fact that $L\mapsto U=L^{\perp}$ is a bijection between the intersection poset $\mathcal{L}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ and the sum poset $\mathcal{U}$ of $\mathcal{P}$ . ∎ 6.4.2 Geometric Invariant Theory Given a reductive group $G$ acting on a projective variety $X$ together with a $G$ -linearised ample line bundle $L$ , there is a standard notion of a point $p\in X$ being GIT stable. In our case of interest, $X$ is embedded in $\mathbb{CP}^{k}$ (for some $k\gg 1$ ) via sections of $L$ and $G$ acts on $X$ through linear transformations of $\mathbb{C}^{k+1}$ . In this case, a point $p\in X$ is GIT stable if the isotropy subgroup $G_{p}$ is finite and the orbit $G\cdot\tilde{p}\subset\mathbb{C}^{k+1}$ is closed, where $\widetilde{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{k+1}$ is any point that projects down to $p\in\mathbb{CP}^{k}$ . To make the connection, let $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted configuration of $m$ distinct points in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and assume that all the weights $a_{i}$ are positive integers. Let $X$ be the product of $m$ -copies of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and let $L_{\mathbf{a}}$ be the polarization on $X$ given by $L_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\pr_{j}^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(a_{j})\right)\,,$ where $X\xrightarrow{\pr_{j}}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the projection to the $j$ -factor. Lemma 6.36. The weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if the point $p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in X$ is GIT stable for the diagonal action of $G=SL(n+1,\mathbb{C})$ on $X$ linearised by the polarization $L_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Proof. This is follows from [Dol03, Theorem 11.2] and Definition 6.32 . ∎ The link to differential geometry is provided by the Kempf-Ness theorem.
