To avoid distinctions between stable and polystable points, which have continuous isotropy subgroups, we assume that $G_{p}=\{\textup{identity}\}$ . Equivalently, we assume that $\mathcal{P}$ is essential and irreducible (see Section B). There is a standard embedding of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}\subset\mathfrak{su}(n+1)^{*}$ , as a coadjoint orbit. Then $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if there is $F\in SL(n+1,\mathbb{C})$ such that the centre of mass of the points $F(p_{i})$ with weights $a_{i}$ in the Euclidean space $\mathfrak{su}(n+1)^{*}$ is equal to zero. This interpretation of the stable condition remains valid for arbitrary positive real weights, see [KLM09, Example 6.3]. Example 6.37. If $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is a collection of points $p_{i}\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ with weights $a_{i}>0$ . Then $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable $\iff a_{i}<(1/2)\cdot\left(\sum_{j}a_{j}\right)$ for all $i$ ; equivalently: $\forall i:\,\,a_{i}<\sum_{j\neq i}a_{j}\,.$ (6.24) In this case, the Kemp-Ness theorem becomes the familiar statement that a collection of $3$ or more points on the unit sphere $S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with masses $a_{i}$ can be moved by an element of $SL(2,\mathbb{C})$ to have zero centre of mass if and only if (6.24) is satisfied. 6.4.3 Stability of pairs A weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ corresponds to a log pair $(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\Delta)$ with $\Delta=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\cdot H\,.$ From MMP, we recall the following. Definition 6.38. The weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is log canonical if the following inequalities hold: $\begin{gathered}\forall\,H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,0<a_{H}\leq 1\,,\\ \forall\,L\in\mathcal{L}:\,\,\sum_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}\leq\codim L\,.\end{gathered}$ (6.25) Remark 6.39. Definition 6.38 is broader than klt condition, in the sense that the strict inequalities $<$ in (1.1) are relaxed to weak inequalities $\leq$ in (6.25). Recall that we write $s$ for the linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ given by $s=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\,.$ Lemma 6.40. Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a log canonical weighted arrangement. (i) If $s>n+1$ then $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable. (ii) If $s<n+1$ then $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if $(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\Delta)$ is $K$ -stable. Proof. (i) If $s>n+1$ then the rescaling factor $\lambda=(n+1)/s$ in Equation (6.11) is $<1$ . Therefore, the weights $a^{\prime}_{H}=\lambda\cdot a_{H}$ of the rescaled arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ satisfy $a^{\prime}_{H}<a_{H}$ for all $H$ . This implies that the weak inequalities $\leq$ in (6.25) become strict inequalities $<$ ; showing that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})$ is klt. (ii) This is an immediate consequence of item (2) in [Fuj21, Theorem 1.5]. ∎ A weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is of general type if $s>n+1$ . It is Fano if $s<n+1$ . Remark 6.41. Log canonical weighted arrangements of general type are studied in [Ale15] under the acronym ‘shas’, for ‘stable hyperplane arrangements’. 7 Hirzebruch arrangements and matroids In Section 7.1 we calculate the partial derivatives $\partial Q/\partial a_{H}$ of the quadratic form and express them in terms of total sums of weights on induced arrangements $\mathcal{H}^{H}$ . In Section 7.2 , we analyse the symmetric case where all the weights of the arrangements are equal. This leads us to define a class of arrangements, which we call Hirzebruch arrangements, for which the main diagonal of $\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ is contained in the kernel of the quadratic form $Q$ . In Section 7.3 , we show that if $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is a complex reflection arrangement defined by an irreducible unitary complex reflection group $G\subset U(n+1)$ , then $\mathcal{H}$ is Hirzebruch (Proposition 7.27) and its quadratic form is negative semidefinite. In Section 7.4 , we reformulate our results in the more general context of matroids. 7.1 Critical points of $Q$ and induced arrangements We calculate the partial derivatives of $Q$ . Lemma 7.3 expresses $\partial Q/\partial a_{H}$ in terms of the sum of the induced weights on the induced arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{H}$ , as defined next. We use the notation for deletion and restriction triples as in [OT92, Definition 1.14]. Fix $H\in\mathcal{H}$ and write $(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime})$ where $\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}$ and $\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{H}^{H}$ is the induced arrangement obtained by intersecting $H$ with members of $\mathcal{H}^{\prime}$ as defined in Section 2.1 . An element $H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}$ is a codimension $2$ subspace of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . In particular, we can distinguish the elements of $\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}$ into two different types depending on whether $H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ or not. Recall that $H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ if and only if its multiplicity $m_{H^{\prime\prime}}=|\mathcal{H}_{H^{\prime\prime}}|$ is $\geq 3$ , where $\mathcal{H}_{H^{\prime\prime}}$ is the localization of $\mathcal{H}$ at $H^{\prime\prime}$ . If $H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ , then $a_{H^{\prime\prime}}$ is defined as one-half of the sum of all the weights of the hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ that contain $H^{\prime\prime}$ . Definition 7.1. The weights of the induced arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}$ are defined as follows: $a^{\prime\prime}_{H^{\prime\prime}}=\begin{cases}a_{H^{\prime\prime}}&\text{if }H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\,,\\ a_{H^{\prime}}&\text{if }\mathcal{H}_{H^{\prime\prime}}=\{H,H^{\prime}\}\,.\end{cases}$ (7.1) The sum of the induced weights on $\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}$ is denoted by $s_{H}$ . More precisely, $s_{H}=\sum_{H^{\prime}|H^{\prime}\pitchfork H}a_{H^{\prime}}\,\,+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}a_{L}\,.$ (7.2) Lemma 7.2. For every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ the following identity holds $B_{H}\cdot a_{H}=s_{H}\,-\,s\,\,+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}a_{L}\,.$ (7.3) Proof. Since every hyperplane $H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}$ intersects $H$ either at $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ or $H^{\prime}\pitchfork H$ , we have $\displaystyle s$ $\displaystyle=a_{H}\,+\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}(2a_{L}-a_{H})\,+\,\sum_{H^{\prime}|H^{\prime}\pitchfork H}a_{H^{\prime}}$ $\displaystyle=-B_{H}\cdot a_{H}\,+\,s_{H}\,\,+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}a_{L}\,.$ and (7.2) follows. ∎ Lemma 7.3. The partial derivatives of the quadratic form are given by $\frac{\partial Q}{\partial a_{H}}=4n\cdot s-4(n+1)\cdot s_{H}\,.$ (7.4) Proof. By Equation (6.2) we have $\frac{\partial a_{L}}{\partial a_{H}}=\begin{cases}1/2&\text{ if }H\supset L\,,\\ 0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}$ Taking the partial derivative of $Q$ with respect to $a_{H}$ in Equation (6.4) gives us $\frac{1}{4(n+1)}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial a_{H}}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}a_{L}\,-\,B_{H}\cdot a_{H}\,-\,\frac{s}{n+1}.$ Using Equation (7.3) we obtain $\frac{1}{4(n+1)}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial a_{H}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n+1}\right)s-s_{H}$ and (7.4) follows. ∎ A critical point $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}$ of $Q$ is a point where all partial derivatives $\partial Q/\partial a_{H}$ vanish. The set of all critical points of $Q$ is the kernel of the quadratic form. Equation (7.4) gives us the following set of defining linear equations for the kernel of $Q$ . Corollary 7.4. A weight vector $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ is a critical point of $Q$ if and only if $\forall\,H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,s_{H}=\frac{n}{n+1}s\,.$ (7.5) We use the above calculation of partial derivatives to give an alternative expression for the quadratic form $Q$ . Notation 7.5. We denote by $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{red}}^{n-2}$ the set of all reducible codimension $2$ subspaces. Concretely, $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{red}}^{n-2}$ if and only if its multiplicity is $m_{L}=2$ . In this case, we write $\mathcal{H}_{L}=\{H,H^{\prime}\}$ with $H,H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}$ and $L=H\pitchfork H^{\prime}$ . Corollary 7.6. The quadratic form of $\mathcal{H}$ is equal to $Q=2n\cdot s^{2}\,-\,4(n+1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{red}}^{n-2}}a_{H}\cdot a_{H^{\prime}}\,-\,4(n+1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,\,,$ (7.6) where the second sum runs over all $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{red}}^{n-2}$ with $L=H\pitchfork H^{\prime}$ . Proof. Since $Q$ is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $2$ , we have $2Q=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial a_{H}}\,.$ (7.7) Replacing the values for $\partial Q/\partial a_{H}$ given by Lemma 7.3 together with Equation (7.2) for $s_{H}$ gives us $\displaystyle Q$ $\displaystyle=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\cdot\left(2n\cdot s-2(n+1)\cdot s_{H}\right)$ (7.8) $\displaystyle=2n\cdot s^{2}\,-\,2(n+1)\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\,\,\sum_{H^{\prime}|\,H^{\prime}\pitchfork H}a_{H}\cdot a_{H^{\prime}}$ $\displaystyle-\,2(n+1)\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\,\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}\,|\,L\subset H}a_{H}\cdot a_{L}\,.$ Note that $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\,\,\sum_{H^{\prime}|\,H^{\prime}\pitchfork H}a_{H}\cdot a_{H^{\prime}}=2\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{red}}^{n-2}}a_{H}\cdot a_{H^{\prime}}$ (7.9) and $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\,\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}\,|\,L\subset H}a_{H}\cdot a_{L}=2\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}}a_{L}^{2}\,.$ (7.10) Equation (7.6) follows from Equations (7.8), (7.9), and (7.10). ∎ 7.2 Hirzebruch arrangements Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an arrangement with $N=|\mathcal{H}|$ hyperplanes. We consider the case for where all hyperplanes have the same weight. Our main result is Theorem 7.14. We begin with an elementary lemma that calculates the total sum of the induced weights on the induced arrangements $\mathcal{H}^{H}$ . Fix $H\in\mathcal{H}$ and let $s_{H}$ be, as in Definition 7.1, given by $s_{H}=\sum_{H^{\prime}|H^{\prime}\pitchfork H}a_{H^{\prime}}+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}|L\subset H}a_{L}\,.$ (7.11) Lemma 7.7. Suppose that all the weights of $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ are equal, say $a_{H}=a$ for all $H$ . Then, for every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , we have $2s_{H}=\left(|\mathcal{H}^{H}|+N-1\right)\cdot a\,.$ (7.12) Proof. We want to replace the values $a_{H}=a$ in Equation (7.11) but there is an observation to do first. The weight $a_{H}$ of our fixed hyperplane $H$ occurs only in the irreducible terms $a_{L}$ but not in the reducible terms $a_{H^{\prime}}$ . We remedy this asymmetry by using the identity $a=(a+a)/2$ . Indeed, since all the weights $a_{H}$ are equal, we can replace each term $a_{H^{\prime}}$ with $(a_{H^{\prime}}+a_{H})/2$ . Since every hyperplane in $\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}$ intersects $H$ at a unique codimension $2$ subspace (see Figure 2) we obtain $2s_{H}=\underbrace{(|\mathcal{H}|-1)\cdot a}_{\text{ contribution of }\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}}+\underbrace{|\mathcal{H}^{H}|\cdot a}_{\text{ contribution of }H}$ from which the statement follows. ∎ $H$ $H^{\prime}$ $L$ $\widetilde{H}$ $2a_{H^{\prime}}=a_{H^{\prime}}+a_{H}$ $2a_{L}=\sum\limits_{\widetilde{H}|L\subset\widetilde{H}}a_{\widetilde{H}}$ Figure 2: Reducible (red) and irreducible (green) intersections. Recall that the multiplicity $m_{L}$ of an element $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is the number of hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ . We denote by $\mathcal{L}^{n-2}$ the set of all codimension $2$ subspaces of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as intersection of elements $H\in\mathcal{H}$ , so for any $L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}$ we have $m_{L}\geq 2$ and $m_{L}\geq 3$ if and only if $L$ is irreducible. Lemma 7.8. The following identity holds: $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}^{2}=N^{2}-N+\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}\,.$ (7.13) Proof. Since every pair of hyperplanes intersect at a codimension two subspace, $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}(m_{L}-1)=N(N-1)\,.$ (7.14) Equation (7.13) follows from Equation (7.14) by rearranging the terms. ∎ Notation 7.9. Let $\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ be the vector with all components equal to $1$ . Lemma 7.10. The value of the quadratic form at $\mathbf{1}$ is given by $Q(\mathbf{1})=(n-1)N^{2}+(n+1)N-(n+1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}\,.$ (7.15) Proof. Since $s(\mathbf{1})=N$ and $2a_{L}=m_{L}$ for all $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}^{n-2}$ , Equation (7.6) gives us $Q(\mathbf{1})=2n\cdot N^{2}\,-\,(n+1)\cdot\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}^{2}\,.$ Using Equation (7.13) for the sum of squared multiplicities gives Equation (7.15). ∎ Lemma 7.11. The vector $\mathbf{1}$ belongs to the stable cone $C^{\circ}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ if and only if $\forall L\in\mathcal{L}:\,\,m_{L}<\codim L\cdot\frac{N}{n+1}\,.$ (7.16) Proof. Immediate from item (ii) of Lemma 6.14 . ∎ Lemma 7.12. The vector $\mathbf{1}$ belongs to the kernel of $Q$ if and only if $\forall H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,|\mathcal{H}^{H}|=\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)N+1\,.$ (7.17) Proof. By Corollary 7.4 , the vector $\mathbf{1}$ belongs to the kernel of $Q$ if and only if for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ the following equation is satisfied: $2s_{H}(\mathbf{1})=\frac{2n}{n+1}s(\mathbf{1})\,.$ By Equation (7.12) (and using that $s(\mathbf{1})=N$ ) this condition is equivalent to $|\mathcal{H}^{H}|+N-1=\frac{2n}{n+1}\cdot N$ which rearranges to (7.17). Alternatively, the result also follows from Lemma 6.4 . ∎ We shall need the following elementary result, whose proof we omit. Lemma 7.13. Let $Q:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a real quadratic form. Suppose that $Q\leq 0$ on an open set $U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}$ and let $\mathbf{x}\in U$ . Then the following holds: (i) $Q(\mathbf{x})=0$ if and only if $\mathbf{x}$ is in the kernel of $Q$ ; (ii) if $Q(\mathbf{x})=0$ , then $Q\leq 0$ in all of $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ . The main result of this section is the following extension of [Pan09, Corollary 7.8] to higher dimensions. Theorem 7.14. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an arrangement with $N=|\mathcal{H}|>n+1$ hyperplanes. Suppose that for all $L\in\mathcal{L}$ we have $m_{L}<\codim L\cdot\frac{N}{n+1}\,.$ (7.18) Then $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}\geq\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)N^{2}+N$ (7.19) and equality holds if and only if $\forall H\in\mathcal{H}:\,\,|\mathcal{H}^{H}|=\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)N+1\,.$ (7.20) Moreover, if (7.20) is satisfied, then the quadratic form $Q$ of the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ is negative semidefinite in all of $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ . Proof. Consider the vector $\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}$ . The assumption (7.18) together with Lemma 7.11 imply that $\mathbf{1}\in C^{\circ}$ . By Theorem 6.18 , $Q(\mathbf{1})\leq 0$ . Using Equation (7.15) to evaluate $Q$ at $\mathbf{1}$ , we obtain $\frac{n-1}{n+1}\cdot N^{2}\,+\,N\,-\,\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}\leq 0\,,$ which rearranges to (7.19). Equality holds in (7.19) if and only if $Q(\mathbf{1})=0$ . By Lemma 7.13 (i) this is equivalent to $\mathbf{1}$ being in the kernel of $Q$ . By Lemma 7.12 $\mathbf{1}$ is in the kernel of $Q$ if and only if Equation (7.20) is satisfied. By Lemma 7.13 (ii), if (7.20) is satisfied then $Q$ is negative semidefinite. ∎ We note that Theorem 7.14 does not necessarily hold for arrangements defined over fields other than $\mathbb{C}$ , as the next example illustrates. Example 7.15. Let $p$ be a prime number and let $\mathbb{F}_{p}$ be the finite field of order $p$ . For $n\geq 2$ , let $\mathcal{H}$ be the arrangement consisting of all hyperplanes in $\PG(n,p)=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n+1})$ . The number $N=|\mathcal{H}|$ of hyperplanes is equal to $\left|\big(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\big)\,\big/\,\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}\,\right|=\frac{p^{n+1}-1}{p-1}\,.$ The next two claims show that the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ violates Theorem 7.14. Concretely, Claim 1 proves that (7.18) holds and Claim 2 proves that (7.19) doesn’t. • Claim 1: the vector $\mathbf{1}$ belongs to the stable cone $C^{\circ}$ , i.e., if $L$ is a non-empty and proper linear subspace, then $\frac{m_{L}}{\codim L}<\frac{N}{n+1}\,.$ (7.21) • Claim 2: the sum of multiplicities of codimension two subspaces satisfies $\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-2}}m_{L}<\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)N^{2}+N\,.$ (7.22) Proof of Claim 1. The multiplicity $m_{L}$ of a linear subspace $L$ is the number of hyperplanes in $\PG(n,p)$ that contain it and it is given by $m_{L}=|\PG(c-1,p)|$ where $c$ is the codimension of $L$ . Note that $1\leq c\leq n$ , as $L$ is proper and non-empty. Therefore, inequality (7.21) is equivalent to $\frac{p^{c}-1}{c}<\frac{p^{n+1}-1}{n+1}\,,$ which follows by noticing that the ratio $p^{c}/c$ is strictly increasing with $c$ . Proof of Claim 2. By Lemma 7.10 , Equation (7.22) is equivalent to $Q(\mathbf{1})>0$ . We prove this by showing that $\mathbf{1}$ is an eigenvector of $Q$ with positive eigenvalue. Since every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects the other hyperplanes of $\PG(n,p)$ along the same number $t$ of codimension $2$ subspaces, namely $t=|\PG(n-1,p)|$ , Lemma 6.4 implies that $Q\cdot\mathbf{1}=\lambda\cdot\mathbf{1}$ with $\lambda=(n-1)\cdot N-(n+1)\cdot(t-1)\,.$ Replacing the values for $N=|\PG(n,p)|$ and $t=|\PG(n-1,p)|$ we have $(p-1)\lambda=(n-1)\cdot(p^{n+1}-1)-(n+1)\cdot(p^{n}-p)$ from which is straightforward to check that $\lambda>0$ for all $(n,p)$ with $n\geq 2$ and $p\geq 2$ . Next, we give a name for the complex arrangements that saturate the inequality (7.19). Note that, by Corollary 2.29 , if $\mathcal{H}$ is as in Theorem 7.14 then $\mathcal{H}$ is essential and irreducible. To include product arrangements, we relax (7.18) to a non-strict inequality. Definition 7.16. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be an essential arrangement. We say that $\mathcal{H}$ is Hirzebruch if the following two conditions are satisfied: (H1) for every subspace $L\in\mathcal{L}$ we have $\frac{m_{L}}{\codim L}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{H}|}{n+1}$ (7.23) where $m_{L}$ is the multiplicity of $L$ ; (H2) every hyperplane $H\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects the other hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}\setminus\{H\}$ along $\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)|\mathcal{H}|+1$ (7.24) codimension $2$ subspaces. Example 7.17. The arrangement of $n+1$ coordinate hyperplanes in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is Hirzebruch. If $n=1$ then any finite collection of points $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}$ with $|\mathcal{H}|\geq 2$ is Hirzebruch. However, if $n\geq 2$ the Hirzebruch condition is much more rigid. A line arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ is Hirzebruch if and only if $|\mathcal{H}|=3k$ for some positive integer $k$ and every line in $\mathcal{H}$ intersects the other lines of the arrangement at $k+1$ points, all known examples are listed in [Hir85] . In dimension $3$ we have the following characterization. Lemma 7.18. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ be an essential arrangement of $2k$ planes with $k\geq 2$ such that every plane in $\mathcal{H}$ intersects the others along $k+1$ lines. Then $\mathcal{H}$ is Hirzebruch. Proof. We have to prove that $\mathcal{H}$ satisfies (H1) . This amounts to show two things: (i) for every point $x\in\mathcal{L}^{0}$ we have $m_{x}\leq(3/2)k$ ; (ii) for every line $L\in\mathcal{L}^{1}$ we have $m_{L}\leq k$ . (i) Let $x\in\mathcal{L}^{0}$ . Since $\mathcal{H}$ is essential, we can take a plane $H_{0}$ that does not go through $x$ . Every pair of planes that contain $x$ must meet $H_{0}$ at different lines, hence $m_{x}\leq|\mathcal{H}^{H_{0}}|=k+1.$ Since $k\geq 2$ , we have $k+1\leq k+(k/2)$ and therefore $m_{x}\leq(3/2)k$ . Let $L\in\mathcal{L}^{1}$ and take a plane $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contains $L$ . Since all the planes in $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ cut $H$ along the same line $L$ whereas the elements in $\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{L}$ cut $H$ in at most $|\mathcal{H}|-m_{L}$ lines, we have $k=|\mathcal{H}^{H}|-1\leq|\mathcal{H}|-m_{L}$ from which we get $m_{L}\leq k$ . ∎ Remark 7.19. In Lemma 7.18 we require that $\mathcal{H}$ is essential. If $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ is an arrangement of $4=2k$ planes that intersect at a common point and whose pairwise intersection are all distinct, then every member of $\mathcal{H}$ intersects the others along $3=k+1$ lines but $\mathcal{H}$ is not Hirzebruch. The next example shows that the class of Hirzebruch arrangements is not closed under restriction. Example 7.20. Let $L$ and $L^{\prime}$ be two skew lines in $\mathbb{CP}^{3}$ and let $\mathcal{H}$ be the arrangement of $2k$ planes with $k$ intersecting along $L$ and $k$ intersecting along $L^{\prime}$ . Then $\mathcal{H}$ is a product Hirzebruch arrangement, it is a particular case of Example 7.21. Let $H$ be one of the planes containing $L$ and let $x$ be the intersection point of $H$ and $L^{\prime}$ . The induced arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{H}$ is the near-pencil consisting of $k$ concurrent lines meeting at $x$ together with the extra line $L\subset H$ . The arrangement $\mathcal{H}^{H}$ is not Hirzebruch if $k\geq 3$ . Example 7.21. Suppose that $\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}}$ and $\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{2}}$ are Hirzebruch. Then the product arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+1}$ is Hirzebruch if and only if $\frac{|\mathcal{H}_{1}|}{n_{1}+1}=\frac{|\mathcal{H}_{2}|}{n_{2}+1}\,.$ 7.3 Complex reflection arrangements We recall the basic definitions on complex reflection arrangements, we follow [OT92]. A linear map $f\in U(n+1)$ is a complex reflection if it has finite order and its fixed point set is a hyperplane $H_{f}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . We call $H_{f}$ the reflecting hyperplane of $f$ . A finite subgroup $G\subset U(n+1)$ is a complex reflection group if it is generated by complex reflections. The collection $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of reflecting hyperplanes of $G$ is called the reflection arrangement of $G$ . The group $G$ is irreducible if the only $G$ -invariant subspaces are $\{0\}$ and $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . For the rest of this section we let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a complex reflection arrangement of an irreducible complex reflection group $G\subset U(n+1)$ . Let $N=|\mathcal{H}|$ . Let $\langle v,w\rangle=\sum_{i}v_{i}\bar{w}_{i}$ be the usual Hermitian inner product on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . For each $H\in\mathcal{H}$ choose a unit vector $e_{H}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ orthogonal to the corresponding linear hyperplane. Write $P_{H^{\perp}}$ for the orthogonal projection to $H^{\perp}$ , given by $P_{H^{\perp}}(v)=\langle v,e_{H}\rangle\cdot e_{H}.$ Lemma 7.22 ([OT92, Proposition 6.93]). For every $v\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ we have $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}P_{H^{\perp}}(v)=a_{0}\cdot v\,,$ (7.25) where $a_{0}=N/(n+1)$ . Proof. This is essentially a consequence of Schur’s lemma. The left hand side of Equation (7.25) defines an invariant Hermitian form for the action of $G$ on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . The assumption that the action is irreducible implies that $\sum_{H}P_{H^{\perp}}$ is a scalar multiple of the identity. The value of $a_{0}$ can be calculated by taking traces. ∎ Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the set of non-empty and proper subspaces obtained as intersection members of $\mathcal{H}$ . The multiplicity $m_{L}$ of $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is the number of hyperplanes $H\in\mathcal{H}$ that contain $L$ , i.e., $m_{L}=|\mathcal{H}_{L}|$ where $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is the localization of $\mathcal{H}$ at $L$ . By slight abuse of notation, we also write $L$ for the corresponding linear subspace of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ , and $L^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ for its orthogonal complement. Recall that $\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}\subset\mathcal{L}$ denotes the subset of irreducible subspaces. Lemma 7.23. Suppose that $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . Then, for all $v\in L^{\perp}$ , we have $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{L}}P_{H^{\perp}}(v)=a_{L}\cdot v\,,$ (7.26) where $a_{L}=m_{L}/\codim L$ . Proof. Consider the subgroup $G_{L}\subset G$ made of elements that fix $L$ . By [OT92, Theorem 6.25], the group $G_{L}$ generated by the reflections in $G$ whose reflecting hyperplanes belong to the localized arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ . We identify $L^{\perp}=\mathbb{C}^{p}$ with $p=\codim L$ and $G_{L}\subset U(p)$ , as the group $G_{L}$ acts faithfully on $L^{\perp}$ . The reflecting hyperplanes of $G_{L}$ are $H\cap L^{\perp}$ for $H\in\mathcal{H}_{L}$ . Since, by assumption, the arrangement $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is irreducible, the action of $G_{L}$ on $\mathbb{C}^{p}$ is also irreducible. Equation (7.26) follows by Lemma 7.22 . ∎ Remark 7.24. Lemma 7.23 implies that $\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{L}}P_{H^{\perp}}=a_{L}\cdot P_{L^{\perp}}\,,$ where $P_{L^{\perp}}$ is the orthogonal projection to $L^{\perp}$ . This result is well known in the context of Dunkl connections, see [CHL05, Lemma 2.13 and Example 2.5] . Lemma 7.25. If $L\in\mathcal{L}$ then the following inequality holds: $\frac{m_{L}}{\codim L}<\frac{N}{n+1}\,.$ Proof. Write $a_{L}=m_{L}/\codim L$ and $a_{0}=N/(n+1)$ . We have to show that $a_{L}<a_{0}$ . Suppose first that $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ . Let $S$ be the common intersection of all hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{L}$ . Note that $L^{\perp}\not\subset S$ , otherwise $L^{\perp}$ would be invariant by $G$ . Let $v\in L^{\perp}\setminus S$ with $|v|=1$ . Since $v\notin S$ , there is at least one $H\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{L}$ such that $\langle v,e_{H}\rangle\neq 0$ . Taking the inner product of Equations (7.26) and (7.25) with $v$ we obtain $\displaystyle a_{L}$ $\displaystyle=\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{L}}|\langle v,e_{H}\rangle|^{2}$ $\displaystyle<\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}|\langle v,e_{H}\rangle|^{2}=a_{0}\,.$ To finish the proof, note that if $a_{L}<a_{0}$ for $L\in\mathcal{L}_{\textup{irr}}$ then $a_{L}<a_{0}$ for all $L\in\mathcal{L}$ , c.f. Lemma 2.25 . ∎ Lemma 7.26. For every $H\in\mathcal{H}$ we have $|\mathcal{H}^{H}|=\left(1-\frac{2}{n+1}\right)|\mathcal{H}|+1\,.$ Proof. This is [OT92, Theorem 6.97]. ∎ Proposition 7.27. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the complex reflection arrangement of an irreducible reflection group $G\subset U(n+1)$ . Then $\mathcal{H}$ is Hirzebruch. Proof. Lemma 7.25 implies Item (H1) and Lemma 7.26 implies Item (H2). ∎ Remark 7.28. At the moment, the only examples we know in dimension $n\geq 2$ of irreducible Hirzebruch arrangements stem from Proposition 7.27. For $n=2$ , it is an old question of Hirzebruch [Hir85, §3] whether all Hirzebruch arrangements come from complex reflection groups. It is proved in [Pan18] that all real Hirzebruch line arrangements come from reflection groups. Using our results one can prove the following. Theorem 7.29. Let $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the complex reflection arrangement of an irreducible reflection group $G\subset U(n+1)$ . Then the quadratic form $Q:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathbb{R}$ is negative semidefinite. Proof. Lemma 7.25 implies that Equation (7.18) is satisfied. By Lemma 7.26 , Equation (7.20) holds. By Theorem 7.14 , the quadratic form $Q$ is negative semidefinite. ∎ Remark 7.30. It would be desirable to find a direct proof of Theorem 7.29 , for example, using the Shephard-Todd classification of irreducible complex reflection groups. 7.4 The Hirzebruch quadratic form of a matroid We formulate our results in combinatorial terms, using the language of matroids. We begin with a review of basic definitions, a standard reference for this material is [Oxl11]. 7.4.1 Matroids: basic definitions In a nutshell, matroids are combinatorial structures that mimic finite collections of vectors in a vector space, including information about their linear dependencies (see Example 7.31). Formally, a matroid is a pair $M=(E,\mathcal{B})$ , where $E$ is a finite set and $\mathcal{B}$ is a non-empty collection of subsets $B\subset E$ which satisfy the exchange property: for any $B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}$ and $b_{1}\in B_{1}\setminus B_{2}$ there exists $b_{2}\in B_{2}\setminus B_{1}$ such that $\left(B_{1}\setminus\{b_{1}\}\right)\cup\{b_{2}\}$ is in $\mathcal{B}$ . The elements of $\mathcal{B}$ are called basis of the matroid $M$ . The set $E$ is referred as the ground set of $M$ . Two matroids $M_{1}=(E_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1})$ and $M_{2}=(E_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2})$ are isomorphic if there is a bijection $f:E_{1}\to E_{2}$ such that $B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}$ if and only if $B_{2}=f(B_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{2}$ . It follows from the exchange property that all bases $B\in\mathcal{B}$ have the same number of elements $|B|$ . The rank $r$ of the matroid $M$ is $r=|B|$ for any $B\in\mathcal{B}$ . A subset of the ground set, $I\subset E$ , is independent if it is contained in a basis, i.e., if $I\subset B$ for some $B\in\mathcal{B}$ . In particular, we agree that the empty set is independent. We denote by $\mathcal{I}$ the collection of all independent subsets. The rank of a subset $S\subset E$ is the largest size of an independent set contained in it, $\rk(S)=\max_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\{|I|\,\,|\,\,I\subset S\}\,.$ An element $x$ of the ground set of $M$ is a loop if the singleton $\{x\}$ is not an independent set, equivalently $\rk(x)=0$ . A pair of elements $x,y$ of $M$ are parallel if none is a loop and $\rk(\{x,y\})=1$ . Example 7.31. Suppose that $k$ is a field and let $E=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{N}\}$ be vectors of $V=k^{n+1}$ that span the whole space. Let $\mathcal{B}$ be the collection of all subsets $B\subset E$ that form a basis of the vector space $V$ . Then $M=(E,\mathcal{B})$ is a matroid of rank $r=n+1$ . An element $v_{i}\in E$ is a loop if $v_{i}=0$ . A pair of non-zero vectors $v_{i},v_{j}\in E$ are parallel if one is a scalar multiple of the other. Definition 7.32. If a matroid $M$ is isomorphic to a matroid as in Example 7.31 , we say that $M$ is representable over the field $k$ . A subset $F$ of the ground set of $M$ is a $k$ -flat if $\rk(F)=k$ and $\rk\left(F\cup\{e\}\right)>\rk(F)$ for any $e\in E\setminus F$ . The set of all flats is denoted by $\mathcal{F}$ . The closure $\overline{S}$ of a subset $S\subset E$ is the intersection of all flats containing it, $\overline{S}=\bigcap_{F\in\mathcal{F}\,|\,F\supset S}F\,.$ The intersection of flats is also a flat, so the closure $\overline{S}\in\mathcal{F}$ . The set of all flats of the matroid $M$ equipped with the partial order given by inclusion, $F_{1}\leq F_{2}$ if $F_{1}\subset F_{2}$ , is a poset $(\mathcal{F},\leq)$ . This poset is actually a lattice, meaning that any two elements $F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}$ have a greatest lower bound (their meet $F_{1}\wedge F_{2}$ ) and a least upper bound (their join $F_{1}\vee F_{2}$ ). These are necessarily unique and given by $F_{1}\wedge F_{2}=F_{1}\cap F_{2},\hskip 5.69054ptF_{1}\vee F_{2}=\overline{F_{1}\cup F_{2}}\,.$ The matroid $M$ is simple if it has no loops and no pairs of parallel points. If $M$ is simple then the poset of flats $(\mathcal{F},\leq)$ is a geometric lattice, meaning that it is semimodular and atomic. The semimodular property is: $\rk(F_{1})+\rk(F_{2})\geq\rk(F_{1}\vee F_{2})+\rk(F_{1}\wedge F_{2})\,.$ Atomic means that every flat $F\in\mathcal{F}$ is a join of singletons $\{x\}$ . The elements $x\in M$ of the ground set (or the singletons $\{x\}\in\mathcal{F}$ ) are called atoms. Conversely, every geometric lattice is the poset of flats of a simple matroid [Sta07, Theorem 3.8]. Example 7.33. Let $\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}\}$ be a finite collection of pairwise distinct hyperplanes in $\mathbb{P}_{k}^{n}=\mathbb{P}(k^{n+1})$ with empty common intersection. For each $H_{i}$ choose a defining linear equation $h_{i}\in(k^{n+1})^{*}$ . The collection of vectors $E=\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}\}$ defines, as in Example 7.31 , a matroid $M=(E,\mathcal{B})$ . The elements of $\mathcal{B}$ correspond to $(n+1)$ -tuples of hyperplanes with empty common intersection. The fact that each $h_{i}\neq 0$ and that every pair of hyperplanes $H_{i}$ and $H_{j}$ with $i\neq j$ satisfy $H_{i}\neq H_{j}$ , implies that $M$ is simple. The correspondence $L\mapsto\mathcal{H}_{L}$ (where $L$ is a linear subspace and $\mathcal{H}_{L}$ is the set of hyperplanes in $\mathcal{H}$ that contain it) defines an isomorphism between the intersection poset $\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ of $\mathcal{H}$ equipped with the order by reverse inclusion and the poset of flats $\mathcal{F}$ equipped with the order by inclusion. The whole space $\mathbb{P}_{k}^{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ (corresponding to the intersection of an empty collection of hyperplanes) is mapped to the minimal element $\hat{0}=\overline{\emptyset}$ of $\mathcal{F}$ , while $\emptyset\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}$ (the centre of the arrangement) corresponds to the unique maximal flat $\hat{1}$ (the join of all $h_{i}$ ). Remark 7.34. If $M$ is simple and representable over the field $k$ , then $M$ must be isomorphic to a matroid as in Example 7.33 . In this case, we say that $M$ is the matroid associated to the hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}$ . Remark 7.35. If the matroid $M$ is associated to the arrangement $\mathcal{H}$ , then $\mathcal{H}$ is assumed to be essential. 7.4.2 Quadratic form for matroids Let $M$ be a simple matroid of rank $n+1$ on the ground set $[N]=\{1,\ldots,N\}$ . Let $F$ be a rank two flat. We say that $F$ is irreducible if $|F|\geq 3$ . Otherwise, if $|F|=2$ then $F$ is reducible. For $i\in[N]$ let $\sigma_{i}$ be the number of irreducible rank $2$ flats that contain $i$ . Definition 7.36. The Hirzebruch quadratic form of $M$ is the function $Q_{M}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}$ given by $Q_{M}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}^{t}\cdot Q\cdot\mathbf{x}\,,$ where $Q$ is the real symmetric $N\times N$ matrix with integer entries $Q_{ij}=\begin{cases}-(n+1)\sigma_{i}+2n&\text{ if }i=j\,,\\ -2&\text{ if }|i\vee j|=2\,,\\ n-1&\text{ if }|i\vee j|\geq 3\,.\end{cases}$ (7.27) By slight abuse of notation, we shall also write $Q$ for the quadratic form $Q_{M}$ as well as for the symmetric matrix. Remark 7.37. If $M$ is the matroid associated to a hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ then Equation (7.27) agrees with Equation (6.5). Remark 7.38 (c.f. Lemma 6.4). The sum of the $i$ -th column (or $i$ -th row) of the matrix $Q$ is given by $\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{ij}=(n-1)\cdot N-(n+1)\cdot(t_{i}-1)$ (7.28) where $t_{i}$ is the number of rank $2$ flats that contain $i$ . Let $P\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}$ be the matroid polytope of $M$ , i.e., $P$ is the convex hull of indicator functions of bases of $M$ . Our results give us the following. Theorem 7.39. Suppose that the matroid $M$ is representable over $\mathbb{C}$ . Then the Hirzebruch quadratic form of $M$ is non-positive on the cone over the matroid polytope, i.e., $Q(\mathbf{x})\,\leq\,0\,\,\textup{ for all }\,\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times P\,.$ (7.29) Proof. This is Theorem 6.29 for the essential arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ associated to $M$ . ∎ 7.4.3 Examples The advantages of presenting our results within the broader context of matroids are twofold. On the one hand, Theorem 7.39 brings in a potential obstruction for representability of a matroid over the complex numbers. On the other hand, it rises the question for which classes of matroids the statement of Theorem 7.39 holds true. The next two examples illustrate this. Example 7.40. Let $p$ be a prime number and let $\mathbb{F}_{p}$ be the finite field of order $p$ . For $n\geq 2$ , let $M$ be the matroid associated to the hyperplane arrangement consisting of all hyperplanes in $\PG(n,p)=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n+1})$ . As Example 7.15 shows, $\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times P$ but $Q(\mathbf{1})>0$ . Therefore, Theorem 7.39 does not hold for $M$ . As a result, $M$ is not realizable over the complex numbers. Example 7.41. The Non-Pappus matroid $M$ is the matroid of rank $3$ on the set of nine elements $[9]=\{1,\ldots,9\}$ . This matroid has $8$ circuits (minimal dependent sets) shown in Figure 3 as lines connecting triplets of dependant points. The number of bases is equal to $\binom{9}{3}-8=76\,,$ as any $3$ points not connected by a line form a basis. $1$ $2$ $3$ $7$ $8$ $9$ $4$ $5$ $6$ Figure 3: The Non-Pappus matroid. We see that $|i\vee j|=3$ if $i$ and $j$ are joined by a line and $|i\vee j|=2$ otherwise. Also, $\sigma_{i}=\begin{cases}3&\text{ if }1\leq i\leq 6\,,\\ 2&\text{ if }i=7,8,9\,.\end{cases}$ Therefore, the matrix $Q$ of the Hirzebruch quadratic form of $M$ is $\begin{pmatrix}-5&1&1&-2&1&1&1&1&-2\\ 1&-5&1&1&-2&1&1&-2&1\\ 1&1&-5&1&1&-2&-2&1&1\\ -2&1&1&-5&1&1&1&1&-2\\ 1&-2&1&1&-5&1&1&-2&1\\ 1&1&-2&1&1&-5&-2&1&1\\ 1&1&-2&1&1&-2&-2&-2&-2\\ 1&-2&1&1&-2&1&-2&-2&-2\\ -2&1&1&-2&1&1&-2&-2&-2\end{pmatrix}\,.$ The matroid $M$ is not representable over any field. However, the statement of Theorem 7.39 holds for $M$ . Indeed, it can be checked with computer55 5 See https://github.com/MdeBorbon/nonpappus that the matrix $P=-Q$ is copositive, meaning that $\mathbf{x}^{t}\cdot P\cdot\mathbf{x}\geq 0$ for any vector $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{9}$ with components $x_{i}\geq 0$ . 7.4.4 Pseudoline arrangements and symplectic BMY. It would be very interesting to extend Theorem 7.39 to some large classes of non-realizable matroids. In this section we speculate about one such possibility. Recall that a pseudoline arrangement is a collection of circles smoothly embedded into $\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ such that each circle is isotopic to a projective line, any two circles intersect in exactly one point, and their common intersection is empty (see [BLVS+99]). Just as with line arrangements, one can associate a matroid to any pseudoline arrangement. Note that the non-Pappus matroid can be obtained in this manner: take a collection of $9$ straight lines in $\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ realizing the Pappus configuration and slightly perturb three lines in the neighbourhood of a triple point. In the spirit of Arnold’s topologocial economy principle66 6 This principle reads as follows: “If you have a geometrical or topological phenomenon, which you can realize by algebraic objects, then the simplest algebraic realizations are topologically as simple as possible”. [Arn99], we would like to state the following provocative (and quite possibly over-optimistic) conjecture. Conjecture 7.42. Let $\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ be an essential pseudoline arrangement and $M$ be its matroid. Then the Hirzebruch quadratic form $Q$ of $M$ is non-positive on the semistable cone of $M$ , and moreover, whenever $Q$ vanishes in its interior, $M$ is realizable over $\mathbb{R}$ and the pseudoline arrangement $\mathcal{L}$ is stretchable. Recall that there is a one-to-one correspondence between (equivalence classes of) pseudoline arrangements and (reorientation classes of) simple rank 3 oriented matroids [BLVS+99, Section 6]. There exists a complete classification of pseudoline arrangements with up to $11$ pseudolines. Moreover, in case of simplicial pseudoline arrangements (that partition $\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ into triangles), the classification exists for up to $27$ pseudolines [Cun12]. This is a promising class of arrangements for finding counter-examples to Conjecture 7.42, as simplicial arrangements seem to be extremal for the Hirzebruch quadratic form77 7 It follows from [Pan09, Pan18] that any essential real arrangement for which the Hirzebruch quadratic form vanishes in the stable cone is simplicial.. We don’t have much evidence for Conjecture 7.42 except that it holds for usual line arrangements and for the non-Pappus arrangement. However, we have a motivation coming from symplectic geometry. Recall, that to every compact $4$ -dimensional manifold $(X,\omega)$ one can associate two Chern numbers $c_{1}^{2}(X)$ and $c_{2}(X)=e(X)$ . Using Seiberg-Witten theory, one can also define the Kodaira dimension of $X$ , which agrees with the standard definition in the case of Kähler surfaces. There exists a folklore conjecture (that we learned from Ivan Smith in 2010), that symplectic $4$ -manifolds of general type satisfy Bogomolov-Myaoka-Yau inequality as well. In particular, no one has been able so far to construct a symplectic $4$ -manifold of general type with $c_{1}(X)^{2}>3c_{2}(X)$ . A counterexample to Conjecture 7.42 would be interesting, as it could be interpreted as a failure of a logarithmic version of the symplectic BMY inequality. Indeed, any pseudoline arrangement $\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ can be extended to an arrangement of symplectic spheres in $\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ disjoint in $\mathbb{CP}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{RP}^{2}$ . Such extensions are constructed in [RS19][Theorem 1.4] (we learned this fact from Stepan Orevkov in 2011). Going further, following Hirzebruch, one can then consider a (symplectic) blow up of $\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ in points of $\mathcal{L}$ of multiplicity $\geq 3$ and then take an appropriate ramified cover in a hope of being lucky enough to get a counter-example to symplectic BMY88 8 This is also a suggestion hinted upon in [RS19].. In his seminal paper [Hir83], using Kummer covers of $\mathbb{CP}^{2}$ Hirzebruch was able to construct three algebraic surfaces with $c_{1}^{2}=3c_{2}$ , each associated to one complex reflection group. Appendix A Auxiliary Results Section A.1 provides self contained proofs on basic linear algebra related to filtrations of vector spaces, adapted basis, and nested sets. These results are used in the proof of the locally abelian property of our parabolic bundle, Theorem 4.29. Sections A.2 , A.3, and A.4 contain results on exterior algebra, saturated subsheaves, and distributions on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ that are used in the proof of the stability Theorem 5.1. A.1 Filtrations, adapted basis, and nested sets The key results proved in this section are: • Lemma A.15 , which characterizes splittings of tuples of filtrations in terms of adapted bases; • Corollary A.30 , which shows that nested sets have adapted bases. A.1.1 Filtrations of vector spaces Let $V$ be a finite dimensional vector space. Definition A.1. A filtration $\mathcal{F}=\{F_{a}\,|\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}$ of $V$ is a family of vector subspaces $F_{a}\subset V$ parametrized by $a\in\mathbb{R}$ satisfying the following conditions. (i) Increasing: $F_{a}\subset F_{a^{\prime}}$ if $a<a^{\prime}$ . (ii) Semi-continuity: for every $a$ there is $\epsilon>0$ such that $F_{a+\epsilon}=F_{a}$ . (iii) Normalization: $F_{a}=\{0\}$ for $a<0$ and $F_{a}=V$ for $a\geq 1$ . The increasing and semi-continuity properties are equivalent to $\forall a:\,\,F_{a}=\bigcap_{a^{\prime}>a}F_{a^{\prime}}\,.$ Given a filtration $\mathcal{F}=\{F_{a}\}$ of $V$ , we write $F_{<a}=\bigcup_{a^{\prime}<a}F_{a^{\prime}}\,.$ The increasing property implies that $F_{<a}$ is a vector subspace of $F_{a}$ . Definition A.2. The graded components of $\mathcal{F}$ are the quotient vector spaces $\Gr_{a}=F_{a}\,\big/\,F_{<a}\,.$ Definition A.3. We say that $a\in\mathbb{R}$ is a weight of $\mathcal{F}$ if $\Gr_{a}\neq 0$ . The set of all weights of $\mathcal{F}$ is denoted by $\wt(\mathcal{F})=\{a\,|\,\Gr_{a}\neq 0\}\,.$ The normalization condition, together with the fact that $V$ is finite dimensional, imply that $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ is a finite subset of $[0,1]$ . If we write $\wt(\mathcal{F})=\{a^{\mathcal{F}}_{0},\ldots,a^{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\}\,,$ then $\mathcal{F}$ determines a strictly increasing flag of vector subspaces $0\subsetneq F_{a^{\mathcal{F}}_{0}}\subsetneq F_{a^{\mathcal{F}}_{1}}\subsetneq\ldots\subsetneq F_{a^{\mathcal{F}}_{p}}=V\,.$ Conversely, $\mathcal{F}$ is determined by this flag and the weights $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ via $F_{a}=\begin{cases}\{0\}\,&\textup{ if }\,a<a^{\mathcal{F}}_{0}\,,\\ F_{a^{\mathcal{F}}_{i}}\,&\textup{ if }\,a^{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\leq a<a^{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1}\,,\\ V\,&\textup{ if }\,a\geq a^{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\,.\end{cases}$ A.1.2 Tuples of filtrations and splittings Suppose that $\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k})$ is a tuple of $k$ distinct filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{i}=\{F^{i}_{a}\}$ of $V$ . Notation A.4. For $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , let $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ be the vector subspace $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}F^{i}_{a_{i}}\,.$ (A.1) Given $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})$ and $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{k}^{\prime})$ in $\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , we say that $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}$ if $a^{\prime}_{i}\leq a_{i}$ for all $1\leq i\leq k$ . This equips $\mathbb{R}^{k}$ with a partial order such that $F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\subset F_{\mathbf{a}}$ if $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}$ . We write $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}$ if $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{a}$ . Note that $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=F^{1}_{<a_{1}}\cap F^{2}_{a_{2}}\ldots\cap F^{k}_{a_{k}}+\ldots+F^{1}_{a_{1}}\cap F^{2}_{a_{2}}\ldots\cap F^{k}_{<a_{k}}$ (A.2) is a linear subspace of $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Definition A.5. We say that $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ is a weight of $\mathcal{F}$ , if $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\,\subsetneq\,F_{\mathbf{a}}\,.$ The set of all weights of $\mathcal{F}$ is denoted by $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ . Lemma A.6. The set $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ is contained in $\wt(\mathcal{F}^{1})\times\ldots\times\wt(\mathcal{F}^{k})$ . In particular, $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ is a finite subset of $[0,1]^{k}$ . Proof. If $a_{i}\notin\wt(\mathcal{F}^{i})$ then $F^{i}_{<a_{i}}=F^{i}_{a_{i}}$ , so $F^{1}_{a_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap F^{i}_{<a_{i}}\cap\ldots\cap F^{k}_{a_{k}}=F_{\mathbf{a}}\,.$ It follows from this, together with Equation (A.2), that if $\mathbf{a}\notin\wt(\mathcal{F}^{1})\times\ldots\times\wt(\mathcal{F}^{k})$ , then $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=F_{\mathbf{a}}$ ; hence $\mathbf{a}\notin\wt(\mathcal{F})$ . ∎ The main notion we want to introduce is that of a splitting of a tuple of filtrations. Before doing so, we recall the following standard definition. Definition A.7. Let $U_{i}$ be linear subspaces of $V$ indexed by $i\in I$ and let $U=\sum_{i}U_{i}$ be their sum, i.e., $U$ is the smallest linear subspace of $V$ (with respect to the partial order given by inclusion) that contains all $U_{i}$ . We say that the sum $U=\sum_{i}U_{i}$ is direct and write $U=\oplus_{i}U_{i}$ if any of the following equivalent conditions holds. (i) $U_{i}\cap\sum_{j\neq i}U_{j}=\{0\}$ for all $i\in I$ . (ii) For every $u\in U$ there are unique vectors $u_{i}\in U_{i}$ such that $u=\sum_{i}u_{i}$ . (iii) $\dim U=\sum_{i}\dim U_{i}$ . (iv) There is a basis $B$ of $U$ such that $B_{i}=B\cap U_{i}$ is a basis of $U_{i}$ , the sets $B_{i}$ are pairwise disjoint, and $B=\cup_{i}B_{i}$ . The main definition of this section is the next. Definition A.8. A splitting of $\mathcal{F}$ is a family of linear subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}\subset V$ indexed by $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ satisfying the following properties. (i) The subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ are zero except for a finite number of $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , and they form a direct sum decomposition $V=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}U_{\mathbf{a}}\,.$ (A.3) (ii) For any $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , we have $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\,.$ (A.4) The filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k}$ are compatible if $\mathcal{F}$ admits a splitting. Remark A.9. Item (i) follows from (ii) by taking $\mathbf{a}=(1,\ldots,1)$ . For the sake of clarity, we keep item (i) as part of Definition A.8 . Example A.10. If $k=1$ and $\mathcal{F}=\{F_{a}\,|\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}$ , then we can construct a splitting of $\mathcal{F}$ as follows. Set $U_{a}=\{0\}$ if $a\notin\wt(\mathcal{F})$ and, for every $a\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ choose a linear subspace $U_{a}\subset F_{a}$ such that $F_{a}=F_{<a}\oplus U_{a}$ . It is then easy to verify that $\{U_{a}\,|\,a\in\mathbb{R}\}$ satisfies (A.3) and (A.4) . Note that the subspaces $U_{a}$ are isomorphic to the graded components of $\Gr_{a}=F_{a}\,/\,F_{<a}$ of $\mathcal{F}$ . Lemma A.11. Suppose that $\{U_{\mathbf{a}}\,|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}$ is a splitting of $\mathcal{F}$ , then the following holds. (i) $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ is non-zero if and only if $\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ . (ii) For any $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , we have $U_{\mathbf{a}}\cong\dfrac{F_{\mathbf{a}}}{\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}}\,.$ (A.5) (iii) If $\{\widetilde{U}_{\mathbf{a}}\}$ is another splitting of $\mathcal{F}$ then there is a linear isomorphism $\Phi\in GL(V)$ with $\Phi(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}$ such that $\Phi(U_{\mathbf{a}})=\widetilde{U}_{\mathbf{a}}$ for all $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ . Proof. We begin by proving item (ii). For this, we notice that Equation (A.4) implies $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\,.$ Therefore, $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\right)\oplus U_{\mathbf{a}}$ (A.6) and item (ii) follows. By Definition A.5 , (ii) implies (i). To show (iii), we note that by (ii), for each $\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ , we can choose a linear isomorphism $\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}:U_{\mathbf{a}}\to\widetilde{U}_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Since $V=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})}U_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})}\widetilde{U}_{\mathbf{a}}\,,$ the maps $\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}$ define a linear isomorphism $\Phi\in GL(V)$ with $\Phi(U_{\mathbf{a}})=\widetilde{U}_{\mathbf{a}}$ . If follows from Equation (A.4) , that $\Phi$ preserves each of the subspaces $F^{i}_{a}$ of the filtration $\mathcal{F}^{i}$ for all $i$ , so $\Phi(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}$ . ∎ Remark A.12. For $\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ the sum $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ is a proper linear subspace of $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Therefore, for any $\mathcal{F}$ we can find non-zero subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}\subset F_{\mathbf{a}}$ for $\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ such that (A.6) holds. By construction, $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ . The subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ split $\mathcal{F}$ if and only if they form a direct sum. However, in general, the sum $\sum U_{\mathbf{a}}$ will not be direct, as the next example shows. Example A.13. Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be $k$ distinct lines in $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ through the origin with $k\geq 3$ . Let $\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\in(0,1)$ and consider the filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k}$ given by $F^{i}_{a}=\begin{cases}\{0\}&\text{ if }a<0\,,\\ L_{i}&\text{ if }0\leq a<\lambda_{i}\,,\\ \mathbb{C}^{2}&\text{ if }a\geq\lambda_{i}\,.\end{cases}$ For $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ , the intersection $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ is zero if $a_{i}<\lambda_{i}$ and $a_{j}<\lambda_{j}$ for a pair of distinct indices $i,j$ . Hence, the weights of $\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k})$ are $\wt(\mathcal{F})=\{(0,\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{k}),\,(\lambda_{1},0,\ldots,\lambda_{k}),\ldots,\,(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,0)\}\,.$ If $\mathbf{a}\in\wt(\mathcal{F})$ then $F_{\mathbf{a}}=L_{i}$ , where $i$ is the unique component of $\mathbf{a}$ with $a_{i}=0$ , and $\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\lneq\mathbf{a}}F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=0$ . Therefore, if Equation (A.6) is satisfied, we must take $U_{\mathbf{a}}=L_{i}$ . The subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ do not make a direct sum. Therefore, the filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k}$ are not compatible. A.1.3 Adapted bases Let $B=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}$ be a basis of $V$ and let $L\subset V$ be a linear subspace. Definition A.14. We say that $B$ is adapted to $L$ if $B\cap L$ is a basis of $L$ . If $\mathcal{S}$ is a collection of linear subspaces of $V$ , we say that $B$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}$ if $B$ is adapted to $L$ for every $L\in\mathcal{S}$ . Let $\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k})$ be a tuple of filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{i}=\{F^{i}_{a}\}$ as in Section A.1.2. A basis $B$ of $V$ is adapted to $\mathcal{F}$ , if any of the following equivalent conditions holds: • $B$ is adapted to $F^{i}_{a}$ for every $1\leq i\leq k$ and $a\in\mathbb{R}$ ; • $B$ is adapted to $F_{\mathbf{a}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}F^{i}_{a_{i}}$ for every $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ . The main result that we are after is the next. Lemma A.15. The following two conditions are equivalent. (i) The filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k}$ are compatible. (ii) There is a basis $B$ of $V$ that is adapted to $\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k})$ . Proof. (i) $\implies$ (ii). Suppose that $\{U_{\mathbf{a}}\,|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}$ is a splitting of $\mathcal{F}$ . For each $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$ such that $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ is non-zero, choose a basis $B_{\mathbf{a}}$ of $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Then $B=\bigcup_{\mathbf{a}}B_{\mathbf{a}}$ is adapted to $\mathcal{F}$ . (ii) $\implies$ (i). Suppose that $B$ is a basis of $V$ adapted to $\mathcal{F}$ . We want to define subspaces $U_{\mathbf{a}}\subset V$ that split $\mathcal{F}$ . To do this, for each $e\in B$ we consider the subspace $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ with smallest $\mathbf{a}$ that contains $e$ . In detail, note that if both $F_{\mathbf{a}}$ and $F_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}$ contain $e$ , then $F_{\min\{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\}}$ also does, where $\min\{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\}$ is the vector with components $\min\{a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\}$ . Thus, we have a map $\Phi:B\to\mathbb{R}^{k}$ given by $\Phi(e)=\min\{\mathbf{a}\,|\,e\in F_{\mathbf{a}}\}\,.$ It is easy to see that the image of $\Phi$ is $\wt(\mathcal{F})$ , the weights of $\mathcal{F}$ . The preimages $\Phi^{-1}(\mathbf{a})$ partition $B$ into disjoint sets. We let $U_{\mathbf{a}}$ be the span of the vectors in $\Phi^{-1}(\mathbf{a})$ , $U_{\mathbf{a}}=\spn\{e\in B\,|\,\Phi(e)=\mathbf{a}\}\,.$ We want to show that (A.4) holds. Clearly, if $\Phi(e)=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}$ and $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}$ , then $e\in F_{\mathbf{a}}$ . Conversely, if $e\in B\cap F_{\mathbf{a}}$ and $\Phi(e)=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}$ , then $\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}$ . Since $B$ is adapted to $\mathcal{F}$ , $\displaystyle F_{\mathbf{a}}$ $\displaystyle=\spn\{e\,|\,e\in B\cap F_{\mathbf{a}}\}$ $\displaystyle=\spn\{e\,|\,\Phi(e)\leq\mathbf{a}\}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\leq\mathbf{a}}U_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\,.$ This shows that $\{U_{\mathbf{a}}\,|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\}$ splits $\mathcal{F}$ . ∎ Example A.16. If $k=2$ then any two filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1}$ and $\mathcal{F}^{2}$ on $V$ are compatible, see [Xu24, Lemma 3.5]. However, if $k\geq 3$ then generic $k$ -tuples of filtrations $\mathcal{F}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{k}$ are not compatible. For example if $k=3$ , $n=2$ and $\mathcal{F}^{i}$ define $3$ distinct lines in $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ then it is clear that there is no basis of $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ adapted to $\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathcal{F}^{2},\mathcal{F}^{3}$ ; c.f. Example A.13. A.1.4 Nested sets of linear subspaces Let $V$ be a finite dimensional vector space of dimension $n$ and let $V^{*}$ be its dual. We use the partial order on linear subspaces of $V^{*}$ given by inclusion, i.e., $U_{1}\leq U_{2}$ if $U_{1}\subset U_{2}$ . Definition A.17. A $*$ -nested set is a finite set $\mathcal{N}$ of non-zero linear subspaces $N\subset V^{*}$ such that, for any collection of pairwise non comparable elements $N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}\in\mathcal{N}$ (i.e. $N_{i}\not\subset N_{j}$ for $i\neq j$ ), the following holds: (i) their sum $S=\sum_{i}N_{i}$ is direct, i.e., $S=\oplus_{i}N_{i}$ ; (ii) $S\notin\mathcal{N}$ . To emphasize the ambient space, we say that $\mathcal{N}$ is a $*$ -nested set in $V^{*}$ . Remark A.18. One can also omit condition (ii) to get a sensible definition, but we will need it for our purposes later on, see also Remark A.31 . The next lemma follows immediately from Definition A.17 , we omit its proof. Lemma A.19. If $\mathcal{N}$ is a $*$ -nested set and $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ is a subset of $\mathcal{N}$ , then $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ is also a $*$ -nested set. Moreover, if all elements of $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ are contained in a subspace $W\subset V^{*}$ , then $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ is a $*$ -nested set in $W$ . Recall from Definition A.14 that, if $\mathcal{N}$ is a collection of linear subspaces of $V^{*}$ , a basis of $V^{*}$ is adapted to $\mathcal{N}$ , if every element of $\mathcal{N}$ is spanned by the basis vectors contained in it. Lemma A.20. Every $*$ -nested set has an adapted basis. Proof. We proceed by induction on the dimension $n$ of the ambient space $V^{*}$ . The statement is trivially true if $n=1$ . Let $\mathcal{N}$ be a $*$ -nested set in $V^{*}$ with $n=\dim V^{*}$ and assume that the statement holds true for dimensions $<n$ . We want to show that there is a basis $B^{*}$ of $V^{*}$ adapted to $\mathcal{N}$ . We can assume that $V^{*}\notin\mathcal{N}$ ; otherwise take $\mathcal{N}_{0}=\mathcal{N}\setminus\{V^{*}\}$ and note that if a basis of $V^{*}$ is adapted to $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ then it is also adapted to $\mathcal{N}$ . Let $N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}$ be the maximal elements of $\mathcal{N}$ with respect to the partial order given by inclusion. Since $V^{*}\notin\mathcal{N}$ , we have $\dim N_{i}<n$ for all $i$ . The sets $N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}$ are pairwise non comparable, thus they form a direct sum $\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}N_{i}\subset V^{*}\,.$ For $1\leq i\leq k$ , let $\mathcal{N}_{i}=\{N\in\mathcal{N}\,|\,N\subset N_{i}\}\,.$ By construction, every $N\in\mathcal{N}$ is contained in a subspace $N_{i}$ for some $i$ . On the other hand, if a subspace $N$ is contained in both $N_{i}$ and $N_{j}$ with $i\neq j$ , then $N=\{0\}$ . But, by Definition A.17, $\{0\}\notin\mathcal{N}$ . Thus, we have a disjoint union $\mathcal{N}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}_{i}\,.$ By Lemma A.19 , each $\mathcal{N}_{i}$ is a $*$ -nested set in $N_{i}$ . By induction hypothesis, since $\dim N_{i}<n$ , we can chose a basis $B^{*}_{i}$ of $N_{i}$ adapted to $\mathcal{N}_{i}$ . Their union $\cup_{i}B^{*}_{i}$ , extended to a basis of $V^{*}$ if necessary, is a basis $B^{*}$ of $V^{*}$ adapted to $\mathcal{N}$ . ∎ Next, we recall the notion of transversal intersection of linear subspaces. Definition A.21. Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be linear subspaces of $V$ . We say that $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ intersect transversely, or that the their common intersection $M=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}$ is transversal, if $\codim M=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\codim L_{i}\,.$ (A.7) Example A.22. The most familiar case is when $k=2$ . In this case, the subspaces $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ are transversal if and only if $L_{1}+L_{2}=V$ . This follows from the identity $\dim(L_{1}+L_{2})=\dim L_{1}+\dim L_{2}-\dim L_{1}\cap L_{2}$ together with Equation (A.7). Notation A.23. Recall that if $L\subset V$ is a linear subspace, then its annihilator $L^{\perp}$ is the linear subspace of $V^{*}$ made of all linear functions on $V$ that vanish on $L$ . The map $L\mapsto L^{\perp}$ defines an inclusion reversing correspondence between linear subspaces of $V$ and $V^{*}$ , with $\dim L^{\perp}=\codim L$ , and $(L^{\perp})^{\perp}=L$ under the natural identification $V^{**}=V$ . Lemma A.24. Let $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ be linear subspaces of $V$ . Then their common intersection $M=\cap_{i}L_{i}$ is transversal if and only if $M^{\perp}=\oplus_{i}L_{i}^{\perp}$ . Proof. This follows from the identity $\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}\right)^{\perp}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}^{\perp}$ together with Equation (A.7) and the fact that a sum of linear subspaces $U=\sum_{i}U_{i}$ is direct if and only if $\dim U=\sum_{i}\dim U_{i}$ . ∎ Remark A.25. Assume $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ intersect transversely. Then by Lemma A.24 we can choose a basis in $M^{\perp}$ as a union of bases in $L_{i}^{\perp}$ . It follows that $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ intersect transversely if and only if there are linear coordinates $x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}$ on $V$ and pairwise disjoint subsets $I_{i}\subset[n]$ for $1\leq i\leq k$ such that $L_{i}=\{x_{j}=0\,|\,j\in I_{i}\}\,.$ In particular, if $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}$ intersect transversely, then any subset of them also does. Having recalled the necessary background on transversal intersections, we consider the corresponding dual notion to $*$ -nested sets. Definition A.26. A nested set in $V$ is a finite set $\mathcal{S}$ of proper linear subspaces $L\subsetneq V$ such that, for any collection $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ of pairwise non comparable elements, the following holds: (i) their intersection $M=\cap_{i}L_{i}$ is transversal; (ii) $M\notin\mathcal{S}$ . Next, we relate Definitions A.17 and A.26 . If $\mathcal{S}$ is a collection of linear subspaces $L\subset V$ , then $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ denotes the corresponding collection of subspaces of $V^{*}$ which are annihilators of elements in $\mathcal{S}$ , $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}=\{L^{\perp}\,|\,L\in\mathcal{S}\}\,.$ With this notation, the correspondence between nested and $*$ -nested sets can be stated as follows. Lemma A.27. $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set in $V$ if and only if $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ is a $*$ -nested set in $V^{*}$ . Proof. Suppose that $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set in $V$ and let $\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ . We want to show that $\mathcal{N}$ is a $*$ -nested set in $V^{*}$ . First of all, note that the elements $N\in\mathcal{N}$ are non-zero subspaces of $V^{*}$ . Indeed, $N=L^{\perp}$ with $L\in\mathcal{S}$ , since the elements of $\mathcal{S}$ are proper subspaces $L\subsetneq V$ , their annihilators $L^{\perp}$ are non-zero. In order to verify Definition A.17, let $N_{1},\ldots,N_{k}\in\mathcal{N}$ be pairwise non comparable. We want to show: (i) we have a direct sum $\oplus_{i}N_{i}$ and (ii) $\oplus_{i}N_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}$ . (i) For each $1\leq i\leq k$ , we can write $N_{i}=L_{i}^{\perp}$ with $L_{i}\in\mathcal{S}$ . The corresponding elements $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ must also be pairwise non comparable. By item (i) of Definition A.26 , the intersection $M=\cap_{i}L_{i}$ is transversal. By Lemma A.24 , we have a direct sum $M^{\perp}=\oplus_{i}N_{i}$ ; which proves (i). (ii) If $\oplus_{i}N_{i}\in\mathcal{N}$ then $M=(\oplus_{i}N_{i})^{\perp}\in\mathcal{S}$ . However, by item (ii) of Definition A.26 , $M\notin\mathcal{S}$ . Therefore, $\oplus_{i}N_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}$ ; which proves (ii). The proof that if $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ is $*$ -nested then $\mathcal{S}$ is nested is similar and we omit it. ∎ Notation A.28. Let $B=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}$ be a basis of $V$ . The dual basis $B^{*}=\{e^{*}_{1},\ldots,e^{*}_{n}\}$ is the basis of $V^{*}$ defined by $e^{*}_{i}(e_{i})=1$ and $e^{*}_{i}(e_{j})=0$ for $j\neq i$ . Lemma A.29. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a set of linear subspaces of $V$ . A basis $B=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}$ of $V$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}$ if and only if the dual basis $B^{*}=\{e^{*}_{1},\ldots,e^{*}_{n}\}$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ . Proof. This follows from the fact that if $L\subset V$ is the span of $\{e_{i}\,|\,i\in I\}$ for a subset $I\subset[n]$ , then $L^{\perp}$ is the span of $\{e^{*}_{j}\,|\,j\in[n]\setminus I\}$ . ∎ Putting all things together, we arrive at the main result of this section. Corollary A.30. If $\mathcal{S}$ if a nested set in $V$ , then there is a basis of $V$ adapted to $\mathcal{S}$ . Proof. By Lemma A.27 , $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ is $*$ -nested. By Lemma A.20 , there is a basis $B^{*}$ of $V^{*}$ that is adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\perp}$ . By Lemma A.29 , the dual basis $B$ of $V$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}$ . ∎ Remark A.31. In this section we haven’t made any use of item (ii) in the definition of nested and $*$ -nested sets. In particular, Corollary A.30 also holds for families of subspaces which don’t satisfy this extra condition; but we won’t need to use this. On the other hand, using item (ii) in Definition A.26 , it is not hard to show that if $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set in $V$ , then $|\mathcal{S}|\leq\dim V$ . If item (ii) is not satisfied then this is no longer true. For example, if $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ are two distinct lines in $V=\mathbb{C}^{2}$ going through the origin, then the set $\mathcal{S}=\{\{0\},L_{1},L_{2}\}$ satisfies item (i) of Definition A.26 but it does not satisfy item (ii); and $3=|\mathcal{S}|>\dim V=2$ . A.1.5 Nested sets of projective subspaces Definition A.32. A nested set in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is a finite set $\mathcal{S}$ of non-empty and proper projective subspaces $L\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ such that, for any collection $L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}\in\mathcal{S}$ of pairwise non comparable elements (i.e. $L_{i}\not\subset L_{j}$ for $i\neq j$ ), the following holds: (i) their common intersection $M=\cap_{i}L_{i}$ is non-empty and transversal, i.e., $\codim M=\sum_{i}\codim L_{i}\,;$ (ii) $M\notin\mathcal{S}$ . Remark A.33. If $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , then $\bigcap_{L\in\mathcal{S}}L$ is a non-empty subspace of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Let $\pi:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the quotient projection. If $L\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is a projective subspace, then we write $L^{\mathbf{c}}$ for the unique linear subspace of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $\pi(L^{\mathbf{c}})=L$ . If $\mathcal{S}$ is a set of projective subspaces, then $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}=\{L^{\mathbf{c}}\,|\,L\in\mathcal{S}\}$ is the corresponding set of linear subspaces of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Lemma A.34. If $\mathcal{S}$ is a nested set in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , then $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ is a nested set in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Proof. Immediate consequence of Definitions A.32 and A.26 . ∎ The proof of the next lemma is straightforward and we omit it. Lemma A.35. Let $V$ be a vector space and let $\mathcal{S}$ be a nested set in $V$ . Suppose that $T$ is a non-zero linear subspace of $V$ such that $T\subset L$ for every $L\in\mathcal{S}$ . Let $\pr:V\to V/T$ be the quotient projection. Then the following holds. (i) The set $\mathcal{S}/T=\{\pr(L)\,|\,L\in\mathcal{S}\}$ is nested in $V/T$ . (ii) If $B$ is a basis of $V$ adapted to $\mathcal{S}$ such that $T=\spn(B\cap T)$ , then $\pr(B)=\{\pr(e)\,|\,e\in B\setminus(T\cap B)\}$ is a basis of $V/T$ adapted to $\mathcal{S}/T$ . Next, we consider frames of vectors of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ adapted to a nested set. Lemma A.36. Let $\mathcal{S}$ be a nested set in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Let $M$ be the common intersection of all the members of $\mathcal{S}$ and let $p$ be a point in $M$ . Then the following holds. (i) The set $T_{p}\mathcal{S}:=\{T_{p}L\,|\,L\in\mathcal{S}\}$ is a nested set in $T_{p}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . (ii) There is a neighbourhood $U$ of $p$ in $M$ and a holomorphic frame $\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}$ of $T\mathbb{CP}^{n}|_{M}$ defined on $U$ such that, for every $q\in U$ the basis $\{e_{1}(q),\ldots,e_{n}(q)\}$ of $T_{q}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is adapted to $T_{q}\mathcal{S}$ . Proof. (i) Let $\bar{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $\pi(\bar{p})=p$ . The differential $d\pi_{\bar{p}}:T_{\bar{p}}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to T_{p}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ gives us an identification of $T_{p}\mathcal{S}$ with $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}/T$ where $T=\mathbb{C}\cdot\bar{p}$ . The statement follows from Lemmas A.34 and A.35 (i). (ii) Let $\{e_{1}(p),\ldots,e_{n}(p)\}$ be a basis of $T_{p}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ adapted to $T_{p}\mathcal{S}$ . Let $\bar{e}_{1},\ldots,\bar{e}_{n}\in T_{\bar{p}}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $d\pi_{\bar{p}}(\bar{e}_{i})=e_{i}(p)$ and extend $\bar{e}_{i}$ as constant vectors on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Let $e_{0}$ be the Euler vector field in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ and let $M^{\mathbf{c}}$ be the unique linear subspace of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $\pi(M^{\mathbf{c}})=M$ . Claim: there is a neighbourhood $\bar{U}$ of $\bar{p}$ in $M^{\mathbf{c}}$ such that for every $\bar{q}\in\bar{U}$ the vectors $\bar{B}(\bar{q})=\{e_{0}(\bar{q}),\bar{e}_{1},\ldots,\bar{e}_{n}\}$ form a basis of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ . Proof of the claim: the vectors $\bar{B}(\bar{p})$ form a basis of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ . By continuity, the vectors $\bar{B}(\bar{q})$ form a basis of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ for $\bar{q}$ close to $\bar{p}$ . To check that $\bar{B}(\bar{q})$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ , we need to show that each $L^{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ contains $\dim L^{\mathbf{c}}$ vectors from $\bar{B}(q)$ . However, the number of vectors of $\bar{B}(q)$ contained in $L^{\mathbf{c}}$ is independent of $q$ for $q\in M^{\mathbf{c}}$ because $e_{0}(\bar{q})\in M^{\mathbf{c}}\subset L^{\mathbf{c}}$ and the rest of the vectors $\bar{e}_{i}$ with $i\geq 1$ in $\bar{B}(q)$ are constant. Since, $\bar{B}(\bar{p})$ is adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ , it follows that $\bar{B}(\bar{q})$ is also adapted to $\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{c}}$ for all $\bar{q}$ in a neighbourhood $\bar{U}$ of $\bar{p}$ in $M^{\mathbf{c}}$ . This finishes the proof of the claim. Let $U=\pi(\bar{U})$ where $\bar{U}$ is as in the claim. Take an affine hyperplane $K\subset M^{\mathbf{c}}$ that goes through $\bar{p}$ and is transversal to $e_{0}(\bar{p})$ . This way, for every $q\in U$ there is a unique $\bar{q}\in K\cap\bar{U}$ such that $\pi(\bar{q})=q$ ; define $e_{i}(q)$ to be the projection of $\bar{e}_{i}(\bar{q})$ by $d\pi_{\bar{q}}$ . It follows from Lemma A.35 (ii) that $e_{1}(q),\ldots,e_{n}(q)$ is a basis of $T_{q}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ adapted to $T_{q}\mathcal{S}$ . This finishes the proof of (ii). ∎ A.2 Exterior algebra Let $V$ be a finite dimensional complex vector space and let $\Lambda^{r}V$ be the $r$ -th exterior product of $V$ . We refer to elements $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ as $r$ -vectors or multivectors. A.2.1 Multivectors and subspaces Let $S\subset V$ be a linear subspace. Then $\Lambda^{r}S$ is naturally embedded in $\Lambda^{r}V$ as the subspace spanned by vectors of the form $v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r}$ with $v_{i}\in S$ . Definition A.37. We say that the $r$ -vector $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is tangent to $S$ if $v$ belongs to the subspace $\Lambda^{r}S$ . Example A.38. Let $v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}$ be a basis of $V$ and let $S$ be the subspace spanned by $v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}$ . Let $v=\sum_{I}c_{I}v_{I}$ where $I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}$ runs over all $r$ -subsets of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $v_{I}=v_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{i_{r}}$ with $i_{1}<\ldots<i_{r}$ are the basis vectors. Then $v$ is tangent to $S$ if and only if $c_{I}=0$ for all $I$ such that $I\not\subset\{1,\ldots,s\}$ . Remark A.39. (i) If $v=0$ then the tangency condition is trivially satisfied, i.e., $v$ is tangent to any subspace. (ii) If $v$ is tangent to $S$ and $S\subset H$ then $v$ is also tangent to $H$ . (iii) If $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is non-zero and tangent to a subspace $S$ then $\dim S\geq r$ because $\Lambda^{r}S$ is zero if $\dim S<r$ . Definition A.40. We say that $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is decomposable if $v$ is non-zero and tangent to an $r$ -dimensional subspace $S\subset V$ . Example A.41. If $S\subset V$ is an $r$ -dimensional subspace and $v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}$ is basis of $S$ then $v=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r}$ (A.8) is non-zero and tangent to $S$ , thus $v$ is decomposable. Taking different basis of $S$ , say $v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{r}$ , gives a scalar multiple $v^{\prime}=\lambda v$ where $v^{\prime}=v_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r}^{\prime}$ and $\lambda$ is the determinant of the change of basis. The following lemma shows that all decomposable multivectors are of the form given by Example A.41 . Lemma A.42. Suppose that $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is decomposable and let $S$ be an $r$ -dimensional subspace such that $v$ is tangent to $S$ . (i) If $v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}$ is a basis of $S$ then $v=\lambda\cdot(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r})$ with $\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}$ . (ii) The subspace $S$ is uniquely determined by $v$ and it is given by $S=\ker(\wedge v)=\{u\in V\,\,|\,\,u\wedge v=0\}.$ (A.9) (iii) If $H\subset V$ is a linear subspace and $v$ is tangent to $H$ then $S\subset H$ . Proof. (i) Extend $v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}$ to a basis $v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}$ of $V$ and write $v=\sum_{I}\lambda_{I}v_{I}$ where $v_{I}=v_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{i_{r}}$ are the basis vectors of $\Lambda^{r}V$ . Since $v$ is tangent to $S$ , we have $\lambda_{I}=0$ for all $I\neq\{1,\ldots,r\}$ by Example A.38 . (ii) Take a basis of $V$ as above. It follows from item (i) that $S\subset\ker(\wedge v)$ . Conversely, if $u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}v_{i}$ is such that $u\wedge v=0$ then we must have $\lambda_{i}=0$ for all $i>r$ , so $u\in S$ . (iii) If $v\in\Lambda^{r}H$ and $u\notin H$ then $u\wedge v\neq 0$ . Therefore, using (ii), if $u\in S=\ker(\wedge v)$ we must have $u\in H$ . ∎ More generally, for any non-zero $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ , the dimension of $\ker(\wedge v)$ is $\leq r$ and it is $=r$ precisely when $v$ is decomposable. The set of decomposable vectors in $\Lambda^{r}V$ is the zero locus of a set of homogeneous quadratic equations, known as the Plücker relations. If $S\subset V$ is an $r$ -dimensional subspace then $\Lambda^{r}S$ is a complex line thorough the origin in $\Lambda^{r}V$ whose non-zero elements are decomposable $r$ -vectors. Conversely, a decomposable $r$ -vector determines a unique $r$ -dimensional subspace $S\subset V$ and if two decomposable $r$ -vectors $v$ and $v^{\prime}$ determine the same subspace then $v$ and $v^{\prime}$ are scalar multiples of each other. This correspondence between $r$ -dimensional subspaces of $V$ and the closed subvariety of $\mathbb{P}(\Lambda^{r}V)$ of decomposable vectors is known as the Plücker embedding of the Grassmannian of $r$ -planes in $V$ . A.2.2 Contraction Definition A.43 ([Sha94, p. 42]). Let $V$ be a vector space and let $\omega\in V^{*}$ . The contraction is a linear map $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,:\Lambda^{r}V\to\Lambda^{r-1}V$ defined by the following properties. • If $v\in\Lambda^{1}V=V$ then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v=\omega(v)$ . • If $v_{1}\in\Lambda^{r}V$ and $v_{2}\in\Lambda^{s}V$ then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})=(\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v_{1})\wedge v_{2}+(-1)^{r}v_{1}\wedge(\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v_{2}).$ (A.10) The contraction defines a bilinear a map from the direct product $V^{*}\times\Lambda^{r}V$ to $\Lambda^{r-1}V$ ; or equivalently a linear map $V^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{r}V\xrightarrow{\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,}\Lambda^{r-1}V.$ Lemma A.44. The contraction is a non-degenerate bilinear pairing. More precisely, if $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is non-zero then there is $\omega\in V^{*}$ such that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\neq 0$ . Conversely, if $\omega\in V^{*}$ is non-zero then there is $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ such that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\neq 0$ . Proof. Let $v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}$ be a basis of $V$ and write $v=\sum_{I}\lambda_{I}v_{I}$ where the sum is over multi-indices $I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})$ and $v_{I}=v_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{i_{r}}$ are the basis elements of $\Lambda^{r}V$ . Let $\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}$ be the dual basis defined by $\eta_{i}(v_{j})=1$ if $i=j$ and $0$ otherwise. Suppose that $v$ is non-zero. Fix $I$ such that $\lambda_{I}\neq 0$ and let $i\in I$ . Take $\omega=\eta_{i}$ , we claim that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\neq 0$ . Indeed, if $I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{i\}$ then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v=\pm\lambda_{I}v_{I^{\prime}}+\sum_{J\neq I^{\prime}}\tilde{\lambda}_{J}v_{J}\neq 0$ where the sum runs over multi-indices $J\neq I^{\prime}$ with $|J|=r-1$ which do not contain $i$ and $\tilde{\lambda}_{J}=\pm\lambda_{J\cup\{i\}}$ . Conversely, if $\omega\neq 0$ then we can assume that $\omega=\eta_{1}$ and $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r})\neq 0\,.$ This concludes the proof. ∎ If $U\subset V$ is a linear subspace then $\Lambda^{r}U$ is naturally embedded in $\Lambda^{r}V$ as the subspace spanned by elements of the form $u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge u_{r}$ with $u_{i}\in U$ . Lemma A.45. Let $U\subset V$ be a linear subspace and let $\omega\in V^{*}$ . Then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\Lambda^{r}U\subset\Lambda^{r-1}U.$ (A.11) Proof. By linearity, it suffices to consider the contraction of $\omega$ with decomposable $r$ -vectors $u=u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge u_{r}$ with $u_{i}\in U$ . The fact that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,u\in\Lambda^{r-1}U$ follows from Equation (A.10) and induction on $r$ . ∎ Let $e\in V$ be a non-zero vector. Let $V_{r}^{\circ}$ be the subspace of all $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ such that $e\wedge v=0$ and let $W^{\circ}\subset V^{*}$ be the subspace of all $1$ -forms $\omega$ with $\omega(e)=0$ . Lemma A.46. The following holds. (i) If $v\in\Lambda^{r}V$ is non-zero and $r\geq 2$ then there is $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ such that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\neq 0$ . (ii) If $v\in V^{\circ}_{r}$ and $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\in V^{\circ}_{r-1}$ . (iii) If $v\in V^{\circ}_{r}$ and $\omega(e)=1$ then $v^{\prime}=\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v$ satisfies $v=e\wedge v^{\prime}$ . Proof. (i) We complete the vector $e$ to a basis of $V$ , say $v_{1}=e,v_{2},\ldots,v_{n}$ . Let $\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}$ be the dual basis of $V^{*}$ defined by $\eta_{i}(v_{j})=\delta_{ij}$ . The subspace $W^{\circ}$ is spanned by $\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n}$ . Write $v=\sum_{I}\lambda_{I}v_{I}$ where $v_{I}=v_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{i_{r}}$ are the basis elements of $\Lambda^{r}V$ and let $I$ be such that $\lambda_{I}\neq 0$ . Since $|I|=r\geq 2$ , we can find an index $i\in I$ with $i\neq 1$ and we take $\omega=\eta_{i}$ . Then $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ and the argument in the proof of Lemma A.44 shows that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v\neq 0$ . (ii) We need to show that $e\wedge(\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v)$ is zero. This follows from $e\wedge v=0$ and $\omega(e)=0$ together with the identity $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,(e\wedge v)=\omega(e)v-e\wedge(\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v).$ (iii) Since $e\wedge v=0$ and $\omega(e)=1$ , $0=\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,(e\wedge v)=\omega(e)v-e\wedge(\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,v)=v-e\wedge v^{\prime}$ and the statement follows. ∎ A.3 Saturated subsheaves A.3.1 Subsheaves and subbundles Let $X$ be a complex manifold. For clarity, in this section A.3.1 only we distinguish between vector bundles and locally free sheaves by writing holomorphic vector bundles on $X$ with straight letters $E$ and use curly letters $\mathcal{E}$ to denote their corresponding sheaves of holomorphic sections. The correspondence $E\mapsto\mathcal{E}$ defines an equivalence between the categories of holomorphic vector bundles on $X$ and locally free sheaves of $\mathcal{O}_{X}$ -modules. We recall the induced bijection between morphisms on these two categories. Let $x\in X$ . We denote by $\mathcal{E}_{x}$ the stalk of $\mathcal{E}$ at $x$ and by $E_{x}$ the fibre of $E$ at $x$ . The two are related by $E_{x}=\mathcal{E}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}\mathcal{E}_{x}$ (A.12) where $\mathfrak{m}_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X,x}$ is the ideal of germs of functions that vanish at $x$ . If $E$ and $F$ are holomorphic vector bundles with sheaves of sections $\mathcal{E}$ and $\mathcal{F}$ then there is a natural correspondence between homomorphisms of $\mathcal{O}_{X}$ -modules $\varphi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})$ and linear holomorphic maps of vector bundles $\phi\in H^{0}(\Hom(E,F))$ where $\Hom(E,F)$ is the vector bundle whose fibre over $x$ is the set of linear maps $E_{x}\to F_{x}$ .
