More precisely, an element $\varphi$ acts on stalks $\varphi_{x}:\mathcal{E}_{x}\to\mathcal{F}_{x}$ as a linear map of $\mathcal{O}_{X,x}$ -modules and, since $\varphi_{x}(\mathfrak{m}_{x}\mathcal{E}_{x})\subset\mathfrak{m}_{x}\mathcal{F}_{x}$ , it induces a linear map of $\mathbb{C}$ -vector spaces $\phi_{x}:E_{x}\to F_{x}$ giving the action on the fibres of $\phi$ . Conversely, an element $\phi$ acts pointwise on sections to give an element $\varphi$ . The constructions $\varphi\mapsto\phi$ and $\phi\mapsto\varphi$ are inverses of each other. Example A.47. It is clear that if $\varphi_{x}:\mathcal{E}_{x}\to\mathcal{F}_{x}$ is surjective then $\phi_{x}:E_{x}\to F_{x}$ is also surjective. However, it can happen that $\varphi_{x}$ is injective but $\phi_{x}$ is not. For example, if $\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X}$ and $\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)$ is the locally free sheaf of meromorphic functions on $X$ with simple poles along a divisor $D\subset X$ . Then the inclusion of $\mathcal{O}_{X}$ -modules $\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{F}$ defines an element of $H^{0}(\Hom(E,F))$ , or equivalently a section of $F$ , that vanishes along $D$ . We recall the following standard result. Lemma A.48. Let $f:E\to F$ be a surjective holomorphic map of vector bundles. Then $\ker(f)$ is a vector subbundle of $E$ . Definition A.49. Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a locally free sheaf and let $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ be a subsheaf. We say that $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ if $\mathcal{V}$ is locally free and the natural map of vector bundles $V\to E$ is injective. Lemma A.50. Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a locally free sheaf and let $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ be a subsheaf such that $\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{V}$ is locally free. Then $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ . Proof. The quotient projection $\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}:=\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{V}$ corresponds to a surjective map of vector bundles $f:E\to F$ . By Lemma A.48 $\ker(f)$ is a vector subbundle of $E$ and by construction $\mathcal{V}$ is the sheaf of sections of $\ker(f)$ . ∎ A.3.2 Saturated subsheaves and subbundles Definition A.51. Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a locally free sheaf and let $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ be a coherent subsheaf. The subsheaf $\mathcal{V}$ is saturated if the quotient sheaf $\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{V}$ is torsion-free. Proposition A.52 ([OSS11]). If $\mathcal{F}$ is a torsion-free coherent sheaf on $X$ then there is a closed analytic subset $Z\subset X$ with $\codim Z\geq 2$ such that $\mathcal{F}$ is locally free on $X\setminus Z$ . Corollary A.53. Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a locally free sheaf on $X$ and suppose that $\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{E}$ is a saturated subsheaf. Then there exists a closed analytic subset $Z\subset X$ with $\codim Z\geq 2$ such that $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ on $X\setminus Z$ . Proof. By Proposition A.52 there is a closed analytic subset $Z\subset X$ with $\codim Z\geq 2$ such that $\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{V}$ is locally free on $X\setminus Z$ . By Lemma A.50 , $\mathcal{V}$ is a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ on $X\setminus Z$ . ∎ A.3.3 Determinant line bundle Let $\mathcal{E}$ be a vector bundle on a complex manifold $X$ and let $\mathcal{V}$ be a vector subbundle of $\mathcal{E}$ defined on an open set $U=X\setminus Z$ where $Z\subset X$ is a closed analytic subset with $\codim Z\geq 2$ . Let $\imath:U\to X$ be the inclusion map and let $r=\rk\mathcal{V}$ . Definition A.54. The determinant line bundle of $\mathcal{V}$ is the sheaf $\det\mathcal{V}$ on $X$ defined as the double dual of the push-forward of $\Lambda^{r}\mathcal{V}$ by the inclusion map $\det\mathcal{V}=(\imath_{*}(\Lambda^{r}\mathcal{V}))^{**}.$ (A.13) In particular, on the open set $U$ , the sheaf $\det(\mathcal{V})$ is canonically isomorphic to the line bundle $\Lambda^{r}\mathcal{V}$ . The sheaf $\det(\mathcal{V})$ provides a canonical extension of $\Lambda^{r}\mathcal{V}$ to the whole $X$ with the following desirable property. Lemma A.55. $\det\mathcal{V}$ is a line bundle on $X$ . Proof. By definition, $\det\mathcal{V}$ is a reflexive sheaf of rank $1$ . A reflexive sheaf of rank $1$ is a line bundle, see [Har80, Proposition 1.9]. ∎ Definition A.56. Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a saturated subsheaf of a locally free sheaf. The first Chern class of $\mathcal{V}$ is defined as $c_{1}(\mathcal{V})=c_{1}(\det\mathcal{V})\,,$ where $\det\mathcal{V}$ is the determinant line bundle of $\mathcal{V}$ as in Definition A.54 . A.4 Distributions on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ A distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is a holomorphic vector subbundle $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ defined outside a closed analytic subset $Z\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of complex codimension $\geq 2$ . The rank $r$ of the subbundle is also called the rank of $\mathcal{V}$ , we assume that $1\leq r\leq n-1$ . The singular set of $\mathcal{V}$ is the smallest of all such $Z$ ’s, the regular set is the complement $U=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus Z$ . Definition A.57. The index of $\mathcal{V}$ is the unique integer $\imath$ such that there is an isomorphism of line bundles $\det\mathcal{V}\cong\,\imath\cdot\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\,,$ (A.14) where $\det\mathcal{V}$ is as in Definition A.54 . Lemma A.58. The index is less or equal than the rank, $\imath\leq r$ . Proof. Take a generic line $P$ contained in the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ that is not tangent to the distribution, in the sense that $TP\not\subset\mathcal{V}|_{P}$ . Then $\mathcal{V}|_{P}$ is a subbundle of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)^{\oplus(n-1)}$ that doesn’t contain $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)$ , so $\imath=\deg(\mathcal{V}|_{P})\leq r$ . ∎ We handle distributions using multivector fields, as detailed next. Lemma A.59. Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of index $\imath$ and rank $r$ . Then there is a multivector field $\mathbf{v}\in H^{0}\left(\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)\right),$ uniquely determined up to scalar multiplication, such that, on the regular set $U$ of $\mathcal{V}$ , $\mathcal{V}=\{w\in T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\,\,|\,\,w\wedge\mathbf{v}=0\}.$ In particular, the multivector field $\mathbf{v}$ is nowhere zero outside a codimension $2$ analytic subset of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Proof. This is a global version of the Plücker embedding of the Grassmannian. Let $x$ be a point in $U$ and let $v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}$ be tangent vectors at $x$ that make a basis of $\mathcal{V}_{x}$ . Define $\mathbf{v}_{x}^{\prime}=(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r})\otimes\ell$ (A.15) where $\ell\in(\det\mathcal{V}_{x})^{*}$ is given by $\ell(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r})=1$ . The element $\mathbf{v}_{x}^{\prime}$ is independent of the choice of basis and varying $x$ we obtain a nowhere zero holomorphic section $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}$ of $\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes(\det\mathcal{V})^{*}$ defined over $U$ . By Hartogs, $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}$ extends across the singular set of $\mathcal{V}$ as a holomorphic section on the whole $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . Fixing an isomorphism $F:(\det\mathcal{V})^{*}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)$ we obtain a section $\mathbf{v}$ of $\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{P}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)$ . Taking a different isomorphism $\widetilde{F}$ produces another section $\tilde{\mathbf{v}}$ . We can write $\widetilde{F}=\Phi\circ F$ where $\Phi$ is an automorphism of the line bundle $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)$ . Since $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is compact, $\Phi=\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}$ and therefore $\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=\lambda\mathbf{v}$ . ∎ Remark A.60. It is common to present distributions as kernels of differential forms, see for example [CP08, §1.3]. To get this description, fix a nowhere zero trivializing section $\Omega$ of $\Lambda^{n}T^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(n+1)$ . Let $\mathbf{v}$ be as in Lemma A.59 . The contraction of $\Omega$ with $\mathbf{v}$ defines a (twisted) differential form $\omega=\mathbf{v}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\Omega\in H^{0}\left(\Lambda^{n-r}T^{*}\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(n+1-\imath)\right)$ such that $\mathcal{V}=\ker\omega$ on the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ . Definition A.61. The degree $d$ of $\mathcal{V}$ is the difference $d=r-\imath.$ (A.16) By Lemma A.58 , $d$ is a non-negative integer. Remark A.62 (Geometric interpretation of degree). If $Q$ is a generic linear subspace of complimentary dimension $\dim Q=n-r$ , then $d$ is equal to the degree of the hypersurface $Y\subset Q$ made of points $x\in Q$ where the subspaces $\mathcal{V}_{x}$ and $T_{x}Q$ have non-zero intersection. To see this take $\omega$ as in Remark A.60 and note that the pullback of $\omega$ to $Q$ defines a non-zero section of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n-r}}(d)$ . A.4.1 Homogeneous multivector fields on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ We work on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with linear coordinates $x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}$ . For non-negative integers $d$ and $r$ we consider the finite dimensional vector space $V_{d,r}=\mathbb{C}_{d}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]\otimes\Lambda^{r}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ where $\mathbb{C}_{d}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]$ is the space of homogeneous polynomials of degree $d$ . We use the basis of $\Lambda^{r}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ given by the multivectors $\partial x_{I}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}$ where $I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})$ is a multi-index with $0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{r}\leq n$ . An element $\mathbf{v}\in V_{d,r}$ takes the form $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{I}a_{I}\partial x_{I},$ where the coefficients $a_{I}$ are homogeneous polynomials of degree $d$ . Example A.63. The Euler vector field $e$ is the element in $V_{1,1}$ given by $e=\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}.$ (A.17) More generally, the elements of $V_{1,1}$ are linear vector fields in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Taking wedge product with $e$ defines a linear map $V_{d,r}\xrightarrow{\wedge\,e}V_{d+1,r+1}$ . We define $V^{\circ}_{d,r}$ to be the kernel of $\wedge\,e$ , that is $V_{d,r}^{\circ}=\bigl\{\mathbf{v}\in V_{d,r}\,\text{ such that }\,\mathbf{v}\wedge e=0\bigr\}.$ (A.18) As we shall see, elements in $V_{d,r}^{\circ}$ correspond to twisted multivector fields on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . We begin with a preliminary lemma whose proof we omit. Lemma A.64 ([Hir66, Theorem 4.1.3]). An exact sequence of vector bundles $0\to L\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}V\xrightarrow{\pi}W\to 0$ with $\rk L=1$ determines an exact sequence $0\to\Lambda^{r}W\otimes L\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}\Lambda^{r+1}V\xrightarrow{\pi}\Lambda^{r+1}W\to 0,$ given by $\pi(v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r+1})=\pi(v_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\pi(v_{r+1})$ and $\imath_{0}((w_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge w_{r})\otimes\ell)=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r}\wedge\imath_{0}(\ell)$ where $v_{i}\in V$ are such that $\pi(v_{i})=w_{i}$ . The desired correspondence between twisted multivector fields on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and homogeneous multivector fields on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ that annihilate $e$ is given by the next. Proposition A.65. Let $r,d$ be non-negative integers and set $\imath=r-d$ . Then there is a natural linear isomorphism between $H^{0}(\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath))$ and $V^{\circ}_{d+1,r+1}$ . Proof. This is essentially a consequence of Euler’s exact sequence. Let $E=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ be the vector bundle on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ equal to the direct sum of $(n+1)$ -copies of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ . There is an obvious isomorphism between $V_{1,1}$ and the space of global sections $H^{0}(E)$ given by $\sum_{i=0}^{n}\ell_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\mapsto(\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{n})\,,$ where $\ell_{i}$ are linear functions on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Under this isomorphism, the Euler vector field $e\in V_{1,1}$ corresponds to a nowhere zero section $e\in H^{0}(E)$ . Euler’s exact sequence is given by $0\to\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}E\xrightarrow{\pi}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\to 0\,,$ (A.19) where $\imath_{0}(\lambda)=\lambda e$ is the inclusion of the trivial line bundle into $E$ defined by the nowhere zero section $e\in H^{0}(E)$ and $\pi$ projects a linear vector field on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ down to $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ by the differential of the quotient map by scalar multiplication. By Lemma A.64 taking exterior power of (A.19) gives us an exact sequence $0\to\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}\Lambda^{r+1}E\xrightarrow{\pi}\Lambda^{r+1}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\to 0\,,$ (A.20) where the maps $\imath_{0}$ and $\pi$ in (A.20) act on decomposable vectors as follows: • if $w=w_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge w_{r}\in\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ take $v_{i}\in E$ such that $\pi(v_{i})=w_{i}$ then $\imath_{0}(w)=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r}\wedge e\,;$ (A.21) • if $v=v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{r+1}\in\Lambda^{r+1}E$ then $\pi(v)=\pi(v_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\pi(v_{r+1})\,.$ (A.22) Take the tensor product of the short exact sequence of vector bundles (A.20) with $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)$ to obtain $0\to\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{P}^{n}(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}\Lambda^{r+1}E(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\pi}\Lambda^{r+1}T\mathbb{P}^{n}(-\imath)\to 0\,.$ (A.23) Composing $\Lambda^{r+1}E(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\pi}\Lambda^{r+1}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}(-\imath)$ with the inclusion $\Lambda^{r+1}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}\Lambda^{r+2}E(-\imath)$ and using that $\imath_{0}\circ\pi=\wedge\,e$ we obtain an exact sequence $0\to\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{P}^{n}(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}\Lambda^{r+1}E(-\imath)\xrightarrow{\wedge\,e}\Lambda^{r+2}E(-\imath)\,,$ where exactness in the middle term follows from $\ker\pi=\ker(\imath_{0}\circ\pi)$ since $\imath_{0}$ is injective. Taking global sections (which is a left exact functor) gives an exact sequence of vector spaces $0\to H^{0}(\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{P}^{n}(-\imath))\xrightarrow{\imath_{0}}H^{0}(\Lambda^{r+1}E(-\imath))\xrightarrow{\wedge\,e}H^{0}(\Lambda^{r+2}E(-\imath))\,.$ (A.24) Finally, we note that, since $\imath=r-d$ , the space of global sections of $\Lambda^{r+1}E\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(d+1)\otimes\Lambda^{r+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ is naturally isomorphic to $V_{d+1,r+1}$ . Then it follows from Equation (A.24) that $H^{0}(\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(-\imath))$ embeds in $V_{d+1,r+1}$ as the subspace of vectors $\mathbf{v}$ such that $\mathbf{v}\wedge e=0.$ ∎ Remark A.66. The case $\imath=r-d=0$ corresponds to genuine multivector fields on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ - without any twisting. In this case the induced action $\mathbb{C}^{*}$ action on $V_{r+1,r+1}$ by scalar multiplication is trivial. Following next, we combine Lemma A.59 and Proposition A.65 together to associate a homogeneous multivector field $\mathbf{v}$ on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ to a distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . In order to state the result we first recall the notion of pullback distribution. Let $X$ and $Y$ be complex manifolds and suppose that $f:X\to Y$ is a holomorphic submersion, i.e., the differential $df_{x}$ is surjective for all $x\in X$ . In this situation, given a distribution $\mathcal{V}$ on $Y$ we define its pullback $f^{*}(\mathcal{V})$ as a distribution on $X$ which is equal to $df_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{y})$ at all points $y=f(x)$ that belong to the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ . We consider the case where $f:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the quotient map by scalar multiplication. Corollary A.67. Let $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be a distribution of rank $r$ and degree $d$ . Then $\mathcal{V}$ defines a unique up to scalar multiplication homogeneous multivector field $\mathbf{v}\in V^{\circ}_{d+1,r+1}$ such that $\ker(\wedge\,\mathbf{v})$ is equal to the $\mathbb{C}^{*}$ -invariant distribution $f^{*}(\mathcal{V})$ on $f^{-1}(U)$ , where $U\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ . In particular, $\mathbf{v}$ is nowhere zero and pointwise decomposable outside a codimension $2$ analytic subset of $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Proof. Lemma A.59 and Proposition A.65 combined together give us a vector $\mathbf{v}\in V^{\circ}_{d+1,r+1}$ . If $y\in\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ belongs to the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ and $x\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}$ projects down to $y$ under the quotient map $f:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}\,,$ then, unravelling definitions, the value of $\mathbf{v}$ at $x$ is equal (up to a scalar factor) to $\mathbf{v}_{x}=\tilde{v}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\tilde{v}_{r}\wedge e_{x}\,,$ (A.25) where $\tilde{v}_{i}\in T_{x}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ satisfy $df_{x}(\tilde{v}_{i})=v_{i}$ and $v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}$ make a basis of $\mathcal{V}_{y}$ . It follows from Equation (A.25) that $\ker(\wedge\mathbf{v}_{x})$ is equal to $df_{x}^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{y})$ ∎ Example A.68. Let $1\leq r\leq n-1$ and consider the linear subspace $M$ of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ of dimension $r-1$ given by the set of points $[x_{0}:\ldots:x_{n}]$ such that $\{x_{r}=x_{r+1}=\ldots=x_{n}=0\}\,.$ The collection of $r$ -planes that contain $M$ defines a distribution $\mathcal{V}$ of rank $r$ and degree $d=0$ . The multivector field $\mathbf{v}\in V^{\circ}_{1,r+1}$ of $\mathcal{V}$ is given by $\mathbf{v}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{0}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{r-1}}\wedge e\,.$ Remark A.69. Let $p=n-r$ denote the codimension of a distribution. Then, an analogue of Proposition A.65, shows that there is a canonical identification between $\Omega^{p}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(n+1-\imath)$ (as in Remark A.60) and the vector space of $p$ -forms on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with homogeneous coefficients of degree $d+1$ whose contraction with the Euler vector field is identically zero. In particular, codimension $1$ distributions on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ correspond to homogeneous $1$ -forms $\omega=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}dx_{i}\,,$ where $a_{i}$ are homogeneous polynomials of degree $d+1$ with $\sum_{i}x_{i}a_{i}=0$ and such that the common zero set of the polynomials $a_{i}$ has codimension $\geq 2$ . A.4.2 Multivector fields tangent to hyperplanes Definition A.70. Let $X$ be a complex manifold and let $D\subset X$ be a smooth complex hypersurface. We say that an $r$ -vector field $\mathbf{v}\in H^{0}(\Lambda^{r}TX)$ is tangent to $D$ at $x$ if $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ belongs to the linear subspace $\Lambda^{r}T_{x}D\subset\Lambda^{r}T_{x}X$ . We say that $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $D$ if $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $D$ at all points $x\in D$ . Remark A.71. By continuity, if $D^{\circ}$ is an open dense subset of $D$ and $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $D^{\circ}$ , then $\mathbf{v}$ is also tangent to $D$ . We are primarily interested in the case that $X$ is $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ and $D$ is a complex hyperplane going through the origin. Lemma A.72. Let $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{I}a_{I}\partial x_{I}$ be an $r$ -vector field on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with polynomial coefficients $a_{I}\in\mathbb{C}[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]$ . Then $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to the hyperplane $\{x_{j}=0\}$ if and only if $x_{j}$ divides $a_{I}$ for every $I$ such that $j\in I$ . Proof. By Example A.38, the tangency condition means that the coefficients $a_{I}$ vanish along $\{x_{j}=0\}$ for every $I$ such that $j\in I$ . On the other hand, since $a_{I}$ is a polynomial, $a_{I}$ vanishes along $\{x_{j}=0\}$ if and only if it is divisible by $x_{j}$ . ∎ Lemma A.73. Let $\mathbf{v}\in V_{d,r}$ be a non-zero homogeneous multivector field on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Suppose that $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to the $k$ coordinate hyperplanes $\{x_{j}=0\}$ for $j\in I_{0}$ where $I_{0}\subset\{0,\ldots,n\}=[n]$ with $|I_{0}|=k$ . Then $r-(n+1)+k\leq d.$ (A.26) Proof. Write $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{I}a_{I}\partial x_{I}$ , where the sum runs over all $I\subset[n]$ with $|I|=r$ and the coefficients $a_{I}$ are homogeneous polynomials of degree $d$ . Fix $I$ such that $a_{I}$ is non-zero. By Lemma A.72, if $j\in I\cap I_{0}$ then $x_{j}$ divides $a_{I}$ . In particular, $|I\cap I_{0}|\leq\deg a_{I}=d.$ (A.27) On the other hand, since at worst $I$ contains all the $n+1-k$ elements from $[n]\setminus I_{0}$ $|I|-(n+1-k)\leq|I\cap I_{0}|.$ (A.28) Combining inequalities (A.27) and (A.28) gives (A.26) . ∎ Definition A.74. Let $\mathbf{v}$ be multivector field in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . We write $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ for the set of all linear hyperplanes $H\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $H$ . Proposition A.75. Let $\mathbf{v}\in V_{d,r}$ be a non-zero homogeneous multivector field on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ . Suppose that $d<r$ so $\imath=r-d>0$ . Then there is a linear subspace $M\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ with $\dim M\geq\imath$ such that $M\subset H$ for all $H\in\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ . Proof. Let $H_{j}$ be $k$ linearly independent hyperplanes in $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ . We prove the proposition by showing that $\imath\leq n+1-k.$ (A.29) Without loss of generality, we can assume that $H_{j}=\{x_{j}=0\}$ for $j\in I_{0}$ where $I_{0}=\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\subset[n]$ . By Lemma A.73 $r-(n+1)+k\leq d=r-\imath$ which is equivalent to Equation (A.29) . ∎ We say that a distribution $\mathcal{V}\subset T\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is tangent to a hyperplane $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ if for every point $x\in H\cap U$ , where $U$ is the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ , we have $\mathcal{V}_{x}\subset T_{x}H$ , where $\mathcal{V}_{x}\subset T_{x}\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ denotes the fibre at $x$ of the vector subbundle $\mathcal{V}|_{U}$ . We denote by $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ the collection of all hyperplanes $H$ such that $\mathcal{V}$ is tangent to $H$ . We note the following. Lemma A.76. Let $\mathcal{V}$ be a distribution on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and let $\mathbf{v}\in V^{\circ}_{d+1,r+1}$ be its multivector as in Corollary A.67 . Then a linear hyperplane $H\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ belongs to $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ if and only if its projection $\mathbb{P}(H)\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ belongs to $\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ . Proof. Let $U\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ be the regular set of $\mathcal{V}$ and let $\widetilde{U}=f^{-1}(U)$ , where $f:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is the quotient projection. Given a linear hyperplane $H\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ , we write $H^{\circ}=H\cap\widetilde{U}$ , and note that $H^{\circ}$ is an open dense subset of $H$ . As pointed out in Remark A.71, $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $H$ if and only if $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ is tangent to $T_{x}H$ at all points $x\in H^{\circ}$ , . On the other hand, given $x\in H^{\circ}$ , the decomposable multivector $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ is tangent to $H$ at $x$ if and only if $\ker(\wedge\mathbf{v}_{x})$ is contained in $T_{x}H$ , see Remark A.39 and Lemma A.42 . If we let $\pi=df_{x}$ , then $\ker(\wedge\mathbf{v}_{x})$ is equal to $\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{y})$ where $y=f(x)$ . We get that $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ is tangent to $T_{x}H$ if and only if $\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{V}_{y})\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{y}\mathbb{P}(H))=T_{x}H.$ (A.30) Since $\pi$ is surjective, Equation (A.30) is satisfied if and only if $\mathcal{V}_{y}\subset T_{y}\mathbb{P}(H).$ (A.31) We conclude that $H\in\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ if an only if Equation (A.31) is satisfied at all points $y\in\mathbb{P}(H)\cap U$ , which by definition means that $\mathbb{P}(H)\in\Tan(\mathcal{V})$ . ∎ Definition A.77. Let $T_{r}$ be the linear subspace of $V_{r,r}$ consisting of $r$ -vector fields on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ , with homogeneous polynomial coefficients of degree $r$ , that are tangent to the $n+1$ coordinate hyperplanes $\{x_{j}=0\}$ . Write $T_{r}^{\circ}\subset T_{r}$ be the linear subspace of all $\mathbf{v}$ such that $\mathbf{v}\wedge e=0$ . Lemma A.78. An element $\mathbf{v}\in V_{r,r}$ belongs to $T_{r}$ if and only if $\mathbf{v}$ is of the form $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})}c_{I}\cdot x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{1}}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{2}}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i_{r}}}$ (A.32) where $c_{I}\in\mathbb{C}$ . Proof. Write $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{I}a_{I}\partial x_{I}$ with $\deg a_{I}=r$ and suppose that $\mathbf{v}$ belongs to $T_{r}$ . Let us fix an index $I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})$ . By Lemma A.72 every $x_{j}$ with $j\in I$ divides $a_{I}$ . Since $\deg a_{I}=r$ we must have that $a_{I}=c_{I}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{r}}$ with $c_{I}\in\mathbb{C}$ . Conversely, if $\mathbf{v}\in V_{r,r}$ is of the form given by Equation (A.32) then by Lemma A.72 $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to the coordinate hyperplanes and hence belongs to $T_{r}$ . ∎ Example A.79. The space $T_{1}$ is the set of all linear vector fields $\mathbf{v}$ on $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ that are tangent to the coordinate hyperplanes. These are precisely the vector fields of the form $\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}$ with $c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}$ . Let $W$ be the $(n+1)$ -dimensional vector space made of meromorphic $1$ -forms $\omega$ of the form given by $\omega=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}\frac{dx_{i}}{x_{i}}$ with $\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{C}$ . Lemma A.80. If $\omega\in W$ and $\mathbf{v}\in T_{r}$ , then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\mathbf{v}$ belongs to $T_{r-1}$ . Proof. Let $j\in[n]$ and $I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subset[n]$ . Write $x_{I}=x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{r}}$ . Then $\frac{dx_{j}}{x_{j}}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,x_{I}\partial x_{I}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }j\notin I,\\ \pm\,x_{I^{\prime}}\partial x_{I^{\prime}}&\text{ if }j\in I,\end{cases}$ (A.33) where $I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{j\}$ . The statement follows from Lemma A.78 and linearity. ∎ Consider the linear subspace $T_{r}^{\circ}\subset T_{r}$ of all $\mathbf{v}\in T_{r}$ such that $\mathbf{v}\wedge e=0$ . Under the isomorphism of Proposition A.65, in the case $\imath=0$ , the vector space $T^{\circ}_{r+1}$ is identified with the subspace of $H^{0}(\Lambda^{r}T\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ of multivector fields tangent to the coordinate hyperplanes. Let $W^{\circ}\subset W$ be the linear subspace of all $\omega$ such that $\omega(e)=0$ . More explicitly, if $\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}dx_{i}/x_{i}$ then $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ if $\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}=0$ . We can think of elements in $W^{\circ}$ as meromorphic $1$ -forms on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with simple poles at the coordinate hyperplanes. Lemma A.81. The following holds: (i) If $\mathbf{v}\in T_{r}$ is non-zero and $r\geq 2$ then there is $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ such that $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\mathbf{v}\neq 0$ . (ii) If $\mathbf{v}\in T^{\circ}_{r}$ and $\omega\in W^{\circ}$ then $\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\mathbf{v}\in T^{\circ}_{r-1}$ . (iii) If $\mathbf{v}\in T^{\circ}_{r}$ and $\omega(e)=1$ then $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\mathbf{v}$ satisfies $e\wedge\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}$ . Proof. This Lemma follows from A.46 once we introduce a number of identifications. We identify $T_{r}\cong\Lambda^{r}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ by means of the linear isomorphism $\sum c_{I}\partial x_{I}\mapsto\sum c_{I}x_{I}\partial x_{I}.$ (A.34) Similarly, we identify $W\cong(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ by $\sum\lambda_{i}dx_{i}\mapsto\sum\lambda_{i}dx_{i}/x_{i}.$ (A.35) Under these identifications, the pairing $W\otimes T_{r}\xrightarrow{\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,}T_{r-1}$ agrees with the usual contraction $(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{r}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\xrightarrow{\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,}\Lambda^{r-1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ , as follows from Equation (A.33), and similarly for wedge products. The subspace $T^{\circ}_{r}$ is identified with the subspace of all $v\in\Lambda^{r}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that $v\wedge e=0$ where $e$ is the non-zero vector $e=(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ and $W^{\circ}$ is the subspace of all $\omega\in(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ such that $\omega(e)=0$ . ∎ Recall that $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ denotes the set of all linear hyperplanes $H\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$ such that the multivector field $\mathbf{v}$ is tangent to $H$ . Proposition A.82. Let $\mathbf{v}$ be a non-zero element of $T_{r}^{\circ}$ with $r\geq 2$ . Then there is a linear vector field $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in T_{1}$ such that $e\wedge\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\neq 0$ and $\Tan(\mathbf{v})\subset\Tan(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})$ . Proof. By repeated applications of items (i) and (ii) of Lemma A.81, we can find $1$ -forms $\omega_{i}\in W^{\circ}$ for $1\leq i\leq r-2$ such that $\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=\omega_{r-2}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\left(\omega_{r-3}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\left(\ldots(\omega_{1}\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,X)\ldots\right)\right)$ is a non-zero element in $T^{\circ}_{2}$ . If $r=2$ we simply take $\tilde{\mathbf{v}}=\mathbf{v}$ . Let $\omega\in W$ be such that $\omega(e)=1$ , say $\omega=dx_{0}/x_{0}$ , and define $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in T_{1}$ as $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\omega\mathbin{\lrcorner}\,\tilde{\mathbf{v}}.$ By item (iii) of Lemma A.81, $e\wedge\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\neq 0.$ We are left to show that $\Tan(\mathbf{v})\subset\Tan(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})$ . Notice that, since $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in T_{1}$ , the coordinate hyperplanes are contained in $\Tan(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})$ . Now, let $H$ be a hyperplane in $\Tan(\mathbf{v})$ different from the coordinate hyperplanes and take a point $x\in H$ outside the origin. The multivector $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{x}$ is obtained by successive contractions of $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ by elements in $(T_{x}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})^{*}$ . Since $\mathbf{v}_{x}$ is tangent to $H$ , it follows from Lemma A.45 that $\mathbf{v}^{\prime}_{x}$ is tangent to $H$ . Therefore, $H\in\Tan(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})$ and the proposition follows. ∎ Appendix B Essential and irreducible configurations A configuration of points $\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}$ in $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is essential if $\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\,.$ Equivalently, $\mathcal{P}$ is essential if it contains $n+1$ linearly independent points. We say that $\mathcal{P}$ is reducible if there are two disjoint linear subspaces $U$ and $V$ such that $\mathcal{P}\subset U\cup V$ with both $U\cap\mathcal{P}$ and $V\cap\mathcal{P}$ non-empty. We say that $\mathcal{P}$ is irreducible if it is not reducible. The main result of this section is the following. Proposition B.1. If the configuration of points $\mathcal{P}$ is essential and irreducible, then there is a weight vector $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$ such that $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable. A hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ corresponds to a configuration of points $\mathcal{P}\subset(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{*}$ . It is straightforward to verify that: $\mathcal{H}$ is essential (irreducible) if and only if $\mathcal{P}$ is essential (irreducible). Proposition B.1 together with Lemma 6.35 , give us the following. Corollary B.2. If the hyperplane arrangement $\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is essential and irreducible, then there is a weight vector $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{H}}_{>0}$ such that $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is stable. Remark B.3. Proposition B.1 follows from the fact that the matroid polytope (see Section 6.3) of $\mathcal{P}$ has dimension $|\mathcal{P}|-1$ precisely when $\mathcal{P}$ is essential and irreducible. See [BGW03, Theorem 1.12.9] and [Sch03, p. 698]. For completeness, we present a self-contained proof. We will use the following notion. Definition B.4. We say that $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{P}$ is a basis if: (i) $\sum\limits_{p\in\mathcal{B}}p=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ ; (ii) $|\mathcal{B}|=n+1$ . Remark B.5. If $\mathcal{P}$ is essential then it contains at least one basis. We shall need the following elementary result, whose proof we omit. Lemma B.6. Let $P$ and $Q$ be linear subspaces of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ such that $P+Q=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and $P\cap Q\neq\emptyset$ . Then $\dim P+\dim Q\geq n$ . The key result we need to prove Proposition B.1 is the next. Lemma B.7. Suppose that $\mathcal{P}$ is essential and irreducible. Then, for every $U$ in the poset $\mathcal{U}$ , there is a basis $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{P}$ such that $|\mathcal{B}\cap U|<\dim U+1\,.$ (B.1) Proof. Let $U\in\mathcal{U}$ . Consider the projective subspace $Q\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ given by $Q=\spn\,\{\,p\in\mathcal{P}\,|\,p\notin U\}\,.$ Since $\mathcal{P}$ is essential, $U+Q=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\,.$ (B.2) In particular, since $U\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ , the subspace $Q$ is non-empty. Choose a linearly independent subset $\mathcal{Q}\subset\{\,p\in\mathcal{P}\,|\,p\notin U\}$ (B.3) with $|\mathcal{Q}|=\dim Q+1$ that spans the subspace $Q$ . Since $\mathcal{P}$ is essential, we can extend $\mathcal{Q}$ to a basis $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{P}$ . It follows from (B.3) that $\mathcal{B}\cap U\subset\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{Q}$ , hence $\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}\cap U|$ $\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{Q}|$ (B.4) $\displaystyle=n+1-|\mathcal{Q}|=n-\dim Q\,.$ On the other hand, $\mathcal{P}\subset U\cup Q$ and both $U\cap\mathcal{P}$ , $Q\cap\mathcal{P}$ are non-empty. Since $\mathcal{P}$ is irreducible, we must have $U\cap Q\neq\emptyset\,.$ (B.5) It follows from Equations (B.2) and (B.5) together with Lemma B.6 that $n-\dim Q\leq\dim U\,.$ (B.6) It follows from Equations (B.4) and (B.6) that $|\mathcal{B}\cap U|\leq\dim U$ which is equivalent to Equation (B.1). ∎ The semi-stable and stable cones. Let $m=|\mathcal{P}|$ and label the points, say $\mathcal{P}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}\,.$ Let $s$ be the linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{m}$ equal to the total sum of the components, $s(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\,,$ where $\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ . Similarly, for an element $U$ in the sum poset $\mathcal{U}$ of $\mathcal{P}$ , let $s_{U}$ be the linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{m}$ given by $s_{U}(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{i\,|\,p_{i}\in U}a_{i}\,.$ For $U\in\mathcal{U}$ , let $f_{U}$ be the linear function on $\mathbb{R}^{m}$ given by $f_{U}(\mathbf{a})=\frac{\dim U+1}{n+1}\cdot s(\mathbf{a})-s_{U}(\mathbf{a})\,.$ (B.7) Definition B.8. The semi-stable cone $C$ is the closed convex polyhedral cone in $\mathbb{R}^{m}$ obtained as intersection of the half-spaces $\{f_{U}\geq 0\}$ for $U\in\mathcal{U}$ together with $\{a_{i}\geq 0\}$ , $C=\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}\cap\left(\bigcap_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\{f_{U}\geq 0\}\right)\,.$ The stable cone is the interior of the semi-stable cone: $C^{\circ}=\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}\cap\left(\bigcap_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\{f_{U}>0\}\right)\,.$ Lemma B.9. The weighted configuration of points $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable if and only if $\mathbf{a}$ belongs to the stable cone. Proof. For $U\in\mathcal{U}$ , Equation (6.22) is equivalent to $f_{U}>0$ . The statement then follows from Lemma 6.33 . ∎ Since $C$ is a cone defined by linear inequalities, we have the following. Lemma B.10. If $\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in C$ then $\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\in C$ . Let $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{P}$ be a basis. The indicator function of $\mathcal{B}$ is the vector $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$ with components given by $(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }p_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\,,\\ 0&\text{ if }p_{i}\notin\mathcal{B}\,.\end{cases}$ Lemma B.11. Let $\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{P}$ be a basis and let $U\in\mathcal{U}$ . Then $f_{U}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})=\dim U+1-|\mathcal{B}\cap U|\,.$ (B.8) In particular, the following holds: (i) $f_{U}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})>0$ if $|\mathcal{B}\cap U|<\dim U+1$ ; (ii) $f_{U}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})\geq 0$ . (iii) $\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}}\in C$ ; Proof. Note that $s(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})=|\mathcal{B}|=n+1$ (B.9) and $s_{U}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}})=|\mathcal{B}\cap U|\,.$ (B.10) Equation (B.8) follows from (B.7) together with Equations (B.9) and (B.10). Items (i) and (ii) follow from Equation (B.8) together with the fact that, since the elements of $\mathcal{B}$ are linearly independent, $|\mathcal{B}\cap U|\leq\dim U+1\,.$ Item (iii) holds since (ii) holds for all $U$ . ∎ Lemma B.12. If $\mathcal{P}$ is essential then $C\cap\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}$ is non-empty. Proof. Since $\mathcal{P}$ is essential, for any $p_{i}\in\mathcal{P}$ we can find a basis $\mathcal{B}_{i}\subset\mathcal{P}$ with $p_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}$ . The vector $\mathbf{a}_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}_{i}}$ belongs to $\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}$ . It follows from Lemma B.11 (iii) and Lemma B.10 , that $\mathbf{a}_{0}\in C$ . ∎ Proof of Proposition B.1. Start with $\mathbf{a}_{0}\in C\cap\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$ as given by Lemma B.12. For each $U\in\mathcal{U}$ , let $\mathcal{B}_{U}\subset\mathcal{P}$ be a basis with $|\mathcal{B}_{U}\cap U|<\dim U+1$ as provided by Lemma B.7 . Set $\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{a}_{0}+\sum_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{B}_{U}}\,.$ Clearly, $\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$ . It follows from Lemma B.11 (i) and (ii) that $f_{U}(\mathbf{a})>0$ for all $U\in\mathcal{U}$ . Therefore, $\mathbf{a}\in C^{\circ}$ . By Lemma B.9 , $(\mathcal{P},\mathbf{a})$ is stable. ∎ Appendix C Miyaoka-Yau and Kähler-Einstein metrics For compact Kähler manifolds, the Miyaoka-Yau inequality follows from the existence of Kähler-Einstein metrics together with the Chern-Weil formula $\left(2(n+1)c_{2}(X)-nc_{1}(X)^{2}\right)\cdot[\omega_{\KE}]^{n-2}=\lambda_{n}\int_{X}|\mathring{\textup{Riem}}|^{2}\cdot\omega_{\KE}^{n}\,\,,$ (C.1) where $(X^{n},\omega_{\KE})$ is a compact Kähler-Einstein manifold, $\mathring{\textup{Riem}}$ is the trace free part of the Riemann curvature tensor of $\omega_{\KE}$ , and $\lambda_{n}$ is a positive dimensional constant. As a consequence, if equality holds in the Miyaoka-Yau inequality, then $\omega_{\KE}$ must have constant holomorphic sectional curvature. The Miyaoka-Yau inequality has been extended to the setting of log pairs $(X,\Delta)$ ; see for instance [Lan03], [Li21], and [GT22] . However, a general version of the Miyaoka-Yau inequality for ktl pairs that characterizes the equality case through the existence of constant holomorphic sectional curvature metrics with conical singularities is still missing. In this appendix we show that, under the hypothesis of Theorem 1.1 , the pairs $(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\Delta)$ with $\Delta=\sum a_{H}\cdot H$ , admit weak Ricci-flat Kähler metrics with prescribed singularities at the hyperplanes. To state this precisely, we introduce some notation. Let $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ be a weighted arrangement of hyperplanes $H\subset\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ and let $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ be the arrangement complement. Let $\omega_{\FS}$ be the Fubini-Study metric on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ with $\omega_{\FS}\in 2\pi\cdot c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))$ and let $|\cdot|$ be the usual Hermitian metric on $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ with curvature $-i\cdot\omega_{\FS}$ . Consider the function $f=\prod_{H\in\mathcal{H}}|\ell_{H}|^{-2a_{H}}\,$ (C.2) where $\ell_{H}$ are sections of $\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)$ with $H=\{\ell_{H}=0\}$ . The main result of this appendix is the next. Proposition C.1. Suppose that the weighted arrangement $(\mathcal{H},\mathbf{a})$ is klt and CY. Then there is a Ricci-flat Kähler metric $\omega_{\RF}$ on the arrangement complement $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ whose volume form is proportional to $f\cdot\omega_{\FS}^{n}$ . Moreover, we can write $\omega_{\RF}=\omega_{\FS}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi\,,$ where $\varphi$ extends continuously to $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ . To prove Proposition C.1 , we need to establish some preliminary lemmas. Write $L^{p}=L^{p}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ for the Lebesgue space of measurable functions $u$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ such that $\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{n}}|u|^{p}\cdot\omega_{\FS}^{n}<\infty\,.$ Lemma C.2. If the klt condition (1.1) is satisfied then $f\in L^{p}$ for some $p>1$ . Proof. Let $\mathcal{L}$ be the collection of non-empty and proper subspaces $\emptyset\neq L\subsetneq\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ obtained as intersection of members of $\mathcal{H}$ . Let $p^{*}=\min_{L\in\mathcal{L}}\frac{\codim L}{\sum\limits_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}}\,.$ The klt condition implies that $p^{*}>1$ . Fix any $1<p<p^{*}$ . We will prove that $f\in L^{p}$ . Consider the stratification of $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ given by the elements of $\mathcal{L}$ . Specifically, let $K_{i}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{n-i}}L$ where $\mathcal{L}^{n-i}$ is the subset of $L\in\mathcal{L}$ with $\codim L=i$ . E.g. $K_{1}$ is union of all hyperplanes, $K_{2}$ is the union of all codimension $2$ subspaces and so on. Since every $L\in\mathcal{L}$ is contained in another $L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}$ with $\dim L^{\prime}=\dim L+1$ , we have a decreasing filtration $K_{1}\supset K_{2}\supset\ldots\supset K_{n}\supset K_{n+1}=\emptyset.$ The complements $\Omega_{i}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus K_{i+1}$ form an increasing sequence of open sets $\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\Omega_{n}=\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ where $\Omega_{0}=(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ is the arrangement complement and $\Omega_{i}\setminus\Omega_{i-1}=K_{i}\setminus K_{i+1}=\bigcup_{\codim L=i}L^{\circ}$ where the union is taken over all $L\in\mathcal{L}$ of codimension $i$ and $L^{\circ}=L\setminus\bigcup_{H|L\not\subset H}(L\cap H).$ Claim: $f\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega_{i})$ for all $0\leq i\leq n$ , where $L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega_{i})$ denotes the space of locally $L^{p}$ functions on $\Omega_{i}$ . Proof of the claim: by induction on $i$ . For $i=0$ the function $f$ is smooth on $\Omega_{0}$ and the statement is obvious. Let $1\leq i\leq n$ and assume that $f\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega_{i})$ . Let $p\in\Omega_{i}\setminus\Omega_{i-1}$ . We want to show that there is an open set $U$ that contains $p$ such that $f\in L^{p}(U)$ . By construction of the stratification, $p\in L^{\circ}$ for some $L\in\mathcal{L}$ with $\codim L=i$ . We can assume that if $H\in\mathcal{H}$ intersects $U$ then $H\supset L$ . Take linear coordinates $z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}$ centred at $p$ , so that $L=\{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{i}=0\}$ . Up to multiplication by a smooth positive factor, we can assume that $f=\prod_{L\in\mathcal{H}_{L}}|\ell_{H}|^{-2a_{H}}$ where $\ell_{H}$ are linear functions on $z_{1},\ldots,z_{i}$ and $|\cdot|$ is the usual absolute value on $\mathbb{C}$ . By Fubini’s theorem it is enough to show that $f$ is locally in $L^{p}$ in a neighbourhood of the origin of the $\mathbb{C}^{i}$ factor transversal to $L$ . At the same time, $U\setminus L\subset\Omega_{i-1}$ and by induction hypothesis $f\in L^{p}_{\loc}(U\setminus L)$ . In particular, the integral of $|f|^{p}$ on a sphere about $0\in\mathbb{C}^{i}$ is finite. Taking spherical polar coordinates on $\mathbb{C}^{i}$ and by homogeneity of $f$ , we see that $|f|^{p}$ is locally integrable around $0\in\mathbb{C}^{i}$ if and only if $\int_{0}^{1}r^{-2\cdot p\cdot a_{L}}r^{2i-1}dr<\infty$ (C.3) where $a_{L}=\sum\limits_{H|L\subset H}a_{H}$ . Clearly, Equation (C.3) holds if and only if $p\cdot a_{L}<i$ which is guaranteed for our choice of $p$ . This finishes the proof of the claim. The claim for $i=n$ implies that $f\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ . On the other hand, since $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is compact, we have $L^{p}_{\loc}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})=L^{p}(\mathbb{CP}^{n})$ . ∎ We say that two volume forms $dV_{1}$ and $dV_{2}$ on a manifold are proportional, if they are equal up to a constant positive factor. We write this as $dV_{1}\propto dV_{2}$ . Lemma C.3. Suppose that $\omega$ is a Kähler metric on $(\mathbb{CP}^{n})^{\circ}$ with $\omega^{n}\propto f\cdot\omega_{\FS}^{n}\,.$ If the CY condition (1.2) is satisfied then $\Ric(\omega)=0$ . Proof. The identity $i\bar{\partial}\partial\log(\omega^{n}/\omega_{\FS}^{n})=\Ric(\omega)-\Ric(\omega_{\FS})$ together with $\Ric(\omega_{\FS})=(n+1)\cdot\omega_{\FS}$ imply that $\Ric(\omega)=(n+1)\cdot\omega_{\FS}+i\bar{\partial}\partial\log f\,.$ (C.4) On the other hand, since $i\bar{\partial}\partial\log|\ell_{H}|^{2}=\omega_{\FS}$ on $\{\ell_{H}\neq 0\}$ , we have $i\bar{\partial}\partial\log f=-\left(\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\right)\cdot\omega_{\FS}\,.$ (C.5) Equations (C.4) and (C.5) give us $\Ric(\omega)=\left(n+1-\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}a_{H}\right)\cdot\omega_{\FS}=0,$ where the second equality holds because of the CY condition. ∎ Next, we finish the proof of Proposition C.1. Proof of Proposition C.1. Since $f\in L^{p}$ for some $p>1$ and $L^{p}\subset L^{1}$ , the integral of $f$ on $\mathbb{CP}^{n}$ is finite. Let $F=C\cdot f$ where $C$ is a positive constant such that $\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{n}}F\cdot\omega_{\FS}^{n}=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{n}}\omega_{\FS}^{n}\,.$ Consider the complex Monge-Ampère equation $\left(\omega_{\FS}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi\right)^{n}=F\cdot\omega_{\FS}^{n}$ (C.6) for an unknown real valued function $\varphi$ . By [Kol98, Theorem 2.4.2 and Example 2 in p.91] there is a continuous solution $\varphi$ to (C.6) . On the arrangement complement, $\varphi$ is smooth and $\omega_{\RF}=\omega_{\FS}+i\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi$ is a Ricci-flat Kähler metric satisfying the required conditions. ∎ Remark C.4. By uniqueness of solutions to (C.6), our Conjecture 1.3 implies that if $Q(\mathbf{a})=0$ then the Ricci-flat metric $\omega_{\RF}$ is actually flat. Heuristically, an extension of the Chern-Weil formula (C.1) to metrics with conical singularities, should express the ‘energy’ or $L^{2}$ -norm of the Riemann curvature tensor of $\omega_{\RF}$ as a function of the weights $E(\mathbf{a})$ so that $\omega_{\RF}$ is flat precisely when $E(\mathbf{a})=0$ . However, little is known on the behaviour of $\omega_{\RF}$ near the support of $\mathcal{H}$ , see [dBS23] for the case $n=2$ . In particular, it is not known whether $|\textup{Riem}(\omega_{\RF})|^{2}$ is locally integrable.
